Comptroller's Manual for Corporate Activities 

Foreword 

COMPTROLLER CURRENCY /1-30-92/1R 

The Comptroller's Manual for Corporate Activities was published originally in 1987. It is a detailed guidebook that describes how national banks and others file corporate applications and how OCC personnel review and analyze those applications. Generally the manual contains policies, procedures, and forms pertaining to those activities. The Introduction contains a more detailed explanation of the purpose, background, and organization of the manual.

This three-volume manual replaces the two-volume loose leaf manual issued in 1988. The revision not only changes the manual's format, but also contains several additions and innovations. The revision adds a policy statements section, modifies the conversions section to include thrift conversions, expands the failure section, consolidates several mergers sections, and incorporates updated policies and procedures that reflect changes in the laws governing corporate activities. 

We hope that the new manual will serve national banks and other applicants, and the OCC, well. We appreciate receiving any ideas for refining and developing the manual further. Comments may be sent to the 

Director for Licensing Policy and Systems, Comptroller of the Currency, Washington, DC 20219.

The three-volume set of the Comptroller's Manual for Corporate Activities may be purchased by writing 

Comptroller of the Currency, P.O. Box 10004, Chicago, Illinois 60673-0004. A check for $90 payable to the

Comptroller of the Currency must accompany each request.

Comptroller's Manual for Corporate Activities Introduction Purpose 

The Comptroller's Manual for Corporate Activities explains the Office of the Comptroller of the Currency's (OCC) policies and procedures to form a new national bank, enter the national banking system, and effect structural changes and corporate expansion. The OCC acts on filings for corporate activities in accordance with 12 USC 1 et seq., which grants the Comptroller the general authority to regulate national banks, and 12 

CFR 5, Rules, Policies, and Procedures for Corporate

Activities. The manual standardizes OCC processing of corporate filings to bring consistency to recordkeeping and decision making processes.

The manual provides specific guidelines for documentation and recordkeeping requirements and criteria generally considered in reviewing a corporate filing. 

However, the publication is only a procedural guide and does not include all factors that may be assessed during the analysis phase. The scope of analysis is determined individually for each application and may require requests for additional information that are not identified here.

Although the manual is intended to serve as the primary procedural guide for OCC management, corporate analysts, and examiners, it also is designed to aid the banking industry. Bankers and other interested parties may use this information to determine where and how to file an application, to identify the regulatory requirements for corporate changes, and to further their understanding of the corporate applications' filing processes and time frames.

The manual is available to the public for $90 upon request. Copies may be obtained by writing Publications Control-Lock Box, P.O. Box 10004, Chicago, IL 

60673-0004. Questions regarding the contents of the manual should be addressed to an appropriate OCC district office. Commonly referenced OCC names, addresses, and telephone numbers are listed in the Appendixes in Volume 1. Background

In October 1980 the OCC instituted the Corporate 

Activities Review and Evaluation Program (CARE). This program included a comprehensive study of OCC rules, policies, procedures and forms for corporate activity filings. Filing and processing procedures, for most activities were streamlined, reducing significantly the costs and paperwork burdens on the applicants, the agency, and the public.

Manual Organization 

Volume 1 of the manual is divided into eight parts:

Table of Contents; Introduction; Initial Activities; Expansion Activities; Other Changes; OCC Activities; Policy State ments; and Appendixes.

Most parts include sections that address specific cor porate activities. For example, Expansion Activities consists of seven sections: Bank Service Corporations;

Domestic Branches, Seasonal Agencies and CBCT 

Branches; Failure Acquisitions; Fiduciary Powers;

Mergers; Operating Subsidiaries and Other Equity In vestments; and Statewise Mergers. Each section contains the following three standard subsections. • Introduction-The introduction to each section describes briefly the corporate activity (applicability), the role of other bank regulators, the background on changes in the filing process, the process, definitions, key policies, and generally applicable procedures. The latter includes references to related rules and regulations, and unusual aspects of the filings, such as special accounting treatment, state law considerations, and publication requirements. 

Documents needed for an activity are listed at the end of the introduction to each section. • Laws, Regulations, Rulings, and Issuances-This subsection sets forth the specific laws, rulings, regulations, and OCC issuances that pertain to a particular corporate activity. The information is cross-referenced to the Comptroller's Manual for 

National Banks. • Procedures-The procedures subsection explains the processing of a filing from pre-filing contacts through receipt, analysis, and decision to final action. The procedures subsection is formatted in two columns. The first column identifies the person(s) responsible for performing the steps in the process.

The second column contains corresponding stepby-step instructions grouped generally under the following headings: Prefiling, Filing the Application/Notice, Public Comments and Hearings, 

Processing the Application/Review, Decision, and

Close Out. 

Volume 2 contains applications and notices and applicant documents referenced in Volume 1. Applications and notices appear in alphabetical order by section name, followed by standard documents that are common to multiple applications and notices. Sample documents are provided in alphabetical order by document name as examples of those needed from the 1

Comptroller's Manual for Corporate Activities Introduction applicant to process the filing. Their use could reduce processing time and related legal expenses. However, the documents are samples only, and may be amended as necessary or appropriate to suit a particular filing. 

Volume 3 contains sample OCC documents in alphabetical order by document name. The documents are samples of those typically used by the OCC when processing a filing. The OCC also may deviate from the sample formats as necessary or appropriate for a particular filing. 

Applicant's Responsibility 

Generally the OCC assumes that a filing contains all the information that an applicant believes is necessary under current guidelines to aid the OCC in making an informed decision. Therefore, the OCC expects that applicants have prepared carefully, correctly, and completely all filings submitted to the OCC. Applicants requesting approval for activities or actions involving precedential, unusual, or complex legal issues must research those issues carefully before filing and provide a part of the filing with the OCC an appropriate written analysis to support the proposed activity. 

Pursuant to General Services Administration (GSA) 

policy on eliminating legal-sized files and forms, all corporate applications, notices, and supporting documentation must be submitted only on standard letter-sized paper (no larger than 8 1/2 by 11 inches).

After the initial receipt of a filing, the OCC may request additions or corrections before it begins its review. An applicant may be asked to fill in missing parts of an application or to clarify obvious errors or inconsistencies.

After the submission of that information, the OCC will review the filing. The OCC relies upon applicants to submit complete filings, reflecting their best judgment and care, and will decide an application based on the record before it. It is the applicant's continuing responsibility to ensure that the record reflects the most current relevant information. Deficiencies may form the basis for denial of a filing. 

Pursuant to 12 CFR 5.7 and once its review has begun, the OCC may request any additional information it believes is necessary to make an informed decision. 

However, during that time, those requests are limited generally to matters that need clarification or information that an applicant could not have been expected to provide under current OCC guidance.

Applicants, at their initiative, may submit additional information any time prior to when the OCC makes its decision. Sometimes the submission of additional information may require a new public notice and an additional opportunity for public comment in conformance with 12 CFR 5.8 and 5.10.

General Filing Instructions 

Generally, national bank applicants submit filings to the district office that has supervisory responsibility for the bank. New entrants to the national banking system generally submit filings to the district office that will be the supervisory office for a new national bank, federal branch, or newly acquired national bank. Bank holding companies that operate in more than one OCC district should submit filings to the district office that has (or will have) primary supervisory authority for their existing national banks. Filings from companies operating banks that are not supervised by the OCC should be sent to the Comptroller's Office in the district that will have the primary supervisory authority for the national bank. If the filing is an interstate application, the public comment period may be extended to allow sufficient time for all interested parties to respond. 2

Introduction Summary of the Process 

The process for establishing a new national bank requires substantial personal and financial commitment by the organizers and the proposed chief executive officer (CEO). OCC policy encourages and expects such a commitment. The commitment exhibited by the organizers and the proposed CEO will be an important influence on the OCC's decision to approve a new national bank charter. 

Organizers of a full service commercial national bank, a national trust company, a national credit card bank, or a national bankers' bank must receive a charter from the Office of the Comptroller of the Currency (OCC) before they commence the business of banking. The chartering process consists of five phases: pre-filing discussions, application filing and processing, preliminary decision, organization of the bank, and pre-opening procedures.

A national bankers' bank is an institution owned exclusively by depository institutions and engaged solely in providing services for other depository institutions and their officers, directors, and employees as permitted by 12 U.S.C. 27(b). Procedures for chartering a bankers' bank are incorporated throughout this section of the manual.

Pre-filing Discussions 

As soon as the organizers are prepared to proceed, a representative should contact the OCC's district office that will have supervisory authority over the new bank and schedule a pre-filing meeting. Multinational banks and their affiliates should contact the Deputy Comptroller for Multinational Banking in Washington, D.C.

At a pre-filing meeting or in informal discussions, the district OCC staff reviews with the organizing group the OCC's chartering policy and procedures and the requirements for filing an application for a national bank charter and for organizing a bank. The organizers should review this section of the manual in its entirety prior to the pre-filing meeting to be familiar with the procedures involved in chartering a new national bank. 

Any plans of a group interested in organizing a bankers' bank to seek waivers or exemptions from statutes or regulations should be discussed at the pre-filing meeting.

The district office will accept applications only after a pre-filing meeting has been held. The organizers may request a waiver of the pre-filing meeting. However, waivers are granted only under limited circumstances. 

All organizers of the proposed new national bank are expected to attend the pre-filing meeting. 

All independent organizing groups must have identified a proposed chief executive officer (CEO) before their application is filed. The proposed CEO should be actively involved in the development of the charter application and should be thoroughly familiar with the proposed bank's business plan since that person must implement the proposed plan successfully once the bank opens. It is strongly recommended that the CEO's identity be disclosed in the application. 

If the organizing group elects not to disclose the proposed CEO's name at the time of application, it must certify that a CEO has been identified and is working with the organizers during the application phase. The group must provide a detailed description of the person's background, experience and qualifications, which is sufficiently specific to permit matching the application information with the person once his/her identity is revealed. The proposed CEO must still complete and submit the Biographical Report and the Financial Report. All questions should be answered, except those that disclose identifying information. Identifying information generally is considered to include the person's name, address, social security number, the names of the financial institutions where the person is or was employed, other organizations where the person is or was affiliated as an officer, and other information which, by its nature, would readily identify the proposed CEO.

The OCC strongly encourages all organizing groups to disclose their proposed CEO at the time of application and will honor all requests for confidential treatment until preliminary approval is granted. Disclosing the proposed CEO enhances the OCC's ability to evaluate the apparent abilities of the proposed management. A strong proposed CEO, as a strong organizing group and proposed board of directors, enhances the likelihood of preliminary approval. Organizing groups that do not disclose their proposed CEO run the risk that preliminary approval could be withdrawn should that person prove unsatisfactory to the OCC once his/her identity is disclosed.

Selection of a qualified CEO is one of the organizing group's most important decisions. Organizers must investigate thoroughly the background and qualifications of the proposed CEO prior to submission to the OCC. The CEO is viewed as one of the basic factors in the OCC's overall assessment of the likelihood of a new bank's success. Failure to propose a suitable CEO reflects poorly on the organizer's judgment and raises doubts about the probable success of the proposed bank. Selection of a CEO whom the OCC finds unqualified for the position, whose prior banking ex- 3

Introduction perience is unsatisfactory, or who is otherwise unacceptable to the OCC could reflect negatively on the organizers and result in the application being disapproved. The OCC generally will not grant approvals subject to the condition that a more suitable CEO be proposed.

Application Filing and Processing 

After the pre-filing meeting the group files an application including an operating plan and biographical and financial reports on the organizers and the proposed CEO. The application must designate a member of the organizing group as spokesperson. This person will serve as the primary contact between the OCC and the organizers.

Bank holding companies and certain organizing groups that have a previous record of working together in banking may be exempted from certain filing requirements (see streamlined procedures) if banks with which they are or were affiliated are found by the OCC to have satisfactory performance records. Exemptions must be requested from and will be granted by the 

 (DDA) or the Deputy Comptroller for . 

The OCC evaluates the information in the application to determine whether the proposed bank will be operated in a safe and sound manner and will have a reasonable likelihood of success. A field investigation may be conducted based on the information submitted. 

The organizers must provide sufficient information in their initial complete application to convince the OCC that they thoughtfully conceived their plan to establish a new national bank and that the new bank has a reasonable prospect for success. Generally, the OCC will not ask organizers to expand upon the information submitted in the application. If the information provided is not sufficient to make a determination about the bank's prospects for success, the application generally will be disapproved. At any time, however, the OCC may seek clarifications or request additional information that organizers could not have reasonably been expected to provide.

Significant amendments to the application during the review period may result in the OCC concluding that the original application is obsolete. In such cases, the original application will be considered withdrawn and a new application and filing fee will be required. 

Although the OCC strives to reach a prompt decision on all applications, it cannot guarantee that delays will not occur. Processing may be delayed because of public hearing requests, the need for special analysis or investigation, or unusual scheduling problems.

Therefore, the OCC suggests that real estate or employment commitments related to an application for a new national bank include provisions for delays in obtaining preliminary approval and opening the new bank. The OCC will inform the spokesperson promptly when a decision is made.

Preliminary Decision 

Following review of the application, the OCC will decide whether to grant preliminary approval to proceed to organize or deny the request to charter a new national bank. Preliminary approval only indicates permission to proceed with the organization of the bank according to the plan set forth in the application. It is not an assurance that the OCC will grant a new national bank charter. If an application is denied, the denial letter will contain the reasons for the denial. 

A significant change in the ownership, CEO, organizers, operating plan or capitalization for the new national bank, from that originally presented, will result in the OCC reevaluating the original proposal in light of the change and may cause revocation of preliminary approval.

Preliminary approval will be revoked for failure to perform all the steps required to organize a national bank or if, prior to opening, any information becomes available to the OCC that provides sufficient cause to change its evaluation of the prospects for success of the proposed new national bank or the likelihood that the bank will be operated in a safe and sound manner. 

Organization of a New National Bank 

The organizers will have 12 months from the date of preliminary approval to sell the capital stock as proposed in the application. Failure to sell the capital stock as proposed within 12 months will cause preliminary approval to be rescinded. Extensions to the 12month period are granted under only limited circumstances. 

Stock in a bankers' bank may be sold only to depository institutions. Participating national banks involved in a bankers' bank are limited to a total investment of not more than 5 percent of capital and unimpaired surplus. 

A national bank's total investment in the stock of one or more bankers' banks is limited to 10 percent of the investing bank's unimpaired capital and surplus. 

The organizers will have 18 months from the date of preliminary approval to complete the organization of the new national bank. Failure to complete the organization within 18 months will cause preliminary ap- 4

Introduction proval to be rescinded. Extensions to the 18-month period are granted under only limited circumstances. 

During the organizing phase, the bank hires the remainder of its management, establishes its banking premises at the proposed site, raises its capital, and develops its banking systems. An independent, external audit in conformance with generally accepted auditing standards may be a condition of the approval. If so, the external auditor is hired during the organization phase and the initial audit period commences with the date the organizing group forms a body corporate.

In general, the OCC does not look favorably on a proposal, or a series of proposals, that would materially alter the underlying factors on which the decision to approve the application was based. If during the organizing phase changes occur which significantly alter the facts on which the OCC based its preliminary approval, that approval may be revoked. However, some changes enhance the application and organizers should not hesitate to propose a change that could be beneficial. All proposed changes are reviewed for significance and their effect on the basis used to grant preliminary approval. If a substantial additional review must be performed, an additional filing fee may be required. 

For example, revocation of preliminary approval may result from changes in: • Organizing group composition. • Chief executive officer. • Ownership distribution. • Adverse information on the CEO or organizers not previously disclosed. • Financial condition of organizers. • Condition of a sponsoring parent holding company. • Condition of affiliated or participating banks. • Content of the operating plan. 

Organizers must notify the OCC promptly of material changes when they occur or are proposed. Generally, previously granted approvals will be revoked if the OCC discovers material violations of law, misrepresentations, or any fraudulent activity on the part of the applicants.

In addition, proposals that normally require the filing of a separate application and fee (such as relocations, branches, fiduciary powers or other activities) will be considered as potential significant changes relative to the original charter when they are proposed subsequent to preliminary approval. 

Pre-opening Procedures

At least two weeks before the bank plans to open, the OCC examines it to ensure that it meets all requirements for commencing business. If the examination reveals that the bank has met the requirements for opening, a charter is granted. If the examination uncovers numerous exceptions or significant deviations from the originally approved proposal or if the OCC discovers other reasons for reassessing the organizers' capabilities (such as deteriorating financial condition of the organizers, parent holding company, or participating banks in a bankers' bank proposal), the opening may be delayed or the charter may not be granted and preliminary approval may be revoked.

Applicable Policies and Procedures 

General

The policies and procedures in this section cover requests for new full service national bank charters, national trust companies, national credit card banks, and national bankers' banks. Thrifts and state banks wishing to convert to national bank status should follow the procedures in the "Conversion" section of this manual.

Holding companies wishing to charter an interim national bank to facilitate a corporate reorganization should follow the procedures in the "Mergers" section of this manual. A foreign bank wanting to establish a federal branch or agency should follow the "Establishing a Federal Branch or Agency" section of this manual. Existing banking organizations that wish to engage in fiduciary activities should follow the procedures in the "Fiduciary Powers" section of this manual.

Limited Charters 

A national bank is authorized by its charter to exercise all expressed or implied powers of national banks. A national trust company, a credit card bank, and a bankers' bank limit their activities through their articles of association to the exercise of specific powers. If a national trust company, a national credit card bank, or a bankers' bank wants to expand their powers to full service commercial bank powers, they must amend their articles of association and submit a request to the district office that includes a proposed operating plan, filing fee, and list of management. Although a complete application is not required, the district may request certain parts of the new bank charter application, to evaluate sufficiently the proposed change to full service banking. 5

Introduction Bank Holding Company Sponsored Charters 

Bank holding companies, or others affiliated with the same banking organizations, that wish to obtain a national bank charter must comply with the policies and procedures in this section. In addition, bank holding companies must obtain Federal Reserve Board approval to establish a new bank before the OCC will issue the charter. 

A new bank is a subsidiary of a holding company if 25 percent or more of its voting stock is owned or controlled by a holding company or if the Federal Reserve Board determines that a holding company otherwise has the power to elect a majority of the directors or to control the bank in any other manner. An entity controlling 25 percent or more of a bank must register as a bank holding company if the controlled bank is covered by the definition of a bank found in the Bank Holding Company Act (12 U.S.C. 1841(c)). Certain limited purpose banks (e.g., credit card banks, trust banks) are not defined as banks under the Bank Holding Company Act. For such banks, parent companies need not register with the Federal Reserve as bank holding companies. However, a holding company may be considered a bank holding company under state law and organizers are encouraged to have legal counsel review state law in this regard prior to filing. 

Streamlined Procedures

The full procedures in this section are appropriate when the OCC does not know the capabilities of the organizing group or the resources available to them. The OCC may waive several requirements and procedural steps for certain organizing groups experienced in banking, or when the bank is being established by an existing banking organization. Applicants qualifying for streamlined procedures may be exempted from: filing detailed market analyses and financial plans; providing much of the biographical and financial information on directors, shareholders and management; and designating the chief executive officer with the application. 

An organizing group is considered "experienced-inbanking" if a majority of its members have three or more years of significant involvement in policymaking as directors or executive officers in the same or affiliated federally insured institutions which the OCC finds have performed satisfactorily. Executive officer positions normally are defined as all vice presidents and above 

(see Federal Reserve Board Regulation O). 

Generally, the following applications will be eligible for streamlined procedures: • Those filed by bank holding companies when the holding company and/or its subsidiary banks have been in operation for three or more years and are not subject to special supervisory concerns. • Those filed by independent organizing groups when a majority of its members exhibit banking experience and cohesiveness. To demonstrate cohesiveness, a majority of the members must have been associated for the past three or more years as directors or officers of the same or affiliated financial institutions that are in satisfactory condition. They must state their intentions regarding affiliation with the institution should the new bank proposal receive approval. 

For streamlined procedures, an "existing bank holding company" is defined as one that has previously received Federal Reserve Board approval to become a bank holding company and has been in operation for at least three years prior to its application to organize a new bank. An affiliated bank is one which meets the definition found in 12 U.S.C. 221a. 

To be eligible for streamlined procedures, the OCC should be able to safely extrapolate from the applicants' record that they possess sufficient knowledge of banking practices, trends, laws, and regulations; have the ability to perceive and respond to changing market forces; and operate banks in a safe and sound manner.

The cohesiveness of independent groups must be well established and proven. If the CEO has not worked previously with the organizing group, streamlined procedures may be denied.

In granting streamlined procedures, the OCC also considers the characteristics of the market for the new bank because the criteria for the success of a new bank in a volatile, highly competitive, or changing market may differ from past experience. If the organizing group demonstrates cohesiveness but lacks experience in operating in a particular type of market, the OCC may deny streamlined procedures. 

Organizers must request streamlined procedures in the 

Application to Organize a National Bank (see Applications and Notices, New Bank Charters, Application). The request should contain sufficient evidence that the organizing group meets the criteria for streamlined procedures. Based on the information provided in the application, the OCC will determine if streamlined procedures are applicable. If the organizing group does not qualify for streamlined processing, it must submit all the information required under the standard procedures.

The OCC will make its decision on an application accepted for streamlined processing on the basis of the 6

Introduction information provided. For example, if information developed during the review reveals that two subsidiaries of a bank holding company seeking an additional charter are in less than satisfactory condition, the OCC may determine that the proposed bank does not have a reasonable likelihood of success, based on the holding company's record. The application will be denied, and the OCC will not request the organizers to supplement it with an operating plan that might prove that the company is capable of successfully operating a new bank.

Another example is an independent organizing group that is granted streamlined procedures because it claims in the application to be experienced-in-banking. 

However, if the OCC finds in processing the application that less than a majority of the organizers satisfy that requirement, the application may be denied. 

The OCC reserves the right to require additional information or to terminate streamlined processing at any time to reach an informed judgment about the application. 

Filing Fees 

The appropriate filing fee must accompany an application to charter a national bank (refer to 12 CFR 8 and the Annual Notice of the Comptroller of the Currency Fees). An application is deemed incomplete if the filing fee is not included. Filing fees are not refunded. 

Legal Opinions 

The OCC expects applicants requesting approval for activities or actions involving precedential, unusual, or complex legal issues to research the issues carefully before filing and provide the appropriate written analysis in support of the proposed activity as part of their filing. Both the applicant and the OCC benefit when the applicant has addressed adequately significant legal issues raised by an application. Among other things, the processing of the filing can proceed more efficiently.

Organizing Groups and Management 

Organizing groups applying for a national bank charter must be comprised of five or more persons. The group must have identified a proposed CEO before the application for a charter is made. Management is critical to the success of a new bank and selection of a qualified CEO is one of the organizing group's most important decisions. The organizers are responsible for thoroughly investigating the background and qualifications of the candidate prior to submission to the OCC and for involving the proposed CEO in the application process. 

Selection of a CEO whom the OCC finds unqualified for the position, whose prior banking experience is unsatisfactory, or who is otherwise unacceptable to the OCC could reflect negatively on the organizers and result in disapproval or revocation of preliminary approval in those cases where the CEO's identity was not disclosed in the application. 

Information on the proposed CEO, including the identity if disclosed in the application, will be treated confidentially, if requested, until preliminary approval of the charter is granted.

To insure the equal treatment of all CEO candidates, the 

OCC has established the following general guidelines.

Under those rules, the potential CEO: • Must possess skills that complement the directors' skills and fit the needs of the new national bank. • Must be thoroughly familiar with the proposed new bank's operating plan. • Should have managed a bank or similar financial institution successfully or have successful experience as an officer in an area relevant to the proposed bank's marketing strategy and needs. • Should have executive experience in operations or administration.

These guidelines are consistent with the OCC's requirement that a well-rounded person be chosen for the leadership of a new national bank. Decisions regarding a proposed CEO are based on a person's suitability for a particular position with a new bank and are not intended to determine eligibility for other jobs in existing banking institutions.

The proposed bank must be able to attract executive officers with the experience, competence, and willingness to manage the bank as required by the operating plan. If the operating plan contains proposals for specialized types of services, executive officers should have experience relevant to the development and administration of those services. The identification of officers at an early date is beneficial and reflects positively on the organizing group and its operating plan.

The Depository Management Interlocks Act (Act) (12 

U.S.C. 3201) prohibits management interlocks between nonaffiliated depository organizations. The regulation (12 CFR 26) allows the OCC to permit an otherwise prohibited interlock under limited circumstances. An interlock relationship may be permitted if one of the organizations involved is a newly-chartered bank and 7

Introduction the relationship is deemed "necessary to provide management or operating expertise to [the] organization." Specifically required information on the interlocking relationship is detailed in the accompanying procedures section and should be submitted by the organizing group. 

Community Service, Environmental Effects, and Historic Properties 

The organizing group must know and have plans for serving its community. The operating plan must reflect the organizing group's plans for achieving the bank's affirmative obligation to help meet the credit needs of its entire community, including low- and moderate-income neighborhoods. The Community Reinvestment 

Act (CRA) applies to all new bank charters, except banks engaged solely in correspondent banking business, trust company business, or acting as a clearing agent. Those limited purpose banks are specifically exempt under 12 CFR 25.101.

The proposed new bank charter plans must not have an adverse effect on the environment or on historical properties.

Decision Factors 

The statutory factors that the OCC must consider in deciding whether to approve or disapprove applications to organize a bank are: • The bank's future earning prospects. • The general character of its management. • The adequacy of its capital structure. • The convenience and needs of the community to be served by the bank. 

Its compliance with the National Bank Act and the 

Federal Deposit Insurance Act. 

• The risk presented by the proposed bank to the insurance fund.

In making those judgments, the OCC is guided by the following principles: • OCC has responsibility for maintaining a sound banking system. • The marketplace normally is the best regulator of economic activity. • Competition promotes a sound and more efficient banking system that better serves customers.

Operating Plan 

The operating plan of the organizing group must show that the proposed bank will: • Have reasonable earning prospects. • Be able to hire and retain competent managers. • Maintain adequate capital. • Offer services responsive to community needs. • Be operated in a safe and sound manner in compliance with applicable laws, policies, and procedures. 

Operating plan assumptions about the market must be reasonable, and projections consistent. The operating plan should present data that accurately reflects the economic condition of the delineated market and addresses statutory and regulatory changes that may affect the operations of the proposed bank. Proposals should reflect the "realities" of the marketplace. The organizers, as well as the proposed CEO, should be familiar with the operating plan and their part in its successful implementation.

Capital 

The organizing group must plan to raise capital sufficient to support the projected volume and type of business planned. Factors that are considered in determining the adequacy of capital include: • Organizing expenses. • Earning prospects. • Economic and competitive conditions in the community to be served. • The experience and competence of management. • The risks inherent in the expected asset and liability mix. • The amount of fixed asset investment. • The dependability of plans to raise, or the ability of the directors to supply, additional capital when needed. Initial tangible Tier 1 capital, after organizing expenses are capitalized or charged to the bank's capital, must be adequate to successfully implement the proposed operating plan and effectively compete in the selected market area. Organizers must justify their proposed capital level to the OCC. The OCC may determine that higher or lower amounts of capital from that proposed are necessary based on local market conditions or the operating plan of the organizing group. 8

Introduction 

Generally, the OCC does not look favorably on new bank proposals that rely on debt or other instruments that require repayment or dividends during a new bank's initial years.

Capital must be raised within one year from the date of preliminary approval, and the bank must complete its organization and begin operations within 18 months of preliminary approval. Generally, the OCC will not grant extensions.

Organizer Compensation 

OCC considers the general character of management and the adequacy of the capital structure when evaluating a new bank charter. Compensation proposals that reflect negatively on those aspects of an application are unacceptable.

OCC guidelines for compensation proposals follow these general principles: 

1. Compensation should be structured so it does not hinder the bank's future ability to raise required capital. 

2. Compensation should be structured such that organizers assume financial risk. Rewards should be proportional to the financial success of the bank. 

3. Compensation should be structured so that any gains are proportionate to each organizer's financial risk and the success of the bank. 

Compensation proposals that allow organizers to purchase or acquire a separate class of holding company stock with greater voting rights are unacceptable. 

Proposals that allow organizers to purchase bank or holding company stock at an original issue price lower than that paid by other investors are also unacceptable. 

Such arrangements may hinder the bank's ability to raise needed capital since other investors do not receive control proportionate to their financial investment. In addition, they allow organizer control without a proportionate financial commitment. Immediate stock appreciation resulting from such arrangements allows organizers to benefit from the mere establishment of the bank without regard to its financial success. 

Another method of compensating new bank organizers is with warrants. Typical plans grant organizers one warrant for each share of stock purchased. Each warrant allows the organizer to purchase an additional share of stock at the original offering price during a certain period. When the bank is successful, organizers can benefit by increasing their investment in the bank at a price less than the current market price.

Warrants can be an acceptable form of compensation when structured properly. For example, a structure of one warrant for each share purchased, exercisable at original issue price for 10 years or less, is acceptable. 

The 10-year maximum term places an upper limit on the reward. The size of the reward depends on the financial success of the bank/bank holding company as reflected in the increase in its stock price over the original issue price. Warrants granted to organizers with an exercise price set below the original issue price are not acceptable since they allow organizers to benefit from the mere establishment of the bank. 

The potential dilutive effects of organizer warrants may hinder a bank's efforts to raise capital. As a result, the OCC will require that warrants be subject to an exercise or forfeit provision that will be triggered if capital falls below regulatory minimums as set forth in 12 CFR 3 or a higher requirement as determined by the OCC, and if it is determined that the existence of outstanding warrants impairs the bank's ability to raise capital.

Background Investigations 

The organizing group and its CEO must have the experience, competence, and willingness to direct the bank's affairs in a safe, sound, and legal manner. Organizing groups that do not meet those criteria are denied permission to organize a bank. Organizers and 

CEOS whose previous banking experience is tied to failed or problem financial institutions will be closely scrutinized by the OCC to determine their ability to safely and adequately carry out their duties. 

The OCC will conduct background checks on candidates for the position of CEO, president, other executive officer positions, as well as all organizers, principal shareholders, and directors. The OCC's investigation will scrutinize the competence, experience, integrity, and financial ability of these persons. The OCC will make an independent determination of the accuracy and completeness of any information submitted in conjunction with those persons and must have no objection to their serving in their proposed capacity. 

All information filed in connection with any application is subject to review and verification. Any material misrepresentation or omission of fact in an application will generally result in its disapproval. If a misrepresentation or omission is discovered after the bank has commenced business, the charter may be revoked and the bank closed. Additionally, such a finding may subject the organizing group to severe criminal penalties, including those provided in 18 U.S.C. 1001. 9

Introduction 

The OCC routinely performs Federal Bureau of Investigation (FBI) and other background checks on persons seeking entry into the national banking system. The 

OCC performs standard background checks on all such persons through the: • FBI. • United States Custom Service. 

Drug Enforcement Administration. 

• LEXIS-NEXIS and Enforcement and Compliance In formation System (ECIS) records search. • Other regulatory agencies' records when the acquiring parties have been with institutions under another agency's jurisdiction.

The OCC may not conduct some of the standard background checks if, in individual cases, the performance of the check would be redundant, would not serve the OCC's purpose for background investigation, or for other reasons. However, the OCC will pursue any additional background checks believed appropriate to the circumstances. Checks may include, but are not limited to, requests for background information from the Internal Revenue Service (IRS), Securities Exchange Com mission (SEC), Commodity Futures Trading Commission (CFTC), National Association of Securities Dealers (NASD), INTERPOL, Central Intelligence Agency (CIA), U.S. Immigration and Naturalization Ser vice, World Bank, International Monetary Fund (IMF),

U.S. State Department, and state and local law enforcement agencies. The OCC may require fingerprints from an acquiring party if they are needed to access information necessary to complete a background investigation. Any information received by the OCC as a result of its background investigation may be shared with the other federal financial institutions' regulators. 

The OCC may request additional information deemed necessary to make an independent determination of the accuracy and completeness of any statements in the charter application.

Other Factors 

Any contracts with an executive officer for services should include provisions addressing obligations of, and options available to, the parties in the event that the OCC disapproves the charter application, or objects to a proposed executive officer. In addition, the 

OCC reviews and must have no objections to all directors and executive officers appointed during the first two years the bank is open for business. 

The OCC may require that, prior to opening, the national bank engage an independent, external auditor to perform an audit in accordance with generally accepted auditing standards of sufficient scope to enable the auditor to render an opinion on the financial statements of the bank (or consolidated holding company), taken as a whole. The audit period shall commence on the date that the organizing group forms a body corporate and may end on any calendar quarter-end no later than 12 months after the bank opens. The OCC expects that such audits will be performed annually for at least three years following commencement of operations. Engagement of an auditor will be verified during the pre-opening examination.

Organizing groups that wish to apply for approval to establish branches in conjunction with their new bank application must: • Submit complete branch/CBCT applications and the appropriate filing fee. • Reflect in the operating plan the impact of branches. • Ensure that the proposed Community Reinvestment Act statement addresses the areas served by the branches.

Organizing groups that wish to apply for fiduciary powers must incorporate the trust department objectives into the operating plan. At a minimum the plan should state the projected earnings, size, services, and target market of the trust department. In addition, the appropriate fiduciary powers filing fee must be submitted and the requirements in the Fiduciary Powers section of this manual must be met. These include: • An opinion of bank counsel that the proposed activities are not in contravention of state or local law. • A statement that the capital and surplus of the bank is not less than that required by state law for state banks, trust companies, and other corporations that exercise fiduciary powers.

Biographical Reports on the proposed trust management, including educational and professional credentials and five-year employment history with specific reference to trust area experience.

FDIC insurance is required if a national trust company will accept deposits. The proposal should clearly state that deposits are not anticipated to prevent FDIC assessment by the insurance coverage. Generally, a fiduciary powers request will be decided at the same time as the charter application. However, a fiduciary powers certificate will not be issued until the bank opens, at which time the bank can begin to exercise those powers. 10

Introduction Standards of Review 

Operating plans from experienced organizing groups that apply for a bank charter in an area with a strong local economy and weak competition generally will be reviewed only to determine that they present a reasonable strategy for success. If the market analysis is reasonable and the strategy is clear and within the reach of a regular banking business, no further analysis normally is needed. If unprecedented or unusual banking services or corporate arrangements are proposed, additional information and a more intensive review may be required.

Operating plans for areas where the economy is weak, or the competition strong, will require a more intensive review. If the organizing group is inexperienced in banking or in the areas of banking it plans to enter, the OCC will pay particular attention to the management and business strategies outlined in the plan as they relate to the market and to the qualifications of the proposed chief executive officer. The plan must be reasonable and persuade the reviewer that the results projected are consistent with the market and competitive environment.

For applications sponsored by bank holding companies or individuals affiliated with other banking organizations, the OCC will evaluate the record of performance of the bank holding company and/or affiliated institution(s). The OCC may review FR Y-6 reports, SEC 10K reports, Annual Reports to Stockholders, reports of examination, financial statements, and any other information available to it in its supervisory capacity. In addition, a bank holding company's overall philosophy and plans, for example, strategy, capital, management, and profitability, will be reviewed for consistency and compatibility with the proposed bank's operating plan. The extent to which the bank's condition will be affected by the overall condition of the consolidated organization will be considered. Serious problems at an affiliate might require management's full attention to assure resolution of those problems prior to further expansion.

The OCC recognizes that the strength of a parent, combined with the direct support it offers, can mitigate concerns over capital, operating plan, or other supervisory concerns. Conversely, the poor condition of a parent company and/or its affiliates, or the absence of any evidence of the parent's support of its subsidiaries, may result in negative action regarding the proposed new bank even though the proposal may have merit. 

Accordingly, the OCC may deny or conditionally approve holding company sponsored new bank applications if the condition of the parent company and/or any affiliates is subject to supervisory concern. Conversely, when the bank holding company or affiliated institutions serve as substantial sources of strength, applications may be approved even in markets in which economic conditions are weak and/or competitive conditions are intense.

If a group applying for a new bank charter plans to establish simultaneously a one bank holding company, the application should discuss and disclose the holding company activities as well as those of the bank. The analysis of the holding company activities and plans are an important part of the charter process. 

Organizers may file amendments to the application during the review process if they consider it necessary. 

Substantial amendments may result in additional filing fees being assessed and/or the original filing being considered withdrawn. Numerous changes to an application during the review process may not reflect positively on the group.

Publication Requirements and Comment Periods 

In compliance with 12 CFR 5.8, all applicants, except existing bank holding companies, must publish a notice of application in a newspaper of general circulation in the community in which the proposed bank will be located. Existing holding companies are exempt from this requirement, because they are subject to similar public notice requirements established by the 

Federal Reserve Board (12 CFR 262.3(b)(1)). Prelimi nary approval of proposals involving bank holding companies will be rescinded, if as a result of the subsequent publication by the bank holding company, any information becomes available to the OCC that provides sufficient cause to change its evaluation of the proposal. 

The notice should state that an application is being filed as of the date of publication and should contain the name of the organizers and the subject matter of the application. Identification of the proposed CEO in the published notice is left to the discretion of the organizers.

A statement containing the date of publication and the name and address of the newspaper in which the notice appeared is furnished with the application. If the application is an interstate filing, the 30-day public comment period may be extended to allow sufficient time for all interested parties to comment. 

Interested persons have 30 days from the date of publication of the notice of application to comment or request a hearing. After the close of the public com- 11

Introduction ment period, no further submissions by interested parties may be entered into the record, unless the public comment period is reopened. OCC staff may not comment publicly on an application under review or make any statement that could be construed as favoring one decision over another.

When a charter is granted, the new bank must publish notice of the charter in a newspaper printed in the city or county where the bank is located. The notice must appear at least once each week for nine consecutive weeks immediately following the opening of the bank for business (see 12 U.S.C. 28).

Additional Procedural Considerations 

After an application is filed, it is reviewed to ensure that it is technically complete. If an application is incomplete, it will be returned for corrections, or the applicants will be informed of the required corrections by letter, or by telephone if they are minor. Applicants will generally be afforded only one opportunity to make technical corrections. If corrections are not made upon resubmission, the application may be considered withdrawn, and the filing fees will not be refunded. An application is technically complete when all questions have been answered and required information submitted. Technical completeness does not imply that the content will be found acceptable or that the application will be approved. In this context, "technical deficiencies" means information that was omitted in the original submission.

All contacts between the OCC and the organizing group will be made through the spokesperson identified at the pre-filing meeting and in the application. 

The spokesperson must be a member of the organizing group and of the board of directors of the proposed bank. If necessary, the OCC may conduct a meeting with the spokesperson, the chief executive officer, and any of the other members of the organizing group to discuss the application. Once the bank is in organization (after preliminary approval), the organizing directors of the association will designate one of their members as a correspondent for contact with the OCC. 

All directors must comply with the requirements set forth in 12 U.S.C. 72. If a request for citizenship waiver is made, procedures detailed in the Citizenship Waiver section of this manual must be followed. 

Generally, expenses incurred by the organizing group in making an application and organizing a bank may be charged to the bank's capital or may be capitalized provided they are documented and disclosed to current and prospective shareholders (see Documents- 

Applicant, Proxy Statement, Standard Sample Docu ment). At the first meeting of the shareholders, those expenses and commitments must be approved and included as an itemized list in the minutes. Expenses typically include the filing and organizational fees, cost of professional and consulting services, travel expenses, printing, postage, telephone, supplies, salaries and rent, and lease or purchase of fixed assets. The 

OCC may prohibit payment of clearly excessive expenses from bank funds or require that additional capital, sufficient to compensate for excessive expenses, be raised prior to the opening of the bank. 

Although some professional and consulting assistance is normal and may be charged properly to the bank's operating expenses, organizers are expected to contribute time and expertise to the bank's organization and should neither unduly rely upon, nor make an excessive charge to its accounts for those services. 

Generally, the existence of a contract involving a fee contingent upon any action, decision, or forbearance by the OCC will be grounds for disapproval of the application or for withdrawal of the preliminary approval. 

Organizational expenses for disapproved applications are the sole responsibility of the organizing group. Such expenses for banks that do not open must be paid by the directors and organizers.

All public bank stock offerings must comply with 12 

CFR 16 and receive OCC clearance. Bank holding companies are exempt from preparing offering circulars for the sale of bank stock. Sale of holding company stock may require filing of documents and/or review/registration with the SEC. Non-public offerings may be exempt from the requirements of 12 CFR 16 (see 12 CFR 16.5).

When substantially similar state and national charter applications are filed by the same organizing group or persons representing the same interest, the OCC generally will consider the national bank application abandoned unless the state bank application is not being processed in due course. 

The decision to lease or purchase the bank premises is the responsibility of the organizing group. However, there are statutory limits on investments in fixed assets. (See 12 U.S.C. 371d, Interpretive Ruling 7.3100 and

FASB #13 for limitations on investment in land, building, leasehold improvements, capitalized leases, furniture, fixtures, and equipment.) Proceeds from the sale of stock must be held in escrow and may not be used to finance the construction/acquisition of bank premises. 

Construction of the banking house must be in compliance with the minimum security standards set out in 12

Introduction 

12 U.S.C. 1882 and 12 CFR 21. After opening for business, the bank will file a report annually, certifying that the bank complies with the stated security standards (see 12 CFR 21.5). If the organizers have entered into a contract (purchase or lease), they should submit a copy of it with the application. 

Extension Policy 

Generally, requests for extensions to raise capital beyond the 12-month period or to commence business beyond the 18-month period will be denied unless dictated by factors clearly beyond the control of the applicants. For example, an extension for a new bank charter may be granted if last minute delays in construction of the interior of an existing building will prevent opening. Decisions on requests for extensions will be processed on a case-by-case basis. 

Limited Purpose Charter Procedures 

Bankers' Banks

Background 

The Garn-St Germain Depository Institutions Act of 

1982 (P.L. 97-320) ("Act") authorizes the Comptroller of the Currency to charter bankers' banks that are owned exclusively by depository institutions and organized to engage exclusively in providing services for other depository institutions and their officers, directors, and employees. Subject to any rules, regulations, and orders deemed appropriate by the Comptroller, a national bankers' bank has the same rights and powers and is subject to the same limitations that would apply under the national banking laws to a national bank. 

The Comptroller may rule on requests from nationally chartered bankers' banks, or from those applying to charter a national bankers' bank, for waiver of specific statutes and regulations. Requests for such waivers should accompany the application and must be supported by adequate justification and legal analysis. 

Key Policies 

A national bank is permitted to own up to 5 percent of the voting shares of a federally insured bankers' bank or of a bank holding company that controls a bankers' bank, provided that all subsidiaries of the holding company provide services only to depository institutions. A national bank's total investment in the stock of one or more bankers' banks is limited to 10 percent of the investing bank's unimpaired capital and surplus. 

Generally Applicable Procedures 

Applicants should follow the procedures detailed in this section of the manual for chartering a new national bank. Organizing groups for a national bankers' bank generally are subject to the same requirements and standards as specified in 12 CFR 5.20 for organizers of a national bank. The OCC also accepts applications for the establishment of a national bankers' bank on a similar application form and under the same procedures used to establish a national bank. 

Organizing groups should list the anticipated activities of the proposed bankers' bank in the application, request any waivers deemed appropriate for their particular scope of operations, and justify such requests with adequate analysis. The OCC will review on a case-by-case basis, waiver requests by national bankers' banks and make individual determinations as to whether the facts as presented by the organizers support the waiver. 

Trust Companies

The OCC charters national trust companies on the same basis that it approves any national bank charter, that is, that the proposal exhibits a likelihood of success. Generally, all policies and procedures in this section apply to chartering a trust company. The organizing group must propose a business plan and sufficient capital to ensure a successful operation. 

If the trust company will accept deposits that will not be immediately invested, FDIC insurance will be required. 

As a standard condition to approving a trust company charter, the OCC requires that the trust company's articles of association limit the activities via article two to the operations of a trust department, and to support activities incidental thereto.

Proposals for a trust company should include projected trust income and expense and projected trust assets and type of trust activities. When an existing trust department will become a separately chartered trust company, the application should include the last three years Trust Call Reports in addition to proformas. 

Credit Card Banks 

Similar to trust companies, the OCC charters credit card banks based on the bank's likelihood of success. 

Banks may chose to limit their activities to credit card operations by stating the limits in article two of their articles of association.

The Competitive Equality Banking Act of 1987 (CEBA) 

(12 U.S.C. 1841(c)) included a special definition of credit card banks that limited their activities to: (i) engage only in credit card operations; 13

Introduction 

(ii) not accept demand deposits or deposits that the depositor may withdraw by check or similar means for payment to third parties or others; (iii) not accept any savings or time deposits of less than $100,000; (iv) maintain only one office that accepts deposits; and (v) not engage in the business of making commercial loans 

Holding companies that control CEBA credit card banks do not need to register as bank holding companies under the Bank Holding Company Act.

Credit card banks are required to be FDIC-insured and are subject to the Community Reinvestment Act (CRA). 

Generally, all policies and procedures in this section apply to credit card banks.

Conversion from Limited to Full Service Charter 

General guidelines are provided in this section for changing an existing national trust company or national credit card bank to a full service bank via an amendment to the company's articles of association.
