Introduction Applicability 

A national bank that wishes to establish a domestic branch (brick and mortar), seasonal agency, or Customer-Bank Communication Terminal (CBCT) branch must apply to the Office of the Comptroller of the Currency (OCC) for approval and authorization. Applications and instructions to relocate a domestic or CBCT branch are found in Change in Location section of the 

Comptroller's Manual for Corporate Activities. 

The conversion of an existing CBCT to a brick and mortar branch requires a new branch application and the standard filing fee. The conversion of an existing brick and mortar branch to a CBCT requires only that the appropriate district office be given prior written notification of the conversion. No application is required.

An application to establish a CBCT branch jointly with one or more national banks or other financial institutions may be submitted by only one of the national banks acting as agent for the other parties. The application must indicate in an attachment the names and head office addresses of all of the national banks that propose to share the CBCT branch. A national bank that arranges for its customers to use another financial institution's automated teller device on a transactional fee basis does not have to file a separate CBCT branch application.

Approval is not required to install additional CBCT-type devices at an existing approved CBCT branch location. 

Additionally, CBCTS to be established at the main office or an existing authorized branch do not need approval. 

A cash dispensing machine or similar device on bank premises solely for use by bank employees is not a branch pursuant to 12 U.S.C. 36, and therefore, requires no application.

A CBCT branch application must be filed for a bank to operate a point of sale (POS) terminal where it has purchased or rents the terminal. However, when a bank is merely a placing member for an electronic funds transfer system (EFTS) and the POS terminals are purchased, owned, and operated by an entity other than the bank, no application is required. 

An application is not required to establish a night depository on the premises of a CBCT branch to provide deposit acceptance for those customers who do not have ATM privileges for their accounts, provided such arrangements are allowed under state law. A CBCT application is sufficient when a night depository is constructed into the exterior of a CBCT and will provide a service that may also be provided by the CBCT. 

Periodically, a national bank desires to establish banking facilities on military installations. If a national bank desires such a facility, contact the OCC for further information.

Background 

In 1982, OCC policies and regulations were changed to reflect the position that certain corporate activities, such as the geographic distribution of banking facilities, are legitimate business decisions of the applicant and should be permitted with minimal regulatory involvement, except when legal issues arise or there are concerns about supervisory or Community Reinvestment Act (CRA) performance. Additionally, in 1982, CBCT branch filing procedures were changed to permit a bank to submit a single application to establish as many CBCT branches as it wished within a nine-month period.

Summary of Process Domestic Branches and Seasonal Agencies 

For domestic branches and seasonal agencies, banks submit an application with a filing fee to the appropriate OCC district office (multinational banks and their affiliates to Multinational Banking Division in Washington).

They also publish notice of the proposed branch in a local newspaper. Interested parties generally have 30 days from the date of publication to comment on the application. Applicants must familiarize themselves with the capital requirements of 12 U.S.C. 36 and 51. 

The OCC reviews the application for accuracy and completeness. The application must include: • The exact location of the branch or seasonal agency. • Certification of publication of the application. • Certification that the establishment of the proposed branch and/or seasonal agency complies with state laws, including citations of relevant statutes. • Certification that the capital requirements of 12 

U.S.C. 36 and 51 have been met. • Certification of compliance with 12 U.S.C. 371d and 

12 CFR 7.3100 regarding investment limits in bank premises. • Discussion of any planned variances in the applicant's lending policy, procedures, or services at the proposed branch. .. 61

Introduction 

• Certification that the proposed branch does not violate the provisions of the National Environmental 

Policy Act and the National Historic Preservation Act. 

• Certification that the establishment of the branch or seasonal agency does not involve a prohibited insider transaction or management interlock. 

If the proposed branch or seasonal agency involves issues not in compliance with the aforementioned laws and regulations, the bank must submit information with its application that will allow the OCC to determine whether there is a violation. 

After the 30-day comment period, the applications for domestic branches and seasonal agencies are analyzed and a decision made to grant preliminary or conditional approval or to deny the application. Any comments received within the 30-day comment period are considered in the decision together with any requests for a hearing. Within seven days after the decision, the OCC notifies the applicant in writing of the decision. If approved, the bank has 18 months in which to open the branch. If the branch is not opened within that time, the approval expires and a new application is required. An extension may be granted in writing by the district office, if extraordinary circumstances prevented the opening. The applicant must notify the district office 10 days before opening, whereupon the OCC will issue a letter authorizing the operation of the branch. A branch may not be opened until the authorization letter is issued by the OCC. 

CBCT Branches 

For CBCT branches, the bank mails an application and filing fee to the appropriate district office (multinational banks and their affiliates to Multinational Banking Division in Washington). The filing date of the application is the date that it was sent by registered mail, return receipt requested. The bank may request approval, through a single application, for more than one CBCT branch. Each proposed location must be listed in the application.

Within five days after filing a CBCT application, the bank publishes once in a newspaper(s) of general circulation in the community(ies) in which the CBCT branch(es) will be located, a notice containing: (1) the name of applicant; (2) the subject matter of the application; (3) the date on which the application was filed; and (4) a statement that any written comments on the application must be submitted within 10 days to the Deputy Comptroller (District) (or the Deputy Comptroller for  for multinational banks and their affiliates). Immediately thereafter, the applicant furnishes the district office or DCMNB with a statement containing the date of publication and the name and address of each newspaper in which the notice was published. 

For CBCT branch applications, if the bank has not been notified on or before 11 days after the end of the 10-day comment period that further consideration is required, it may conclude that the application is approved and proceed to establish the CBCT branch(es). Written notification of approvals will be forwarded to all interested parties within 30 days after the end of the comment period. If the application is disapproved, the bank will be informed of the basis for the decision. 

A CBCT branch will be considered established on the date it opens for customer use. Within seven days of that date, the bank must notify the district office by letter, confirming the location(s) of the CBCT branch(es) and the opening date(s) and the popular name(s) by which the branch(es) will be known. CBCT branch approvals will expire if the branches are not opened within nine months after the date of approval. Definitions

A domestic branch, as provided in 12 U.S.C. 36(f), includes any branch bank, office or agency; additional office; or any branch place of business, except a CBCT branch, located in any state or territory of the United States or in the District of Columbia at which deposits are received, checks paid, or money lent. 

A seasonal agency is an office established in a resort community to receive and disburse deposits, issue and cash checks and drafts, and perform incidental business. Seasonal agencies of national banks may be approved by the OCC only as permitted by state law and if no bank is located and doing business where the proposed agency will be located. Capital requirements of 12 U.S.C. 36 do not apply to seasonal agencies. Any authorization of a seasonal agency will be revoked upon the opening of a state or national bank in such community.

A CBCT branch is an automated device, established (i.e., owned or rented) by a national bank at a location separate from the main office or a domestic branch, that takes deposits or disburses cash drawn against a customer's deposit account or a customer's pre-approved loan account.

An automated device that otherwise would qualify as a CBCT branch, but which is permanently staffed (either full- or part-time, directly or indirectly) by personnel employed by the applicant, is a domestic branch that requires an application pursuant to 12 CFR 5.30. 62

Introduction Key Policies 

The OCC approves applications to establish and operate domestic branches, seasonal agencies, and CBCT branches, provided that the approval would not violate the provisions of applicable federal or state law. 

In determining whether to approve an application to establish and operate such offices, the OCC is guided by the following principles: • It must encourage a bank to help meet the credit needs of the bank's entire community. • The marketplace normally is the best regulator of economic activity. • Competition promotes a sound and efficient banking system that serves customers well. 

As provided in 12 CFR 5.13, the OCC reserves the right to deny applications or to grant approval subject to fulfillment of certain conditions, if: • There are significant supervisory concerns about the applicant or any affiliated organizations as defined by 12 U.S.C. 221a. • The applicant's record of helping to meet the credit needs of its entire community, including low- and moderate-income neighborhoods, consistent with the safe and sound operation of the bank, is less than satisfactory. • Any financial or other business arrangement, direct or indirect, involving the proposed domestic branch, seasonal agency, or CBCT branch and bank insiders (directors, officers, employees, and shareholders owning or controlling, directly or indirectly, 10 percent or more of any class of the subject bank's voting stock) includes terms and conditions more favorable to the insiders than would be available in a comparable transaction with unrelated parties. 

Capital 

Capital requirements under 12 U.S.C. 36 provide that the aggregate capital of every national bank and its branches shall equal the capital and surplus required of state chartered institutions for the establishment of branches.

In addition, national banks are subject to other statutory and regulatory minimum capital requirements. The minimum of capital required pursuant to 12 U.S.C. 51 is determined by the population of cities or other places where the bank has offices. A bank must have at least $50,000 in capital stock for each city in which it has an office(s) and the population does not exceed 6,000; at least $100,000 in capital stock for each city in which it has an office(s) and the population is between 6,001 and 50,000; and at least $200,000 in capital stock for each city in which it has an office(s) and the population is greater than 50,000. In addition, the bank must have surplus at least equal to 20 percent of capital stock. 

The applicant should also refer to 12 CFR 3, Minimum Capital Ratios, for the required minimal acceptable capital ratio for national banks. 

Generally Applicable Procedures Technically Complete Applications 

An application is reviewed to ensure technical completeness. An incomplete application will be returned for correction or the applicant will be informed of the required corrections by letter, or by telephone, if they are minor. The applicant will be afforded one opportunity to correct technical deficiencies. Failure to correct deficiencies upon resubmission may cause the application to be considered withdrawn. Filing fees will not be refunded. An application is technically complete when all questions have been answered and required information submitted. Technical completeness does not imply that the content will be found acceptable or that the application will be approved. In this context, "technical deficiencies" means information that was omitted in the original submission. 

Additional Information 

The application must state accurately and fully the facts the OCC needs to determine that the establishment of a proposed office is consistent with law and applicable regulations. The OCC may require additional data at any time during the processing of the application. When additional information is requested, the decision generally will be delayed. If an inadequate response is received to requests for additional information, the application may be considered withdrawn or disapproved.

Publication Requirements 

As required by 12 CFR 5.8 for domestic branches and seasonal agencies, notice of the application must be published in a newspaper of general circulation in the community in which the applicant proposes to establish the branch. The notices must state that an application is being filed to establish a branch or seasonal agency and include the name of the bank and the exact location of the proposed branch. 

As required in 12 CFR 5.31 for CBCT branches, notice of the application must be published in a newspaper(s) of general circulation in the community(ies) in which the 63

Introduction 

་་ ་་་ applicant proposes to establish the CBCT branch(es). 

The notice must be published within five days after filing and state that written comments must be submitted by interested persons within 10 days after the publication date. The notice must also state the applicant's name, subject matter of the application, and the date on which the application was filed. Fees

A filing fee must accompany the application in accordance with 12 CFR 5.5 and the current Notice of the 

Comptroller of the Currency Fees. Filing fees are not refunded. 

State Law Considerations

Cites of state laws pertaining to branching and bank certification, and a statement that the proposal complies with state law including geographic limitations, and any applicable qualitative factors are required as part of the application. All state factors (geographic and qualitative) are considered in the decision process. 

Investment in Bank Premises Limitation

The addition of the office must not cause the bank to exceed the limits for investment in bank premises (12 

U.S.C. 371d and 12 CFR 7.3100). If the branch would increase the outstanding amount above the prescribed limit, the applicant should either request in the application, approval for an increase in the investment in bank premises or address the way it will correct the problem. 

Approval of the Deputy Comptroller (District) or the Deputy Comptroller for Multinational Banking for multinational companies and their affiliates must be obtained before the consummation of any plans under which the bank's investment in bank premises will be increased to an amount exceeding its capital stock. The 

Deputy Comptroller (District) or the Deputy Comptroller for Multinational Banking ordinarily will approve an investment in fixed assets aggregating an amount up to 50 percent of capital stock, surplus, and undivided profits if reasonable need is shown. 

Community Reinvestment Act (CRA) 

If the proposed office will change the bank's existing community delineation as designated in its CRA statement, the applicant's board of directors must act upon the change at its first regular meeting after the establishment of the branch. In the application, the bank must indicate the ways it intends to vary its lending policy, procedures, or services at the proposed branch, if at all. In deciding on an application for the establishment of a domestic branch or other facility with the ability to accept deposits, the OCC will consider the national bank's record of performance. 

For any market area analysis that may be required by state law, the bank's community as delineated in its 

CRA statement should be used.

Filing and Decision Process Pre-Filing 

1.

Applicability 

OCC approval is required when the resulting bank in a failure acquisition is a national bank or a bank located in the District of Columbia. There are many ways to structure failure acquisitions. However, the procedures in this section are applicable to only two typical types of failure transactions that may involve OCC regulated banks. They are: • Purchase of assets and assumption of liabilities of an insured failed institution by an existing national bank. • Establishment of a new national bank to be used as an acquisition vehicle to purchase the assets and assume the liabilities of an insured failed institution. 

Banks or other groups unfamiliar with this process should discuss their plans and any related questions with an OCC representative prior to submitting an application. This section does not apply to the creation and/or acquisition of a bridge bank pursuant to 12 U.S.C. 1821(i); the creation and/or acquisition of a deposit insurance national bank (DINB) pursuant to 12 U.S.C. 1821(h); or an emergency bank merger acquisition (see Mergers). Definitions

The following terms are associated with failure acquisitions.

Clean Purchase and Assumption Transaction-In this transaction, the receiver keeps all known problem assets and provides the purchaser with specific options for recourse to the receiver on certain assets within specified periods of time.

Deposit Insurance Transfer and Asset Purchase Agree ment (DITAPA)-This type of transaction involves the transfer of insured deposits (e.g., deposits, including interest, of $100,000 or less) and certain assets of a failed institution to the purchaser by the receiver. The procedures for a purchase of assets and assumption of liabilities apply to this type of transaction. The purchaser must honor the contract rate of deposits for a specified number of days, but can renegotiate the rate to reflect market conditions.

Failed Institution-A failed bank or savings association. 

OCC Representative-This person may be the District 

Director for Analysis, a Multinational Banking representative, a licensing analyst, a national bank examiner, or any other OCC employee designated to represent the 

OCC during failure acquisition transactions. 

Potential National Bank Bidder-A potential bidder is: (1) an existing national bank that has been preliminarily approved to bid on the acquisition of a failed institution; or (2) a group that has been preliminarily approved to establish and organize a new national bank to bid to acquire a failed institution.

Premium-A premium generally is paid by the assuming bank to the receiver to purchase the assets and assume the liabilities of a failed institution. Depending upon the type of transaction, a positive premium is paid by the acquiring bank (purchaser) to the receiver or a negative premium is paid by the receiver to the acquiring bank (purchaser). A negative premium may be paid by the receiver for "total asset purchase and assumption" transactions (see "total asset purchase and assumption transactions" below) and "small loan purchase and assumption transactions" (see "small loan purchase and assumption transactions").

Primary Bank or Thrift Regulator-The OCC is the primary regulator of nationally chartered banks, and statechartered banks are regulated by the appropriate state banking regulatory authority. In bank failure transactions, the OCC or the appropriate state banking regulatory authority notifies the FDIC that a bank may be in imminent danger of failing. The OCC or state authority later declares the bank insolvent and typically appoints the FDIC as receiver. In thrift failure transactions, the 

Office of Thrift Supervision (OTS) or the appropriate state thrift regulatory authority notifies the RTC or the FDIC that a thrift may be in imminent danger of failing. 

Purchase and Assumption Transactions (P&A)-Acqui sitions of failed institutions generally are structured by the receiver as purchase and assumption transactions.

A purchase and assumption transaction is governed by a P&A agreement between the purchaser and the receiver, which transfers certain assets and liabilities to the purchaser for a premium. If problem or classified assets are acquired, provisions in the executed agreements normally address resale to the receiver under certain specified conditions. In purchase and assumption transactions, all liabilities usually are assumed. If the failed institution is a state bank in a depositor preference state, only deposit liabilities would be assumed. The following types of purchase and assumption transactions are commonly used: clean P&A; deposit insurance transfer and asset purchase agreement (DITAPA); small loan P&A; and total asset P&A (see Definitions). Approval of a national bank's acquisition of a failed institution is required under 12 U.S.C. 

1828(c). There is no statutory requirement for shareholders of the acquiring bank to approve the transaction. 72

MERWADU TAW LIDIVWAT 

Receiver-The FDIC is typically appointed receiver for institutions that are insured by the Bank Insurance Fund 

(BIF). The Resolution Trust Corporation (RTC) is ap pointed receiver for institutions that were once FSLICinsured and that were placed in conservatorship or receivership between January 1, 1989 and August 9, 1992. The FDIC is also appointed receiver for thrifts that become insured by the Savings Association Insurance 

Fund (SAIF) after August 9, 1989. 

Small Loan Purchase and Assumption Transaction 

(SLAP) In this transaction, the purchaser is required by the receiver to purchase all loans, regardless of asset quality, below a specified dollar amount. The purchase of other assets is similar to a clean purchase and assumption transaction, wherein options with limited recourse are provided to the receiver. 

State-tiered Bidding Requirements-Certain states have laws governing eligible bidders that restrict the bidding process. Some state laws will permit submission of a bid from an out-of-state group only after all in-state bids have been rejected by the receiver. Opportunities for an in-state group to match out-of-state group bids are sometimes provided for in state statutes. 

In other cases, applicable state law may restrict bid submission to groups within the same county or contiguous counties, etc.

Total Asset Purchase and Assumption Transaction 

(TAPA) In this transaction, the purchaser acquires all assets, including problem and charged off assets, and normally assumes all liabilities, including contingent ones. If the failed institution is located in a depositor preference state, only deposit liabilities would be assumed. This transaction subjects the purchaser to considerably more risk than clean purchase and assumption transactions, because the purchaser acquires all assets, including classified and charged off loans. The purchaser does not have further recourse to the receiver for additional losses incurred. A "negative" premium is normally involved in this transaction, which is sometimes referred to as a "take all" or "whole" purchase and assumption transaction.

Summary of Process 

The FDIC is appointed receiver whenever a national bank fails and, in most cases, when a state bank fails. 

The RTC is typically appointed receiver when a thrift fails. In anticipation of a failure, the Comptroller of the 

Currency (in the case of nationally chartered banks) or the state banking regulatory authority (in the case of state-chartered banks and thrifts or the OTS (in the case of federally chartered thrifts)) advises the appropriate receiver that an institution is in imminent danger of failing. The receiver prepares to sell that institution after it is declared insolvent and the receiver is appointed.

FDIC Transactions 

In some cases, the receiver may choose to pay off the depositors and liquidate the remaining assets of the failed institution. Unsecured creditors, uninsured depositors, and the shareholders of the failed institution would share with the receiver in the net proceeds of the liquidation.

Failing institutions are not notified when the receiver has been advised by the Comptroller or the state banking regulatory authority to proceed with preliminary sale/liquidation procedures. Prior to the time and date that the declaration of insolvency has been pre-scheduled, ongoing efforts may be made to prevent the institution from failing. For example, a capital infusion could prevent an institution from being declared insolvent and all procedures in this section would be abandoned.

After being notified of a potential bank failure, the FDIC compiles a preliminary list of banks, bank holding companies, individuals, and chain banking groups that may be interested in acquiring the failed bank and that meet specific requirements established by the FDIC. This list is commonly referred to as the "bid list." 

The FDIC's criteria for compiling bid lists depend on the size and structure of the failing institution and state and national banking law restrictions on expansionary activities that may affect the acquisition of a failed institution. However, the following general criteria apply to most potential acquirers. • Banks and Bank Holding Companies Generally, banks and bank holding companies must have a record of safe, sound, and legal operations. Their management teams must be capable. The size of potential acquiring banks and bank holding companies relative to that of the failing institution is considered, as well as capital adequacy and antitrust issues. • Individuals and Chain Banking Groups-Only persons or chain banking groups (i.e., groups that control several banks albeit not through a holding company structure) who have extensive banking experience and known financial and managerial resources are included. Because the winning bidder must open a bank the next business day, persons and groups with nominal banking experience and/or those absent from banking for even a short time are scrutinized closely and may be found ineligible to 73

bid. In addition, groups composed of persons who have not worked together in the past may be subject to closer review even though they have current banking experience. Such close scrutiny of persons and groups assures the continuity of banking services to a failed institution's community. 

Potential bidding groups that meet the above criteria usually are included in the preliminary list prepared by the FDIC, whether or not they have expressed specific interest in acquiring the institution that is in imminent danger of failing. Some groups notify the FDIC of their interest in institutions in a designated geographical area, but others are included without requests for inclusion.

The FDIC circulates the preliminary bid list to the OCC, the Federal Reserve, and the state banking regulatory authority to obtain additional supervisory information about the prospective bidders. This process also enables the FDIC to discover those institutions or persons upon which the other regulators have placed conditions (e.g., management plan, capital plan) to bid or those they would not approve to bid. The FDIC retains the ultimate authority to include or exclude a potential bidder, although the FDIC would probably exclude a bidder, if all the bank regulatory agencies object to its inclusion on the list.

The FDIC schedules a bid information meeting once a bid list is completed. At least 24 hours prior to the scheduled pre-bid meeting, the FDIC invites potential bidders to attend. The OCC, the Federal Reserve, and the state banking regulatory authority are also invited to send representatives.

The receiver presides over the bid information meeting. 

Specific confidential information about the proposed transaction is communicated to the groups attending or represented. This includes, but is not limited to, information pertaining to the failing institution's: • Assets and liabilities. • Income and expenses. • "Banking" house and branching sites. • Contracts. • Litigation. • Legal documents that would be executed with the receiver by the winning bidder. • Minimum dollar amounts and percentage levels of capitalization. • Application procedures that would be required by each of the regulators.

The information neither includes the failing institution's examination reports, nor does an invitation to attend the meeting mean that the potential bidder will be allowed to submit a bid. It merely permits the potential bidder to obtain confidential information about the institution that is in imminent danger of failing and to prepare an acquisition proposal, if so desired. The evaluation and analysis of the proposal by the appropriate regulatory agencies, including the resulting institution's primary supervisor, will determine whether the bid may be submitted to the receiver. 

Each representative from the OCC, the Federal Reserve, and the state banking regulatory authority discusses the procedures to be followed to obtain his/her agency's approval(s) to consummate the transaction. 

Normally, the OCC representative discusses briefly requirements for a national bank to acquire the failed institution or for a new national bank to be established for that purpose.

For national banks, supervisory and/or other considerations may affect a specific national bank's interest in bidding on a failed institution. A national bank's ability to meet the general requirements and address supervisory and/or other concerns adequately will determine whether the OCC will approve the proposed acquisition. Failure to meet the general guidelines, or submission of a proposal that may have an adverse supervisory effect on the acquiring national bank, may lead the OCC to withhold approval of a potential national bank bidder's proposal. 

After the meeting is adjourned, national banks interested in bidding should talk to the OCC representative who attended the meeting to discuss short- and longrange effects of the transaction, current supervisory concerns, and other issues. Instructions are provided for filing the application(s) needed to acquire the failed institution. Potential national bank bidders can contact the OCC representative from after the close of the meeting to prior to the time and date designated for the submission of bids.

RTC Transactions 

Many thrifts are placed in conservatorship prior to a declaration of insolvency. The RTC is typically appointed conservator. This appointment is made to: preserve the franchise value for potential acquirers and existing shareholders; minimize costs to the insurance fund; and eliminate the potential for more losses by existing management. 74

By comparison to the FDIC process, the RTC advertizes in national publications for interested parties to attend a scheduled bid meeting when marketing thrifts in conservatorship. Although the attendees may not have been pre-cleared by the appropriate regulatory agency(ies), the bidding criteria are the same as those discussed above.

Failing SAIF-insured thrifts are also resolved through the RTC's Accelerated Resolution Program, a program that seeks to market failing thrifts prior to placement in conservatorship. Invitations to bid meetings, when the thrift has not been placed in conservatorship, follow the 

FDIC's invitation process.

Interim Processing for National Bank Bidders 

Potential bidders should be prepared to discuss their proposals thoroughly. A written application must be submitted for all transactions. However, District Deputy 

Comptrollers or their designees may waive submission of certain portions. A thorough written proposal is re quired for TAPA or SLAP transactions. 

Questions about the proposed transaction should be resolved prior to the time set by the receiver for the submission of bids. Failure to do so may disqualify a potential national bank bidder's bid. The receiver will announce the specific date for the submission of bids any time after the bid information meeting.

Prior to that date, potential national bank bidders must send the OCC draft applications with appropriate attachments. The drafts will be reviewed for accuracy, completeness, and adherence to key policies and general requirements. The OCC will notify the RTC that all the appropriate documentation has been received from national bank bidders for failed thrift institutions, but will analyze only proposals identified later by the RTC.

After a proposal for an FDIC transaction is complete, or a proposal has been identified by the RTC as a likely winner, the OCC will analyze it promptly and notify the applicant and the receiver informally of its decision on the various corporate elements. 

If approval is granted, the potential national bank bidder will be notified informally that it is eligible. An ineligible national bank bidder will also be notified that it may not bid. Final OCC certification of the purchase and assumption is granted after a national bank bidder is notified that it is the winning bidder subject to declaration of insolvency and court approval of the purchase and assumption transaction.

Prior to accepting a bid, the receiver will ask the appropriate regulatory agency(ies) for any objections to each proposal. If the OCC has preliminarily approved a proposal, the receiver will be informed that the OCC will not object to the bidder's submission of a bid and will grant the necessary final certifications to consummate the transaction if the bidder wins. If the OCC has decided not to grant the necessary approval(s), the group will not be permitted to participate in the bidding process as a national bank bidder. 

Bids must satisfy pertinent legal requirements including, restrictions on entrance to a state by out-of-state bank holding companies, or state-tiered bidding requirements. Bids must also satisfy national banking laws applicable to acquisitions of failed institutions. In some communities that have a small number of banks, bids from local groups may be considered only after all others have been rejected because of potential antitrust concerns.

The Comptroller of the Currency is a member of both the FDIC and RTC board of directors. The appropriate board reviews bids submitted and declares the winner. 

If the bids are unacceptable, the receiver may negotiate with the bidders. National bank bidders, to maintain maximum flexibility, may disclose a bid range in the proposals submitted to the OCC. The receiver may also consider other bids, including those that were restricted because of state-tiered bidding requirements or antitrust concerns. After the receiver's board has made its final decision, the winning bidder's name is communicated to the bidders and regulators. The amount of the winning bid is announced after the institution is declared insolvent. All bids submitted are available through the receiver in accordance with the 

Freedom of Information Act (FOIA). 

If the winning bidder is a new or an established national bank, the OCC approves formally the bank's application, subject to declaration of insolvency and court approval of the purchase and assumption transaction.

At that time, the winning bidder also executes legal documents with the receiver. 

Court approval of the agreement executed between the winning bidder and the receiver is required by statute. 

Liquidation attorneys employed by the receiver typically meet with a federal district court judge prior to the declaration of insolvency to brief him/her and present any unusual issues. After the institution has been declared insolvent and the proposed acquisition transaction has been approved by the receiver's board and the appropriate regulatory agency(ies), the liquidation attorneys again meet with the judge. The judge approves the proposed transaction that transfers ownership 

MARANGUT RAW ITUMAUKU 

75

and/or control of certain assets and liabilities from the receiver of the failed institution to another corporate entity. The declaration of insolvency is not subject to federal district court review. 

Consummation of most failure acquisition transactions occur within a few hours of the declaration that an institution is insolvent. The purchasing group must accept the assets and liabilities it has purchased and assumed from the receiver and prepare to transact business with that institution's customers the next business day.

TAPA and SLAP Transactions Decisions relating to TAPA and SLAP transactions will be based upon the quality of the application and analysis provided by the potential national bank bidder, and not solely on the size of the bid. A thorough analysis of the due diligence review, including assessment of risks, will reflect positively on the national bank's ability to manage successfully its acquisition of the failed institution. Conversely, a poor analysis will reflect adversely on the national bank and one that is poorly prepared or incomplete may result in disallowing the submission of a bid. Due to the higher degree of risks associated with these transactions, proposals should normally discuss, at a minimum: • The size of the failing institution relative to that of the national bank.

Management depth, size, and expertise (especially work-out personnel). • Staff depth and size. • Assessment of risks (credit, interest, liquidity, funds management, overhead and other costs, and tax considerations). 

Capital plan. 

• Affect of the proposed acquisition on the national bank.

Analysis of the potential national bank bidder's "due diligence" procedures. • Disclosure of the bid based upon the "due diligence" analysis performed. 

Assessment of risks and accounting treatment will be reviewed carefully at subsequent OCC examinations. 

Therefore, a winning national bank bidder should retain a copy of its bid proposal and supporting documentation. 

Key Policies 

The OCC appoints the FDIC as receiver of any closed national, District of Columbia, or insured federal branch of a foreign bank. OCC approval is required whenever an existing national bank or a group organizing a national bank seeks to acquire a failed institution. 

In reviewing a proposed transaction, the OCC considers: • Protecting the interest of the depositors.

Minimizing any negative impact on the affected community. 

Promoting the safety and soundness of the banking system.

Determining the acquiring bank's prospects for success following the transaction.

The OCC also weighs the effects on competition in the markets affected by the proposed acquisition. The 

OCC may approve a proposed acquisition if it determines that the previous concerns outweigh any adverse competitive effects of a proposed acquisition. 

The OCC judges an existing national bank's performance in meeting the credit needs of its community, including the low- and moderate-income neighborhoods. That record may render an existing national bank's proposal to acquire a failed institution unacceptable, which would preclude submission of a bid.

In addition, the OCC weighs banking factors and normally will not approve a failure acquisition that will result in a bank with inadequate capital, unsatisfactory management, or poor earnings prospects.

If the OCC must approve an acquisition immediately (e.g., overnight) to resolve an actual or imminent failure of an insured institution, the acquiring bank is neither required to publish a notice of the proposed transaction, nor does the OCC request reports on the competitive factors involved from the Attorney General, the 

FDIC, and the Federal Reserve.

Standards of Review 

Acquisition proposals from experienced organizing groups, or existing national banks in an area where the local economy is strong and competition weak will be reviewed only to determine that they present a reasonable strategy for success. If the due diligence and market analyses are reasonable and the strategy is clear and within the reach of a regular banking business, no further analysis normally is needed. If unprecedented or unusual banking services or corporate arrangements are proposed, additional information and a more intensive review may be required. 

Proposals sponsored by bank holding companies or persons affiliated with other banking organizations will 76

be evaluated on the performance record of the bank holding company and/or affiliated institution(s). The 

OCC may review FR Y-6 reports, SEC 10K reports,

Annual Reports to Stockholders, reports of examination, financial statements, and any other information available to it in its supervisory capacity. In addition, a bank holding company's overall philosophy and plans, for example, strategy, capital, management, and profitability, may be reviewed for consistency and compatibility with the proposal.

The strength of a parent company, combined with the direct support it offers, can mitigate concerns over capital, operating plan, or other supervisory concerns. 

Conversely, the poor condition of a parent company and/or its affiliates or the absence of any evidence of the parent's support of its subsidiaries may result in negative action on the proposal even though it may have merit. The review and analysis of holding company sponsored proposals will not be confined to the proposed acquisition alone. The extent to which the condition of the bank will be affected by the overall condition of the consolidated organization will be considered. Serious problems at an affiliate might require management's full attention to assure their resolution prior to further expansion. 

Accordingly, the OCC may preclude submission of a bid by a holding company affiliate if the condition of the parent company and/or any affiliates is of substantive concern.

When the proposed bank is affiliated with institutions that are subject to special supervisory concern, the proposal may be rejected or approved preliminarily subject to conditions deemed appropriate by the OCC. 

Conversely, when the bank holding company or affiliated institutions serve as substantial sources of strength, proposals may be approved preliminarily, even in markets where economic conditions are weak and/or competitive conditions intense.

New National Banks-Special Considerations Organizing Groups and Management 

Groups seeking establishment of a new national bank as an acquisition vehicle must be comprised of five or more persons.

The group must identify a proposed CEO before filing the draft applications to establish a new national bank and purchase certain assets and assume certain liabilities of the failed institution. Management is especially critical to the success of a new bank established under these circumstances. Organizers must investigate thoroughly the background and qualifications of the candidate prior to submission to the OCC and involve him/her in the decision to bid on the failed institution. 

Selection of a CEO whom the OCC finds unqualified for the position, whose prior banking experience is unsatisfactory, or who is otherwise unacceptable could reflect negatively on the organizers, result in disapproval of the proposal, and preclude submission of a bid.

Information on the proposed CEO will be treated in confidence, if requested, until the failure transaction is consummated. If a bid is unsuccessful, all data previously submitted to the OCC will be returned. 

To insure the equal treatment of all CEO candidates, the 

OCC has established the following general guidelines. 

Under those rules, the potential CEO: • Must possess skills that complement those of the directors and fit the needs of the new national bank. • Must be thoroughly familiar with the proposed new bank's operating plan. • Should have managed a bank or similar financial institution successfully or have successful experience as an officer in an area relevant to the proposed bank's marketing strategy and needs. • Should have executive experience in operations or administration.

These guidelines are consistent with the OCC's requirement that a well-rounded person be chosen to lead a new national bank. Decisions regarding a proposed 

CEO are based on a person's suitability for a particular position with a new bank chartered under these circumstances and are not intended to determine his/her eligibility for other jobs in existing banking institutions. 

Other executive officers with the experience, competence, and willingness to manage the bank as required by the operating plan must be identified in the draft application. If the operating plan contains proposals for specialized types of services, executive officers should have experience relevant to their development and administration. The Depository Management Interlocks 

Act (Act) 12 U.S.C. 3201, prohibits management interlocks between nonaffiliated depository organizations. 

The regulation (12 CFR 26) allows the OCC to permit an otherwise prohibited interlock under limited circumstances. An interlock relationship may be allowed if one of the organizations involved is a newly-chartered bank and the relationship is deemed "necessary to provide management or operating expertise to [the] organization." Specifically required information on the interlocking relationship should be submitted by the organizing group. 77

The OCC reviews and must have no objections to all directors and executive officers appointed during the first two years a new national bank is open for business. 

Community Service 

The organizing group must know its community and have planned to serve its needs. The operating plan must reflect the group's plans to achieve the bank's affirmative obligation to help meet the credit needs of its entire community, including low- and moderate-income neighborhoods. The Community Reinvestment 

Act (CRA) applies to all new bank charters, except banks engaged solely in correspondent banking business, trust company business, or acting as a clearing agent. These limited purpose banks are specifically exempt under 12 CFR 25.101.

Transaction Discussion 

The organizing group's transaction discussion must reflect that the proposed bank will: • Be able to open immediately after the failed institution has been declared insolvent with no disruption in services. • Identify competent officers and key employees, especially those needed to work out asset and/or liability problems inherent in the transaction. • Realistically project reasonable earning prospects and adequate capital. • Offer services responsive to community needs and stabilize a potentially volatile situation. • Be operated in a safe and sound manner in compliance with applicable laws, policies, and procedures. 

The organizers and the proposed CEO should be familiar with the operating plan and their part in its successful implementation.

Capital 

The organizing group must plan to raise capital sufficient to support the risk-weighted balance sheet acquired from the receiver through the purchase and assumption agreement and the projected volume and type of business planned. Considerations for determining the adequacy of capital include: • Organizing expenses. • Earning prospects. • Economic and competitive conditions in the community to be served. • The experience and competence of management. • The risk inherent in the expected asset and liability mix. • The amount of fixed asset investment. • The dependability of plans to raise, or the ability of the directors to supply, additional capital when needed. Initial tangible Tier 1 capital, after organizing expenses are capitalized or charged to the bank's capital, must be adequate to successfully implement the proposed operating plan and effectively compete in the failed institution's market area. 

Organizers' must justify to the OCC their proposed capital level. The OCC may determine that higher or lower amounts of capital from that proposed are necessary based on local market conditions or the operating plan of the organizing group. 

Generally the OCC does not look favorably on new bank proposals that rely on debt or other instruments that require repayment of dividends during a new bank's initial years.

Generally Applicable Procedures 

Directorate

For organizing national banks, five persons who meet the qualifications of directors as specified by 12 U.S.C. 

72 must be selected. The proposed president must be a member of the board of directors. Unless specifically waived by the OCC, in total or in part, each proposed director and interim director must complete and file with the OCC a confidential biographical and financial report.

National banks must decide whether or not to elect directors of the closed bank to serve on their boards of directors. Nonetheless, the purchasing bank is strongly encouraged not to elect former directors of a failed institution to serve as directors of their boards prior to conducting a thorough investigation. These investigations will be subject to OCC review. 

Management and Staffing

National banks must assure the OCC that appropriate management and staff will be available for the failed institution's reopening. Evidence to support those assurances may be required. 

Unless specifically waived by the OCC, in total or in part, each executive officer of a new national bank must complete and file with the OCC a confidential biographical and financial report. In addition, background checks will be required for executive officers unless waived by the DDA/DCMNB. 78

FURWANITAW LIONMENT 

National banks must decide whether or not to retain officers and staff of the closed institution. Nonetheless, the purchasing bank is strongly encouraged not to place former officers and employees of the failed institution in decision making positions prior to conducting a thorough investigation.

Capital 

Capital requirements under 12 U.S.C. 36 provide that the aggregate capital of every national bank and its branches shall equal the capital and surplus required of state-chartered institutions for the establishment of branches.

In addition, national banks are subject to other statutory and regulatory minimum capital requirements. The minimum capital required pursuant to 12 U.S.C. 51 is determined by the population of the cities or other places where the bank has offices. A bank must have at least $50,000 in capital stock for each city in which it has an office(s) and the population does not exceed 6,000; at least $100,000 in capital stock for each city in which it has an office(s) and the population is between 6,001 and 50,000; and at least $200,000 in capital stock for each city in which it has an office(s) and the population is greater than 50,000. In addition, the bank must have surplus at least equal to 20 percent of capital stock.

For organizing new national banks, the absolute minimum Tier 1, Tier 2, and leverage capital ratios must be met. Notwithstanding those minimum ratios, higher capital levels may be required for newly chartered banks when the OCC believes that the bank's capital is or may become inadequate. In establishing appropriate capital levels, consideration will be given to the size of the bank, quality of assets purchased, experience and competence of proposed management, earnings prospects, competitive factors, adequacy of operating plan, and other features of the proposed transaction.

The resulting tangible capital ratios for existing national banks generally must at least equal the minimum levels set forth in 12 CFR 3. If the resultant ratios are less than those required, the OCC may still be willing to approve the proposal, provided that a reasonable and detailed capital plan in the form of a formal commitment is submitted prior to the time established for submission of bids. Such plan should illustrate how the capital ratios will be improved within a reasonable time frame (i.e., runoff of volatile deposits within six months, earnings retention within one year, capital injection within one year if the ratio remains below the minimum level or a level previously agreed by the bank and the OCC).

The bid must be disclosed to the OCC so that it can assess accurately capital adequacy for the resultant national bank. Any premium paid is excluded from capital for legal and capital adequacy purposes. In 

TAPA transactions, the amount of the discount applied to the purchase of the failed institution's assets does not qualify as capital.

If the potential national bank bidder currently operates under an administrative document with specific capital or management requirements, compliance at the time of the purchase is required unless the OCC grants an exemption. Those banks may be allowed to bid, if the proposed transaction will not adversely affect national banks subject to special supervision. The decision to permit such banks to bid is generally made by the 

District Deputy Comptroller (DCD) or the Deputy Comptroller , or his/her designee, as appropriate. 

Fiduciary Powers

When a failed national or state bank or thrift with trust powers is acquired by a national bank that lacks trust powers, a separate fiduciary powers letter of intent and a filing fee are required. Unless waived by the OCC, the letter of intent should include: • A request for full or limited powers. • An opinion of bank counsel that the bank's proposed fiduciary activities violate state or local law. • A certification that the capital and surplus of the bank is not less than that required by state law for state banks, trust companies, and other corporations that exercise fiduciary powers. • Biographical information on the proposed trust management, including educational and professional credentials and a five-year employment history.

A trust permit will be forwarded to a winning national bank bidder after the transaction consummates. 

Head Office Designation 

The applicant may designate any one of the existing head offices of the merging institutions (i.e., the acquiring or the failed institution) as the new head office of the resulting bank and the other as a branch office. No additional branch or relocation filings or fees are required when the designation appears in the merger application. State laws regarding branches may be applicable. 79

Branch Authorization 

OCC will issue branch authorization letters upon consummation of the merger. The authorizations will cover all present head offices, except the newly designated head office, of the merging institution and all existing facilities (brick and mortar branches and CBCTs) of merging institutions that are not national banks. Operating branches of national banks will be carried over automatically to the resulting institution. Approved, but unopened branches, also must be authorized according to OCC policy (12 CFR 5.30 and 5.31). To receive a branch number, the bank must notify the OCC 10 days before opening any such branch. Approval will expire, if the branch is not opened with 18 months (nine months for a CBCT) after the date of its original preliminary approval regardless of the consummation date of this transaction. Separate filing fees are not required.

The applicant should list the requirements of applicable state and federal branching laws in the application and should certify that the resulting branch structure complies with those laws. Certification of the bank's compliance with state law must include consideration of geographic limitations and any qualitative factors. For any market analysis that may be required by state law, the bank should use its community as delineated in its 

CRA statement.

Operating Subsidiary Authorization 

If a failed national bank owned operating subsidiaries that were purchased by the acquiring bank, the OCC's approval letter will authorize their continued operation. 

If a failed state bank or a failed thrift owned operating subsidiaries that were purchased by the acquiring national bank, the approval letter will authorize their continued operation subject to notification within 90 days of their names, addresses, and activities. Such notification should be accompanied by a legal opinion regarding the authority of a national bank to conduct such activities. In addition, if deemed appropriate, the OCC may require the national bank to file a notice as set forth in 12 CFR 5.34 to continue to conduct those activities.

The establishment of new subsidiaries or the expansion of existing subsidiaries' activities subsequent to the failure acquisition transaction are subject to the normal filing requirements of 12 CFR 5.34. 

Investment in Bank Premises Limits

If the proposed transaction involves the acquisition of additional bank premises and facilities that result in an excess investment in premises (12 U.S.C. 371d), approval of a request for that investment may also be required prior to the transaction or the bank must address the way it will achieve compliance (12 CFR 

7.3100). The DDC or the DCMNB will ordinarily approve an investment in fixed assets amounting to 50 percent of capital stock, surplus, and undivided profits when a reasonable need for such investment can be shown. 

Evidence of Funding Availability 

If additional capital will be injected into the existing national bank prior to the consummation of the transaction or if it is being injected for an organizing national bank, written documentation that financing is unconditionally available is required (e.g., loan commitment or deposit verification). In lieu of providing written documentation, the bank may arrange for the OCC to verify funding availability directly with the lender.

Fidelity Insurance 

To comply with 12 CFR 7.5215, the national bank must ensure through binder, letter, or verbal confirmation that fidelity (blanket bond) insurance is effective on the day the failed institution is reopened. Fees

A filing fee must accompany the final application submitted by a winning national bank bidder. Filing fees are published annually in "The Notice of Comptroller of the 

Currency Fees." Filing fees are not refundable.

Additional Information 

The application must accurately and fully state the facts the OCC needs to determine that a purchase and assumption is consistent with law and applicable regulations. The OCC may require additional information at any time during the processing of the application. It may ask applicants to provide additional detailed information about their financial condition; capabilities to operate in a safe and sound manner; management; ability to maintain adequate capital; earnings prospects; or responsiveness to community needs. The request that identifies concerns and indicates information to be submitted may be made by letter. If the OCC receives an inadequate response to its request for additional information, the application may be considered withdrawn or denied. The short processing times for failure acquisitions normally prevents examinations and field investigations. 

Other Legal Considerations 

When the failed institution is not a national bank, the application should include copies and cites of federal or state laws applicable to the purchase and assumption and an opinion from counsel that the proposal does not violate those laws. Counsel should indicate that any action required by the applicable federal or state law 80

HARWARILLAW LIDNAN 

has been or will be been taken before consummation of the proposal. Confidentiality

Confidential data should be submitted separately and designated as such in the application. The OCC will decide whether to give that material confidential treatment according to the Freedom of Information Act (5 

U.S.C. 552).

Background Investigations 

New national bank organizing groups and their CEOs must have the experience, competence, and willingness to direct the bank's affairs in a safe, sound, and legal manner. Organizing groups that do not meet these criteria are denied permission to organize a bank. 

Organizers and CEOS whose previous banking experience is tied to failed or problem financial institutions will be scrutinized closely by the OCC to determine their ability to carry out their duties safely and adequately. 

The OCC will conduct background checks on new national bank candidates for the positions of CEO, president, and other executive officers, and on all those organizers, principal shareholders, and directors. In addition, candidates for positions at banks that have been open for less than two years are subject to these background checks. The OCC's investigation will scrutinize the competence, experience, integrity, and financial ability of those persons. The OCC will determine independently the accuracy and completeness of any information submitted in conjunction with those persons and must have no objection to their serving in their proposed capacities. 

All information filed in connection with any application is subject to review and verification. Any material misrepresentation or omission of fact in an application will generally result in its disapproval. If a misrepresentation or omission is discovered after the bank has commenced business, the charter may be revoked and the bank closed. Additionally, such a finding may subject the organizing group to severe criminal penalties, including those provided in 18 U.S.C. 1001.

The OCC routinely performs Federal Bureau of Investigation (FBI) and other background checks on persons seeking entry into the national banking system. The 

OCC performs standard background checks on all such persons through the: • FBI. • United States Custom Service. 

• Drug Enforcement Administration. 

• LEXIS-NEXIS and Enforcement and Compliance In formation System (ECIS) records search. • Other regulatory agencies' records when the acquiring parties have been with institutions under another agency's jurisdiction.

The OCC may not conduct some of the standard background checks, if the performance of the check would be redundant, would not serve the OCC's purpose for background investigation, or for other reasons. However, the OCC will pursue any additional background checks believed appropriate. Checks may include, but are not limited to, requests for background information from the Internal Revenue Service (IRS), Securities Exchange Commission (SEC), Commodity Futures Trading Commission (CFTC), National Association of Securities Dealers (NASD), INTERPOL, Central Intelli gence Agency (CIA), U.S. Immigrations and Naturalization, World Bank, International Monetary Fund (IMF),

U.S. State Department, and state and local law enforcement agencies. The OCC may require fingerprints from an acquiring party to access information needed to complete a background investigation. Any information received by the OCC from its background investigation may be shared with the other federal financial institutions' regulators. The OCC may request additional information needed to determine independently the accuracy and completeness of any statements in the application. 

Merger Accounting

Mergers and acquisitions of banks are accounted for under either the "pooling of interests" or "purchase" method of accounting. The pooling method is used generally for combinations accomplished by an exchange of common stock for common stock. This method accounts for combining entities by adding together the existing historical-based amounts. Generally, the only accounting adjustment is that required by the issuance of stock by the surviving entity. 

Purchase accounting results when cash, debt and/or stock is exchanged. It records an acquisition on the books of the acquirer by allocating the purchase price to the assets acquired and liabilities assumed, based on their fair values. The capital section of the acquired bank is eliminated and entries are made to record the cash, debt, or stock issued. Any excess of the cost of the acquisition over the net fair value (i.e, premium paid) of the identifiable assets and liabilities acquired or assumed is recorded as goodwill. (Goodwill is not part of regulatory capital.) The new basis of accounting is recorded on the acquirer's (new parent holding company) books. 81

Push-down accounting also establishes this new basis of accounting on the books of the acquired or subsidiary bank. For transactions occurring after September 

30, 1989, push-down accounting is required when 95 percent or more of the voting stock of the bank is acquired. Push-down accounting may be used when more than 80 percent, but less than 95 percent, is acquired. In push-down accounting, the bank must include all of the purchase acquisition adjustments on its books. As a result, the acquired bank's assets and liabilities are recorded at their fair value. In any other circumstance, the purchase adjustments should be presented only on the financial statements of the parent holding company. 

Under purchase accounting, marketable securities are recorded based on quoted market values. Certain other assets, such as purchased bank premises and OREO, are accounted for based on appraisals. Liabilities, principally deposits, are generally recorded at historical cost.

The loan portfolio is also accounted for at fair value. 

Determination of fair value is generally made on a loan-by-loan basis and should consider both interest rate and credit risk. The discount resulting from this analysis is accounted for on an individual loan basis. If a loan is on accrual status, the discount should be accreted to income to earn a level yield over the remaining life of the loan. Accretion of discount may be appropriate in some circumstances where the loan is on a nonaccural status. (Refer to OCC Examining Circular 

246 for a discussion of accretion of loans on a nonaccural status.) Subsequent impairment of value to a loan should be recognized through a charge to current operations.

An exception to the requirement for a loan-by-loan analysis is made for groups of similar consumer loans. 

The fair value of those loans may be determined on an aggregate basis. However, the discount resulting from this analysis should be applied to all the loans in the pool on a pro rata basis so that each can be accounted for separately.

An appropriate allowance for loan losses of the acquired bank may be included as a purchase adjustment. However, the allowance should not exceed the amount on the target bank's books immediately prior to the acquisition. This allowance must also be segregated and used only for losses on loans of the acquired bank. For regulatory assisted transactions, it is not part of regulatory capital.

Any unidentifiable intangible asset of the acquired bank should be recorded as such and amortized in accordance with the procedures set forth in Section 220 of the Comptroller's Handbook for National Bank Examin ers. Section 220 requires that banks record all purchased identifiable and unidentifiable intangibles as an asset in accordance with generally accepted accounting principles. Intangible assets should be amortized over their useful life, not to exceed 15 years, except for the core deposit intangible, which can be amortized over a period not to exceed 10 years. 

An accelerated method of amortization should be used for identifiable intangible assets in which the determination of the asset's value is based upon declining earning amounts. This would require that larger amounts be amortized in the earlier years of the asset's estimated life. Examples of assets requiring an accelerated method of amortization would be core deposit intangibles and purchased mortgage servicing rights. 

The straight line method of amortization may be used for all other intangible assets, including goodwill. 

Negative Premium Acquisitions 

Cash received from the FDIC or the RTC as a negative premium is considered as one of the assets acquired in the acquisition and should be accounted for accordingly. It is not a capital injection and should not be included as capital. 

In certain negative premium situations, the fair value of the assets acquired, including cash payments received from the FDIC or the RTC, may exceed the liabilities assumed, creating negative goodwill. Generally accepted accounting principles require that the non-current assets, other than investments in marketable securities, be reduced proportionately by the negative goodwill. Non-current assets would include bank premises. Any remaining negative goodwill is recorded as a liability and is not included as capital. 

The negative premium proposal may include amounts designated as payments for core deposit intangibles or future tax benefits. For accounting purposes, those amounts should be considered as an element of the purchase price (goodwill or a reduction of negative goodwill). A core deposit intangible may be recorded only on the basis of a formal economic study to determine its value. Additionally, the core deposit intangible cannot exceed the actual premium paid on an acquisition. Therefore, in negative premium situations, core deposit intangibles would generally not be recorded. 

Reorganizations A merger, consolidation, or substantial purchase and assumption between two or more affiliated institutions is accounted for at historical cost in a manner similar to pooling of interests accounting previously described. 82

Prior to September 30, 1989, OCC policy did not apply push-down purchase accounting to bank acquisitions, unless the bank was acquired through a bank merger. 

Accordingly, push-down accounting was not applied to a number of banks acquired prior to September 30, 1989. When one of those banks is merged into an affiliated bank, the remaining purchase accounting adjustments for the target institution must be pushed down to the bank at the time of the merger. Accordingly, the acquired bank's assets and liabilities would be recorded at their fair value at the time of their original acquisition, adjusted for subsequent dispositions and amortization. 

A purchase and assumption transaction between affiliated institutions that transfers less than 90 percent of the assets of the selling institution should be accounted for at fair value rather than at historical cost.

FDIC or RTC (Receiver)

(Regulators) Receiver

Regulatory Planning 

1. Advises FDIC or RTC (receiver) that an institution is in imminent danger of failing.

NOTE: Banks typically are not placed in conservatorship as part of the failure resolution process. Many thrifts, however, are placed in conservatorship with the RTC. Regardless of whether a thrift has been placed in conservatorship, these procedures generally apply. 

2. Prepares "bid list" and circulates it to appropriate financial institution regulators (i.e., OCC, Federal Reserve, FDIC, RTC, Office of Thrift 

Supervision (OTS), and state regulatory authorities). 

NOTE: The RTC may place advertisements/invitations in major financial publications (e.g., The Wall Street Journal), when attempting to market thrifts in conservatorship. Bid lists are not prepared under those circumstances. 

3. Review bid list and provide the receiver with additional supervisory information about prospective bidders. Advise the receiver about institutions or individuals that would not be approved to bid or who could bid subject to certain conditions (e.g., management plan, capital plan). 

4. Invites regulators and potential bidders to the bid information meeting after considering material from the regulators. 

Bid Information Meeting 

5. Presides over the bid information meeting. Communicates confidential information about the transaction to groups attending or represented at the meeting. Information includes, but is not limited to: • Assets and liabilities. •Income and expenses. • "Banking" house and branching sites. • Contracts. Litigation. • Legal documents that would be executed with the receiver by the winning bidder. • Minimum dollar amounts and percentage levels of capitalization. • Application procedures that would be required by each of the regulators.

NOTE: Information provided does not include the failing institution's examination reports. 

NOTE: An invitation to attend a bid information meeting does not necessarily mean that the potential bidder will be allowed to submit a bid. It merely permits the potential bidder to obtain confidential information about the institution that is in imminent danger of failing and to prepare an acquisition proposal, if so desired. The evaluation and analysis of the proposal by the appropriate regulatory agencies, includ- 86

Failure Acquisitions Procedures HDKUBKITTAAVI I IDAH OCC Representative Potential Bidder OCC Representative Potential Bidder ing the resulting institution's primary supervisor, will determine whether the bid may be submitted to the receiver. 

6. Completes various checklists during the meeting that highlight the primary factors to be considered in reviewing proposals from potential bidders and deciding applications submitted (see Documents-OCC, Bid Meeting Checklist, Bid Meeting Supplemental Checklist, and Thrift Transaction Highlights (Failure Acquisitions)). 

7. Briefly discusses the necessary requirements for an existing national bank to acquire the failed institution or for a new national bank to be established to acquire the failed institution. 

NOTE: A national bank's ability to meet the general requirements and address supervisory and/or other concerns adequately will determine whether the OCC will approve the proposed acquisition. Failure to meet the general guidelines or submission of a proposal that may have an adverse supervisory effect on the acquiring national bank may lead the OCC to withhold approval of a potential national bank bidder's proposal. 

8. Meets with the OCC representative at the bid meeting to express potential interest in acquiring the failed institution. 

9. Provides a copy of pertinent application procedures. 

10. Contacts the appropriate OCC office to express potential interest in bidding on a specific failed institution. Submits proposal in the form of a draft application. 

NOTE: Specific application requirements may vary among OCC districts and Multinational Banking (MNB) depending upon regional differences in the receiver's procedures and the failed institution's market area.

Fiduciary Powers

Introduction Summary of Process 

A bank that wishes to exercise fiduciary powers submits a letter of intent and the appropriate filing fee to the Deputy Comptroller for its district or to the Deputy Comptroller for Multinational Banking, if it is a multinational bank, or a multinational bank affiliate. Approval to exercise fiduciary powers is granted automatically 

30 days after the OCC receives a letter of intent, unless the bank is informed otherwise. The OCC sends a confirmation letter to the bank within seven days after an affirmative decision is made to allow it to exercise fiduciary powers.

This section also addresses the revocation of trust powers by the OCC and surrendering of trust powers by a national bank. Background

Previous OCC regulations required banks to submit extensive applications to exercise fiduciary powers.

Because the decision to exercise such powers is primarily a management decision, the OCC changed its requirement in 1984 from an application to a letter of intent. 

Key Policies Approval

Federal regulation requires national banks to seek prior OCC approval before offering fiduciary services to the public. Prior OCC approval also is required when a state bank possessing trust powers converts to a national bank or a state bank having trust powers merges into a national bank not previously authorized to exer cise trust powers. (NOTE: When a merging bank does not have any fiduciary powers to transfer to a receiving bank, because its trust accounts were serviced by another bank, the receiving bank cannot become a trustee by automatic succession on these accounts.)

The OCC will grant permission to exercise fiduciary powers to banks operating in a satisfactory manner, if: • The proposed activities comply with applicable statutes and regulations, including state, federal, and local laws.

The bank provides for and retains qualified trust management.

A request may be conditionally approved or denied, if the bank does not meet those conditions, or delayed if further information is required. Decision

A decision on the request will be made on or before the thirtieth day after the letter is received by the OCC or the period will be extended. If the applicant has not received notification by the thirtieth day, the bank may consider the request approved and begin exercising fiduciary powers. The bank will be informed in writing of the basis for a decision other than approval. 

Commencement of Business 

The bank must begin its trust activities within 18 months from the date of approval. The OCC also must approve any changes made by the bank in its proposed management before it commences trust business. 

Surrendering of Trust Powers Permit 

A bank that wishes to surrender voluntarily its right to exercise fiduciary powers must file with the OCC a certified copy of a resolution of its board of directors that signifies its desire to surrender such rights. After the OCC is assured that the bank has relieved itself of all fiduciary duties in accordance with state law, it will issue to the bank a notice certifying that the bank is no longer authorized to exercise the fiduciary powers granted. Pursuant to 12

U.S.C. 92a, the OCC may issue and serve a national bank with a notice of intent to revoke the authority of the association to exercise the fiduciary powers granted. 

When such rights are revoked, the bank must surrender the trust powers permit issued by the OCC.
