Mergers

Introduction .. 02 52 1A BALD TRU Applicability 

Generally, the Office of the Comptroller of the Currency (OCC) must approve a merger transaction that results in a national bank. The term "merger," as used in this section, is a generic term for a merger, consolidation, or purchase of assets and assumption of liabilities (P&A). The applicant determines the structure of the transaction based on business or tax considerations. 

Mergers may occur between banks and between banks and other depository institutions such as savings associations. Mergers may occur between unaffiliated operating institutions (real mergers), between affiliated operating institutions (affiliated mergers), and between an interim institution and an operating institution (interim mergers).

Definitions 

Depository Institution-A bank or savings association. 

Merger-A merger, under 12 U.S.C. 215a and 1828(c), generally refers to the merging of one or more depository institutions into an existing national bank or interim national bank. The shareholders of the target institution surrender their equity in return for equity in the acquiring bank or another form of consideration. Shareholders of the merging depository institutions must approve the transaction under 12 U.S.C. 215a, which provides dissenter's rights only for shareholders of the target institution. Shareholders of the acquiring national bank do not have dissenter's rights. 

Consolidation-A consolidation, under 12 U.S.C. 215 and 1828(c), refers to the combination of existing depository institutions under a national bank charter when at least one institution is a national bank. Shareholders of the consolidating institutions surrender their equity in return for equity in the consolidated bank or another form of consideration. Shareholders of the consolidating depository institutions must approve the transaction under 12 U.S.C. 215, which provides dissenter's rights to shareholders of all consolidating institutions. 

Purchase of Assets and Assumption of Liabilities-Pur chase of assets and assumption of liabilities, under 12

U.S.C. 1828(c), refers to a national bank's acquisition of all or part of a target depository institution's assets and liabilities. There is no statutory requirement for shareholders of the acquiring bank to approve the transaction. If the target is a national bank, its shareholders must approve the transaction under 12 U.S.C. 

181 if it will go into liquidation after the purchase and assumption. (In an emergency, OCC may waive the shareholder vote under 12 U.S.C. 181.) Shareholders of participating institutions do not have dissenter's rights in this type of transaction.

A national bank typically uses a purchase and assumption to acquire a target when immediate or expedited action is required to avoid its failure. A national bank also uses a purchase and assumption transaction to acquire branches from another institution.

With OCC approval, Bank Insurance Fund (BIF) insured national banks use purchase and assumption transactions to acquire branches from Savings Association 

Insurance Fund (SAIF) insured savings associations. 

However, the Financial Institutions Reform, Recovery, and Enforcement Act of 1989 (FIRREA) generally places a limit on such sales during a moratorium on insurance fund conversions that is in effect until August 9, 1994 (12 U.S.C. 1815(d)). During the moratorium, FIRREA permits limited branch sales by SAIF insured savings associations if the resulting shift of deposits from SAIF to BIF insurance represents an "insubstantial portion" of deposits of each institution, generally less than 35 percent. Insurance fund exit and entry fees apply. The Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation 

(FDIC) and the Office of Thrift Supervision (OTS) also must approve these transactions.

Common Types of Mergers 

Real Mergers

Real mergers are mergers, consolidations, and purchase and assumption transactions between unaffiliated operating depository institutions. The application for a real merger must include an analysis of the competitive effects of the merger. 

Affiliated Mergers 

Affiliated mergers are mergers, consolidations, and purchase and assumption transactions between two or more operating depository institutions when more than 50 percent of the outstanding voting stock is, or will be, commonly owned before consummation of the merger. 

OCC does not require an applicant to submit a competitive analysis for an affiliated merger since mergers between affiliates have no effect on competition. 

FIRREA allows a SAIF-insured savings association to merge with an affiliated BIF-insured national bank and avoid exit/entrance fees by retaining SAIF insurance on the savings association deposits (12 U.S.C. 1815(d)(3)). 

Since FIRREA allows such mergers only when the institutions are affiliated, a bank's holding company may first charter an interim thrift to acquire assets and liabilities from an unaffiliated savings association and merge the resulting affiliated savings association with 98

Mergers

Introduction the national bank. The Federal Reserve Board and the 

Office of Thrift Supervision must approve the holding company's acquisition of the savings association. 

Interim Bank Mergers

A holding company often uses an interim bank merger to acquire 100 percent of an unaffiliated target institution. Interim bank mergers are also used to facilitate the formation of a bank holding company. 

Interim National Banks-Interim national banks are federally chartered banks established to facilitate interim bank mergers. They do not operate as banks in their own right. Nonetheless, they must comply with chartering and organization requirements. Applicants for interim bank mergers must apply for and receive preliminary approval to organize an interim national bank by filing an application to charter an interim national bank with the merger application. Before consummating the merger or consolidation, the interim bank must organize by preparing required organizational documents. 

Interim national banks must meet the capital requirements of 12 U.S.C. 51. The holding company may borrow from a correspondent bank to capitalize the interim bank. There is no requirement to retain the interim bank's capital in the resulting bank. 

Interim bank directors' residency requirements are the same as those for operating national banks under 12 U.S.C. 72. Interim bank directors can satisfy stock own ership requirements (12 U.S.C. 72 and Interpretive Ruling 

7.4210) by ownership of stock in the operating bank or proposed ownership in the bank holding company. 

Although an interim national bank must be a party to the merger agreement, it does not have to be organized at the time the primary parties execute the initial agreement. The interim bank may become a party to the transaction through an addendum provided in the initial agreement. (See the section on "Agreements" in this introduction.)

Mergers or Consolidations Involving Interim National Banks Interim banks are generally used in two types of transactions. The first type involves an existing bank holding company's acquisition of an unaffiliated target bank. The bank holding company first establishes an interim bank and uses that bank to acquire an unaffiliated target bank by statutory merger (12 U.S.C. 215a) or consolidation (12 U.S.C. 215)). The holding company is assured of 100 percent ownership of the target bank since all shareholders of the target bank must surrender their shares in exchange for consideration. 

The second type of transaction involves the establishment of a bank holding company structure for ownership of a bank. Directors of the existing bank form an interim bank, owned by a corporation, which files with the Federal Reserve Board to become a bank holding company. Then, the existing bank merges into the interim bank. Shareholders of the existing bank normally receive shares of the new bank holding company in exchange for their shares.

For interim bank mergers under 12 U.S.C. 215a, the acquiring bank must be the interim bank and the target bank must be the existing bank. This structure preserves dissenter's rights for the shareholders of the existing bank. Since the purchase and assumption structure does not provide dissenter's rights for shareholders of the target bank, the OCC generally will not approve purchase and assumption transactions that transfer all of a target institution's assets and liabilities to an interim bank.

OCC approval is required for interim bank mergers or consolidations. OCC conditions its approval of an interim bank merger or consolidation on the Federal Reserve's approval of the holding company acquisition or formation. OCC does not require an applicant to submit a competitive analysis for an interim bank merger. The Federal Reserve performs a competitive analysis for holding company acquisitions effected through an interim bank merger. 

Summary of the Process 

A bank must submit a merger application to the OCC for approval. Applicants must submit one complete application that the OCC checks for accuracy and completeness. Once OCC receives an accurate and complete application, it advises the applicant that it has accepted the application and requests four additional copies. OCC sends copies of the application to the 

Department of Justice (DOJ), the Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation (FDIC), and the appropriate Federal Reserve Bank (FRB) for their views on the competitive effects of the proposal. By statute, those agencies generally have 30 days to review and comment on the application.

A depository institution that is party to a merger must publish its intention to merge three times during a 

30-day period. By statute, the public comment period for a merger is 30 days commencing on the date of the first publication.

Following receipt of a complete application, the OCC performs an analysis of the application. If the OCC has concerns about competitive effects, it may ask the ། །་་་་་་་ ། ་་་་་ ་་་་ ་་ཀུ ། 99

Mergers

Introduction 

1 applicants to supply additional information. When the 

OCC has supervisory concerns about merging institution(s), it may request additional information on proposed management, market plan, and the financial condition of the merging institution(s). OCC may delay its decision to await receipt and review of that additional information.

OCC will decide the merger after the end of the 30-day public comment period and the 30-day regulatory comment period. Generally, mergers, except those accepted for processing under a special agreement with 

DOJ, cannot be consummated until 30 days after OCC grants preliminary approval. During the 30-day waiting period, DOJ may seek an injunction that prohibits consummation.

Pursuant to the provisions of a special agreement between the OCC and DOJ, referred to as the "Prospec tive Competitive Factors Report Process," certain mergers are not subject to the 30-day waiting period, but generally can be consummated immediately after satisfying the requirements for OCC approval. Generally, the prospective competitive factors report process covers mergers between affiliated banks, and interim bank mergers filed in conjunction with a holding company application to the FRB.

For all mergers, the applicant must advise the OCC at least 10 days before the desired effective date for the merger so that the OCC may issue a letter certifying the merger. The OCC will issue a certification letter if the bank has met all conditions specified in its approval letter.

Expedited Mergers-The Bank Merger Act provides for expedited mergers when the OCC determines that immediate action is necessary to prevent the probable failure of a depository institution involved in the transaction. Under those circumstances, it allows OCC to eliminate the notice and comment requirements and permits consummation immediately upon approval (refer to the Failure Acquisitions section of this manual). 

Emergency Mergers-The Bank Merger Act also pro vides for expedited mergers when the OCC determines that an emergency exists requiring quick action. Under those circumstances, it permits OCC to shorten the notice and comment periods before approval to 10 days and shortens the delay in consummation after approval to five days. (Policies and procedures for emergency mergers are covered in this section of the manual.)

A national bank interested in acquiring a target on an emergency basis must submit a request to the OCC for emergency processing. The request should contain information to assist the OCC in determining that the target institution is affected by a combination of circumstances sufficiently serious to require swift action. The 

OCC solicits written comments from the target institution's primary supervisory agency to attest to its condition. OCC reviews and evaluates this information and decides whether to approve or disapprove the acquiring national bank's request for emergency processing. If the OCC disapproves the request for emergency processing, the merger can be handled on a nonemergency basis.

The applicant generally submits a standard merger application with the request for emergency processing.

OCC will review the application for accuracy and completeness while it considers the request for emergency processing. Once the OCC has accepted the application, its analysis starts immediately following the OCC decision on the request for emergency processing.

After the OCC accepts the merger application for emergency processing, it provides copies to DOJ, the FRB, and the FDIC for their views on the competitive effects of the proposal. By statute, for an emergency, those agencies have 10 days to review and comment on the application. An institution that is party to an emergency merger must publish its intention to merge two times during a 10-day period. The public comment period for an emergency merger application lasts 10 days.

After OCC approves an emergency merger, the applicant can consummate the transaction at the end of a five-day period during which DOJ may seek an injunction that prohibits consummation. OCC will issue a letter certifying consummation if the bank has met all conditions specified in its preliminary approval letter. 

Mergers with Uninsured Depository Institutions-FDIC must approve any merger with an uninsured depository institution, one without BIF or SAIF insurance. When the resulting bank is a national bank, the OCC also must approve the transaction if it is a merger under 12 U.S.C. 

215a or a consolidation under 12 U.S.C. 215. The OCC 

requires a copy of the FDIC application and assures compliance with legal requirements covering publication, proxy or information statements, and shareholders' approval of the proposed merger or consolidation. A purchase and assumption transaction with an uninsured depository institution requires FDIC approval, but does not require OCC approval, except to authorize any additional branches acquired by a national bank. 

For branch approval, the OCC requires a branch application accompanied by a copy of the FDIC application. 100

Mergers

Introduction Key Policies 

The OCC strives to preserve the soundness of the national banking system and to promote market structures conducive to competition. Normally, it will approve a merger that would not have a substantially adverse effect on competition and would be beneficial to the merging depository institutions and to the public. 

In evaluating a merger, the OCC considers: • The effect of the transaction upon competition. • The convenience and needs of the community to be served. • The financial history of the merging institutions. • The condition of the merging depository institutions, including capital, management, and earnings prospects. • The existence of insider transactions. • The adequacy of disclosure of the terms of the merger.

To determine the effect of a proposed merger upon competition, the relevant geographic market must be identified. The delineation of the relevant market rarely can be precise. However, it should consider the demands of most bank customers and encompass an area where the effect upon competition will be direct and immediate.

In addition, the OCC considers the structure of the market and intensity of competition within it. The OCC weighs, for example, the number of competitors in the market, services offered, pricing of services, advertising, office hours, banking innovations, and the size of the market.

Generally, the OCC will not approve a merger that would result in substantially adverse competitive effects, unless the probable effects of the merger on improved convenience and needs clearly outweigh the adverse effects.

The OCC judges the applicant bank's performance in meeting the credit needs of its community, including the low- and moderate-income neighborhoods, and the merger application may be denied based on that record. In addition, the OCC weighs banking factors and normally will not approve a merger that will result in a bank with inadequate capital, unsatisfactory management, or poor earnings prospects.

In any merger transaction requiring shareholder approval, all shareholders must be informed adequately of all aspects of the transaction.

A merger must be consummated within one year from the date of OCC preliminary approval or the approval will lapse.

Generally Applicable Procedures 

Fees

Applicants must submit a filing fee with the application (refer to 12 CFR 5.5 and the current Notice of the Comptroller of the Currency Fees). If OCC requires an examination, it will charge applicants according to 12 CFR 8.6.

Competitive Analysis 

Applicants have two choices when analyzing the competitive effects of a merger. They may test their proposed merger by using the OCC Quick Check Merger

Screen. The screen quickly identifies those mergers that clearly do not have significant adverse competitive effects. Or, they may submit an independently developed analysis of the competitive effects of the proposed merger, and the OCC will perform an in-depth review to decide if the merger would have a significantly adverse effect on competition.

The submission of the Quick Check Merger Screen saves applicants the time and expense associated with performing an extensive independent analysis. The 

Quick Check Merger Screen uses standard market structure data to test the competitive effects of a proposed merger. There are two tests: an expansive market test and a proximity market test. A proposed merger must pass both tests to demonstrate that it raises no significant adverse competitive effects. 

The Quick Check Merger Screen is merely a procedural device for testing the competitive effects of a merger. 

Experience has shown the screen to be a reliable method for identifying mergers that clearly do not have adverse competitive effects. If a proposed merger fails one or more of the Quick Check Merger Screen tests, OCC makes no presumption about its competitive effects. However, OCC generally requires more detailed information from the applicant. 

Divestitures 

For mergers involving unaffiliated institutions, applicants should review their market areas and determine if any divestitures are anticipated. If so, plans to divest should be discussed at a pre-filing meeting and submitted with the application. Prospective buyers should be identified as soon as possible. If divestitures are required as a condition of approval, applicants may not consummate the merger until they identify buyers and receive approval from the appropriate supervisory 101

Mergers

Introduction 41 02 03 ATT BALL I Pinte agencies. All divestitures must be complete prior to consummation of the merger. 

Forward and Reverse Triangular Mergers 

Pursuant to 12 U.S.C. 215a, the OCC permits forward triangular mergers, mergers in which the target bank is merged into the acquiring bank and shareholders of the target bank receive stock in the holding company of the acquiring bank. OCC generally does not permit reverse triangular mergers, mergers in which the acquiring bank is merged into the target institution which becomes a subsidiary of the holding company. OCC does not permit this structure because it does not provide for dissenter's rights for shareholders of the target bank. 

Exceptions to this policy occasionally have been made when the transaction could not be accommodated through a forward merger and where state law, or other means, afforded protection for dissenting shareholders of the target bank. 

Resulting Bank Charter Number and Title 

In a transaction structured as a merger under 12 U.S.C. 

215a, the resulting bank must generally use the charter number of the acquiring bank. OCC makes an exception to this rule when the acquiring bank is an interim national bank and the target is a national bank. In this case, OCC permits the resulting bank to use the charter number of the target national bank. The applicant determines the title for the resulting bank. 

In a transaction structured as a consolidation under 12 U.S.C. 215, the resulting bank assumes the charter number of a national bank involved in the transaction. 

The applicant determines the title of the resulting bank. 

Head Office Designation 

The applicant may designate any one of the existing head offices of the merging/consolidating depository institutions as the new head office of the resulting bank and may designate the other(s) as a branch office(s). OCC does not require additional branch or relocation filing fees when the designation is made in the merger application. State laws regarding branches are applicable.

Branch Authorization 

OCC will issue branch authorization letters upon consummation of the merger. The authorizations will cover all present head offices of the merging institutions, except the newly designated head office, and all existing branch offices (brick and mortar branches and CBCTS) of merging institutions that are not national banks. Operating branches of national banks will be carried over automatically to the resulting institution. 

Approved, but unopened branches, also must be authorized according to OCC policy (12 CFR 5.30 and 

5.31). To receive a branch number, the bank must notify the OCC 10 days before opening any such branch.

Approval will expire, if the branch is not opened within 

18 months (nine months for a CBCT) after the date of its original preliminary approval.

The applicant should list the requirements of applicable state and federal branching laws in the application and should certify that the resulting branch structure complies with those laws. Certification of the bank's compliance with state law must include consideration of geographic limitations and any qualitative factors. For any market analysis that may be required by state law, the bank should use its community as delineated in its CRA statement. 

When the FDIC is the deciding agency in a purchase and assumption involving an uninsured institution, the OCC must approve any new branches of the resulting national bank. A branch application should be filed with the OCC at the same time the merger application is filed with the FDIC. In such situations, OCC permits a branch application requesting approvals for multiple sites. 

Assumption of Liabilities 12 U.S.C. 215, Consolidation, and 12 U.S.C. 215a, Merger, require the consolidated or receiving association to be liable for all liabilities of the consolidating or merging banks. As a general rule, the OCC applies that requirement to purchase and assumption transactions when the target bank will be dissolved. As a result, for those transactions, OCC requires the acquiring bank to assume all liabilities of the target bank. Any exceptions to this policy require OCC approval on a case-by-case basis. 

Transfers of Low Quality Assets 12 U.S.C. 371c prohibits a bank's purchase of low quality assets from an affiliate. However, the OCC will not generally apply that prohibition to purchase and assumption transactions that are subject to OCC review and approval under 12 U.S.C. 1828 (c). In reviewing those transactions, OCC will consider the effect of the transfer of low quality assets on the resulting bank and may permit transfers that do not raise supervisory concerns. 

Fiduciary Powers

OCC requires a fiduciary powers filing and fee when the resulting bank requests trust powers, unless there is a merging or consolidating national bank that already has them. 

Investment in Bank Premises Limits

The resulting bank in the merger must not exceed the limits for investment in bank premises (12 U.S.C. 371d). 102

Mergers

Introduction UNUVAQTITAINET IRRANT 

If the merger would increase the outstanding amount above the prescribed limit, the applicant should request approval for that increase or address how it will correct the problem (see Interpretive Ruling 7.3100).

Additional Information 

The application must accurately and fully state the facts the OCC needs to determine that a merger is consistent with law and applicable regulations. The OCC may require additional information at any time during the processing of the application. The OCC may ask the applicants to provide additional detailed information about their financial condition, their capabilities to operate in a safe and sound manner, their management, their ability to maintain adequate capital, their earnings prospects, or their responsiveness to community needs. The request will be made by letter that identifies concerns and indicates the information to be submitted. If OCC receives an inadequate response to the request for additional information, the application may be considered withdrawn or denied. The OCC also may conduct examinations and field investigations to develop information further. Applicants will be notified of those activities by letter.

Other Legal Considerations 

When the target institution is not a national bank, the application should include copies and cites of applicable federal or state laws about the consolidation or merger and an opinion from counsel that the proposal is not in contravention of those laws. Counsel should indicate that any action required by the applicable federal or state law has been or will be been taken before consummation of the proposal. Confidentiality

Applicants should prepare confidential data separately and designate it as confidential in the application. The 

OCC will decide whether to give that material confidential treatment according to the Freedom of Information 

Act (5 U.S.C. 552). 

Publication Requirements 

Notice of the application must be published three times in a newspaper of general circulation in each community where the main office of a depository institution involved in the transaction is located. The notice must be published at approximately two-week intervals over a 30-day period beginning with the day the application is filed with the OCC. When published in a daily newspaper, the final publication must be on the thirtieth day after the date of the initial publication or the closest day after that. All notices should be published in the joint names of all depository institutions involved in the transaction and state whether the application involves an interim bank charter application, a merger, consolidation, or purchase and assumption.

Agreements 

An agreement must be approved by a majority of the board of directors of each of the participating depository institutions involved in a merger (12 U.S.C. 215a) or a consolidation (12U.S.C. 215). A purchase of assets and assumption of liabilities agreement is usually executed by management as directed by a board of directors' resolution.

In mergers involving interim banks, the OCC will accept agreements executed between the primary parties, i.e., an operating depository institution and a bank holding company. The agreement must provide for the interim bank to become a party to the agreement upon its organization. This is done through an addendum to the initial agreement. Merger agreements structured in this manner are permissible if: (1) the agreement is a legal instrument satisfying all the requirements of 12 U.S.C. 

215 or 215a; (2) the initial agreement clearly states the intent to include the interim bank in the merger; and (3) the interim bank becomes a party to the agreement before consummation of the merger.

Proxies 

The OCC's division of Securities and Corporate Practices must review and clear proxy or information statements before distribution to shareholders (see 12 CFR 11). This requirement applies to all institutions involved in a merger/consolidation when the resulting bank is a national bank. This requirement applies even when the bank's securities are not subject to the registration provisions of the Securities and Exchange Act of 1934. 

State-member banks having 500 or more shareholders of record must comply with Regulation F (12 CFR 206) for the contents of proxy and information statements. 

State-nonmember banks having 500 or more shareholders of record must comply with Part 335 of the FDIC regulations. Banks not subject to 12 CFR 206 or Part 

335 must comply with 12 CFR 11 for contents of proxy and information statements. OCC encourages applicants to submit proxy and information statements for clearance as early as possible.

The OCC may act to approve a merger before the shareholders' vote. If this occurs, S&CP will notify the bank of the appropriate disclosures to be made in the proxy or information statement. If the OCC approves the merger after S&CP has cleared the proxy statement, but before the shareholders' meeting, S&CP will notify the bank of the requirement for additional disclosures. 103

Mergers

Introduction Shareholder Approval 

When shareholder approval is required, the merger or consolidation agreement must be approved by a twothirds vote of the outstanding shares of each class of stock of each institution. There is no provision for waiving the shareholder approval requirement for a merger or consolidation under 12 U.S.C. 215 or 215a, even in an emergency. If one or more of the institutions is a state institution and the laws of that state require more than a two-thirds affirmative vote for ratification of the agreement, that shareholders' vote must be obtained.

In purchase and assumption transactions, usually no shareholder vote is required for the acquiring or purchasing bank. No shareholder vote is required for the target or selling bank, unless the target is a national bank that will go into liquidation after the purchase and assumption, or unless state law requires it for a target state chartered bank. Shareholders of a liquidating national bank must approve the voluntary liquidation and any related purchase and sale agreement by a two-thirds vote (12 U.S.C. 181). In an emergency, OCC may waive the requirement for shareholders of the liquidating bank to approve the purchase and sale agreement.

Shareholder Meetings 

Notice of the shareholders' meetings to ratify merger or consolidation agreements must be published for four weeks according to 12 U.S.C. 215 and 215a. The publication requirement can be waived if the OCC determines that an emergency exists or by unanimous consent of the shareholders. In addition, at least 10 days prior to the meeting, the banks must mail a notice to each shareholder, except those that specifically waive notice. A meeting should be recessed subject to call if the OCC requires further shareholder participation. 

A wholly-owned bank with only one shareholder may cite unanimous consent approving a merger agreement (if so provided in its holding company's articles of incorporation or bylaws). OCC recognizes action by unanimous consent as the equivalent of holding a shareholders' meeting. Absent a meeting, there is no need to publish notice of the meeting or send a notice to the one shareholder. The OCC will not certify consummation of a merger until it receives the required secretary's certificate(s) for shareholders' approval or certified declaration(s) of unanimous consent from the holding company. 

Oaths of New Directors

The resulting national bank must furnish the OCC with oaths of directors for all new directors added as a result of a merger. In a consolidation, the resulting national bank must furnish the OCC with oaths of directors for all directors.

Merger Accounting 

Mergers and acquisitions of banks are accounted for under either the "pooling of interests" or "purchase" method of accounting. The pooling method is used generally for combinations accomplished by an exchange of common stock for common stock. This method accounts for combining entities by adding together the existing historical-based amounts. Generally, the only accounting adjustment is that required by the issuance of stock by the surviving entity. 

Purchase accounting results when cash, debt and/or stock is exchanged. It records an acquisition on the books of the acquirer by allocating the purchase price to the assets acquired and liabilities assumed, based on their fair values. The capital section of the acquired bank is eliminated and entries are made to record the cash, debt, or stock issued. Any excess of the cost of the acquisition over the net fair value of the identifiable assets and liabilities acquired or assumed is recorded as goodwill. (Goodwill is not part of regulatory capital.) 

The new basis of accounting is recorded on the acquirer's (new parent holding company) books. 

Push-down accounting also establishes this new basis of accounting on the books of the acquired or subsidiary bank. For transactions occurring after September 

30, 1989, push-down accounting is required where 95 percent or more of the voting stock of the bank is acquired. Push-down accounting may be used when more than 80 percent, but less than 95 percent is acquired. In push-down accounting, the bank must include all of the purchase acquisition adjustments on its books. As a result, the acquired bank's assets and liabilities are recorded at their fair value. In any other circumstance, the purchase adjustments should be presented only on the financial statements of the parent holding company. 

Under purchase accounting, marketable securities are recorded based on quoted market values. Certain other assets, such as purchased bank premises and OREO, are accounted for based on appraisals. Liabilities, principally deposits, are generally recorded at historical cost.

The loan portfolio is also accounted for at fair value. 

Determination of fair value is generally made on a loan-by-loan basis and should consider both interest rate and credit risk. The discount resulting from this analysis is accounted for on an individual loan basis. If 

DARNADU IN LIV 25 2 

104

Mergers

Introduction DAUWACHTMEIRRANT 

a loan is on accrual status, the discount should be accreted to income to earn a level yield over the remaining life of the loan. Accretion of discount may be appropriate in some circumstances where the loan is on a nonaccural status. (Refer to OCC Examining Circular 

246 for a discussion of accretion of loans on a nonaccrual status.) Subsequent impairment of value to a loan should be recognized through a charge to current operations.

An exception to the requirement for a loan-by-loan analysis is made for groups of similar consumer loans. 

The fair value of those loans may be determined on an aggregate basis. However, the discount resulting from this analysis should be applied to all the loans in the pool on a pro rata basis so that each can be accounted for separately.

An appropriate allowance for loan losses of the acquired bank may be included as a purchase adjustment. However, the allowance should not exceed the amount on the target bank's books immediately prior to the acquisition. This allowance must also be segregated and used only for losses on loans of the acquired bank. For regulatory assisted transactions, it is not part of regulatory capital.

Any unidentifiable intangible asset of the acquired bank should be recorded as such and amortized in accordance with the procedures set forth in Section 220 of the Comptroller's Handbook for National Bank Examiners. Section 220 requires that banks record all purchased identifiable and unidentifiable intangibles as an asset in accordance with generally accepted accounting principles. Intangible assets should be amortized over their useful life, not to exceed 15 years, except for the core deposit intangible, which can be amortized over a period not to exceed 10 years.

An accelerated method of amortization should be used for identifiable intangible assets in which the determination of the asset's value is based upon declining earning amounts. This would require that larger amounts be amortized in the earlier years of the asset's estimated life. Examples of assets requiring an accelerated method of amortization would be core deposit intangibles and purchased mortgage servicing rights. 

The straight line method of amortization may be used for all other intangible assets, including goodwill. 

Negative Premium Acquisitions 

Cash received from the FDIC in a negative premium situation is considered as one of the assets acquired in the acquisition and should be accounted for accordingly. It is not a capital injection and should not be included as capital. 

In certain negative premium situations, the fair value of the assets acquired, including cash payments received from the FDIC, may exceed the liabilities assumed. In such situations negative goodwill is created. Generally accepted accounting principles require that the noncurrent assets, other than investments in marketable securities, be reduced proportionately by the negative goodwill. Non-current assets would include bank premises. Any remaining negative goodwill is recorded as a liability and is not included as capital. 

The negative premium proposal may include amounts designated as payments for core deposit intangibles or future tax benefits. For accounting purposes, these amounts should be considered to be an element of the purchase price (goodwill or a reduction of negative goodwill). A core deposit intangible may only be recorded on the basis of a formal economic study to determine its value. Additionally, the core deposit intangible cannot exceed the actual premium paid on an acquisition. Therefore, in negative premium situations, core deposit intangibles would generally not be recorded.

Reorganizations A merger, consolidation, or substantial purchase and assumption between two or more affiliated institutions is accounted for at historical cost in a manner similar to pooling of interests accounting previously described. 

Prior to September 30, 1989, OCC policy did not apply push-down purchase accounting to bank acquisitions, unless the bank was acquired through a bank merger. 

Accordingly, push-down accounting was not applied to a number of banks acquired prior to September 30, 1989. When one of those banks is merged into an affiliated bank, the remaining purchase accounting adjustments for the target institution must be pushed down to the bank at the time of the merger. Accordingly, the acquired bank's assets and liabilities would be recorded at their fair value at the time of their original acquisition, adjusted for subsequent dispositions and amortization. 

A purchase and assumption transaction between affiliated institutions which transfers less than 90 percent of the assets of the selling institution should be accounted for at fair value rather than at historical cost.

DDA/MNB Applicants 34. Forwards the application fees, together with a Deposit Memorandum, to P.O. Box 73150, Chicago, IL. 60673-7150. Retains a copy of the memorandum in the official file. 

35. Reviews the application for accuracy and completeness (see Processing Checklist).

NOTE: This review is merely a check to determine if the applicants have completed the application and all required attachments. This review does not address whether the information presented is sufficient to enable the OCC to make a decision on the application. Minor corrections or omissions are handled over the telephone. Other deficiencies are detailed in a letter requesting additions and corrections (see Documents OCC, Information Request, Sample Standard Document). Applicants generally have one opportunity to respond. An inadequate response may cause the application to be considered withdrawn. Filing fees will not be refunded. These instructions are provided to assist in the organizational phase of an interim national bank. After preliminary approval is received to organize the interim national bank, the organizers should proceed immediately to effect the interim national bank's corporate existence.

The following is a general outline for organizing an interim national bank.

I. Establishing A Body Corporate 

To establish a body corporate for the interim bank, organizers should: 

1. Submit an executed, notarized Organization

Certificate (original and 1 copy) (see Docu ments-Applicant, Organization Certificate, Standard Sample Document), (Refer also to 12 

U.S.C. 21, 22, and 23.) 

2. Prepare, execute, and submit articles of association for the interim national bank (original and 1 copy). (See Documents-Applicant, Ar ticles of Association, Standard Sample Docu ment). 

3. Receive OCC's acceptance of the articles of association and the organization certificate. 

Upon receipt of OCC's acceptance, applicants should proceed immediately to organize the interim national bank.

II. Organizing Meetings for the Interim National Bank 

NOTE: Notice of the meetings that follow may be waived, but minutes of the meetings must be documented. (See Documents-Applicant, Organizers'

Meeting-Waiver of Notice, Organizers' Meeting— Minutes, Organizing Board's First Meeting-Waiver of Notice, Organizing Board's First Meeting— Minutes (Standard Sample Documents)). 

A. Organizers hold meeting to: 

1. Adopt the accepted articles of association and organization certificate.

NOTE: The interim bank's corporate exist ence to enter contracts and perform other necessary actions will commence as of the date the organizers adopt the articles of association and organization certificate. 

2. Appoint organizing directors. Procedures

Instructions for Organizing an Interim National Bank

B. Organizing directors hold meeting to: 

1. Execute Oaths of Organizing Directors (see Documents-Applicant, Oath and List of Or ganizing Directors-Individual and Joint, Standard Sample Document). 

NOTE: All organizing directors present should sign the joint oath and any that may be absent should subsequently sign an individual oath. 

2. Appoint acting chairperson and secretary of the board. 

3. Adopt a form of stock (certificate not necessary) for the interim national bank that contains all information required by 12U.S.C. 52. 

4. Designate a bank as a depository of stock subscription funds of the interim national bank. The funds may be invested only in U.S. 

government Treasury securities (repurchase agreements are not acceptable). 

5. Approve any organizational expenses.

Operating Subsidiaries and Other Equity Investments Introduction Summary of Process 

A national bank that wishes to establish or acquire an operating subsidiary or perform new activities through an existing one informs the Director for Analysis in the appropriate OCC district office in a letter of notification. 

Multinational banks and affiliates of multinational banks notify the Deputy Comptroller for Multinational Banking.

The letter describes the subsidiary's proposed activities and location(s). The OCC reviews the proposed activities to determine that they are part of or incidental to banking and to ensure that the proposal is consistent with prudent banking principles and OCC policy. 

A national bank that wishes to make an equity investment in an agricultural credit corporation, an eligible savings association, or any other entity authorized by statute after February 12, 1990 (other equity investments) must also inform the appropriate Director for 

Analysis or Deputy Comptroller for Multinational Bank ing in a letter of notification. The letter describes the business in which the bank proposes to invest, the resources and future prospects of the business, the financial capability of the bank to make the proposed investment, and any statutory limitations. The OCC's decision is based on the legality of the investment, financial capability of the investing bank, and resources and prospects of the business in which the bank proposes to invest.

A subsidiary may begin operating, be acquired or begin performing new activities 30 days after the OCC receives the letter unless the bank is informed otherwise. Also, the bank may make other equity investments 30 days after the OCC receives the letter unless informed otherwise. The 30-day period may be extended upon notice to the bank if the bank's notification raises issues that require additional information or time for OCC analysis. If the OCC notifies the bank of its decision in less than 30 days, the bank may proceed with the proposal upon receipt of the notification. 

Background 

Previous OCC regulations required banks to submit extensive applications to establish subsidiaries. Because subsidiaries perform only those activities that can be carried out by the parent bank, the requirement of an application imposes an unnecessary burden on banks choosing to conduct their activities through operating subsidiaries. Therefore, in October 1983, the 

OCC changed the requirement from an application subject to approval to a letter of notification (48 FR 48454).

National banks are permitted by statute to make certain equity investments. In 1990, the OCC published 12 

CFR 5.36, that governs other equity investments by national banks and sets forth the notification requirement. Currently, the OCC notification requirement is limited to equity investments in eligible savings associations, agricultural credit corporations, and other entities that may be authorized by statute subsequent to the publication of 12 CFR 5.36 (55 FR 998).

Key Policies 

Ownership

To qualify as an operating subsidiary, the parent bank must own at least 80 percent of the corporation's voting stock. Operating subsidiaries may participate in partnerships. Entering a partnership is considered an "activity" of an operating subsidiary, thus, notification is still required when an existing subsidiary wishes to enter a partnership and will engage only in the same business as before.

Equity investments in agricultural credit corporations are authorized under 12 U.S.C. 24 (Seventh) and are limited to 20 percent of the unimpaired capital and surplus of the bank, unless the bank owns at least 80 percent of the stock of the agricultural credit corporation. Equity investments in eligible savings associations are authorized under section 13 of the Federal Deposit 

Insurance Act, as amended by the Financial Institutions Reform, Recovery, and Enforcement Act of 1989. The 

OCC will consider a savings association acquired under this authority to be a "statutory subsidiary." 

Statutory Limitations 

The OCC may impose one or more legal or supervisory conditions before permitting a bank to acquire or establish an operating subsidiary, perform new activities in an existing operating subsidiary, or make equity investments. They shall be enforceable as conditions imposed in writing by the OCC in connection with the granting of a request by a bank within the meaning of 12 U.S.C. 1818. 

Unless specifically required by the OCC in a prior decision or opinion letter, a bank need not submit a subsequent letter of notification to establish or acquire an operating subsidiary, provided the activities of the new subsidiary are limited to those which the subject bank had previously reported to the OCC and the OCC still considers legally permissible. 

Unless otherwise provided by statute or regulation, pertinent book figures of the parent bank and its operating subsidiaries will be consolidated to conform to applicable statutory limitations, such as 12 U.S.C. 56, 60, 84 or 371d. For example, the combined exposure of the parent bank and all of its operating subsidiaries 124

Operating Subsidiaries and Other Equity Investments Introduction to a single borrower shall not exceed the bank's lending limit (12 U.S.C. 84).

Examination and Supervision Operating subsidiaries and other equity investments shall be subject to OCC examination and supervision. 

If, upon examination, the OCC finds that the subsidiary was created or is operated in violation of law or regulation or that the method of operation is unsafe or unsound, it will direct the bank to take appropriate remedial action, which may include disposing of all or part of the subsidiary. If other equity investments are found to be in violation of law or are unsafe or unsound, the OCC will direct the bank to take appropriate remedial action, which may include divestiture of all or part of the equity investment.

Unless otherwise provided by statute or regulation, all provisions of federal banking laws, regulations and rulings applicable to the operations of the parent bank shall be equally applicable to those of its operating subsidiaries. A savings association acquired as a statutory subsidiary may continue to hold assets and exercise powers in accordance with the laws and regulations governing such operations. Statewise Mergers Introduction Applicability 

A merger, consolidation, or purchase and assumption involving a state chartered bank(s) and a national bank(s) that results in a state bank is termed a "statewise merger."

Statewise mergers must comply with the provisions of 

12 U.S.C. 214-214c and 321. Although OCC approval is not required, certain procedures must be followed to consummate the merger of a national bank into a state bank. The major concerns in statewise mergers are: • The return of OCC documents to Central Records. • Timely Licensing Information System entries to update a national bank's institution database records. 

Summary of Process 

A bank must submit to the OCC a letter of notification when the merger application is filed with the state, the Federal Reserve, or the FDIC. The notification permits the OCC to monitor the national bank's compliance with the appropriate laws on mergers, particularly as those statutes relate to shareholders' rights. The OCC sends the national bank instructions for terminating its national bank status upon its receipt of the notification. Once the merger is effective, and the required information is submitted to the OCC, status as a national bank is terminated.

Branch Closings Introduction Summary of Process 

When a bank has decided to close a branch office (brick and mortar or Customer Bank Communication 

Terminal (CBCT)), it must notify by letter the appropriate district office (multinational banks or their affiliates to 

Multinational Banking in Washington) and identify the branch to be closed (by branch certificate number, exact location and popular name) and the effective date of closing. The letter should certify that bank management is acting in accord with a board of directors' resolution. A copy of this action should be kept in the bank's files. 

The bank should enclose the original branch certificate/authorization letter from the OCC in the branch closing notification. If the original cannot be located or if the branch is one of several authorized by the same letter, the bank must indicate in its letter of notification that the certificate was lost, or submit a copy of the original multiple branch authorization letter, and clearly indicate which branch is being closed and the effective date.

If a branch closing results in a change in the bank's existing community delineation as designated in its 

CRA statement (12 CFR 25), that statement must be revised. On the effective date, the OCC will cancel the certification/authorization and remove the branch from the national bank branch records. Policy

Branch closings are management decisions. However, the OCC encourages national banks to develop and implement effective written procedures for branch closings. The OCC's views are stated in Banking Circular 

189, "Branch Closings and Reductions in Service," dated February 20, 1985. Banking Circular 189 states the following policy:

National banks should review their policies for branch closings and reductions in service to ensure that they include procedures to determine objectively which branches are to be closed, what services should be reduced or eliminated, and to communicate anticipated plans to their customers and affected communities well in advance of implementation.

Consistent with prudent management practices, written policies and procedures might include con sideration of: 

• Profits generated by the bank's branch system and the profitability of the targeted branch. • Actions to make the targeted branch viable, e.g., adjusting hours, services, facilities, and automation. • Presence in the neighborhood of other financial institutions, their accessibility and services. • How and when customers of the targeted branch will be notified of proposed reductions in services or closings, e.g., advance meetings with key neighborhood and political leadership. • Actions to minimize the affect of a branch closing on the neighborhood, e.g., offering such special services as check cashing, ATMs, night deposits, and disclosing future plans for the facility. 

Conversion of a Brick and Mortar Branch to a CBCT

If a bank wishes to convert a brick and mortar branch to a CBCT, the appropriate district office (or Multinational Banking in Washington for multinational banks or their affiliates) must be notified in writing prior to the conversion. The district office responds with a confirmation of the branch conversion and assigns a new CBCT number to the branch. The bank should attach the new CBCT branch number to the old branch certificate/authorization letter. If the CBCT is converted subsequently to a full service branch, a new branch application is required and should be filed in accordance with the procedures in this manual for Domestic 

Branches, Seasonal Agencies, and CBCT Branches. 

Fees

There is no fee for a branch closing or conversion to a CBCT.

Change in Bank Control Introduction Summary of Process The Change in Bank Control Act of 1978 (CBCA) (12 

U.S.C. 1817(j)) as amended, requires parties who wish to acquire control of a national bank through purchase, assignment, transfer, pledge, or other disposition of voting stock to notify the Office of the Comptroller of the Currency (OCC) in writing 60 days prior to the proposed acquisition. The required notice must be mailed or delivered to Multinational Banking in Washington or the district office that has the primary supervisory authority over the banking organization. The OCC will consider the notice and may disapprove the acquisition within 60 days or extend the review period if additional information or investigation is required. The acquiring party must publish an announcement of the proposed change in bank control within 10 days of receiving the OCC's confirmation that the notice is technically complete. The notice is subject to a 20-day public comment period. If the notice is an interstate filing, the 20-day public comment period may be extended to allow sufficient time for all interested parties to comment. The notice must contain: • Information about the voting shares to be acquired and shares presently held. • The acquiring party's financing of the purchase, plans for sale or merger of the bank, and other significant bank stock owned. 

Biographical and financial information on the acquiring party.

The OCC reviews the notice to determine that: • All required information has been provided. • The acquisition does not lessen competition. • The financial condition of the acquiring party does not threaten the stability of the bank or prejudice the interests of the depositors. • The competence of bank management is not threatened. • The integrity of the acquiring party is satisfactory. • There is no risk to the bank insurance fund.

The notice is reviewed initially at the district office or in Multinational Banking, and a decision on the acquisition may be made by the Deputy Comptroller (District) or the Deputy Comptroller for Multinational Banking in the case of multinational companies. Cases may be referred to Washington for decision. Applicability

The procedures in this section generally apply when a party wishes to purchase or otherwise acquire controlling interest in a national bank. They also generally pertain to persons purchasing or otherwise acquiring a controlling interest through a voting trust or voting agreement. However, when acquisition of a controlling interest results from foreclosure of a debt previously contracted (DPC) in good faith, a bona fide gift, or succession from an estate, the prior notice requirement in this section does not apply. Instead, the acquiring party must notify the OCC within 30 days after the acquisition by filing the complete notice and the appropriate filing fee. For the "good faith" element of the 

DPC exemption to be satisfied, a lender must either make or acquire a loan secured by bank stock in advance of any known default. A person or persons acting in concert, who purchase a previously defaulted loan secured by bank stock, cannot rely on the DPC exemption to foreclose on that loan, seize or purchase the underlying collateral, and acquire control of the bank while escaping the Act's prior notice requirements. Certain bank holding company transactions and acquisitions of control of foreign banks with federally chartered branches also are exempt from the prior notice requirement.

The regulation requires any party acquiring 25 percent or more of a class of voting securities of a national bank to file a Change in Bank Control notice. In addition, if any party acquires 10 percent or more (but less than 

25 percent), that party must file a Change in Bank

Control notice if: 

• The institution has issued any class of securities subject to the registration requirements of section 12 

of the Securities Exchange Act of 1934 (15 U.S.C. 

781); or • Immediately after the transaction no other party will own a greater proportion of that class of voting securities.

The presumption of control is rebuttable for a purchase of 10 to 25 percent ownership interest. Waiver of the notice requirement will be granted when the presumption of control is refuted successfully by the acquiring party.

Once the OCC has not objected to a person and that person has acquired control of a national bank, there is no further provision under the CBCA or the Comptroller's regulations requiring the control person to give another notice to the Comptroller or file an amended notice in order for that person to purchase additional shares of stock of the same national bank in the future. 137

Change in Bank Control Introduction 

The CBCA may be applicable to an escrow arrangement if absolute voting control over the requisite percentage of a bank's stock is transferred to the escrow agents. Generally, applicability will be determined by the district office or Multinational Banking in Washington. 

Simultaneous Acquisitions

If two (or more) parties, not acting in concert, each propose to acquire simultaneously equal percentages of 10 percent or more of a national bank's voting stock, the OCC interprets the CBCA regulations to mean that both (or all) parties may have acquired control and, consequently, must comply with the CBCA prior notice requirement or rebut the control presumption. 

Hart-Scott-Rodino Antitrust Improvements Act of 1976 

Persons required to file a notice under the Change in Bank Control Act (CBCA) should consider the applic ability of the Hart-Scott-Rodino Antitrust Improvements 

Act of 1976 (the "HSR") to their proposed acquisition. 

Among other things, the HSR amended Section 7 of the Clayton Antitrust Act to establish premerger notification and waiting requirements for persons planning to consummate very large mergers and acquisitions. 

Although the Federal Trade Commission (FTC) has by regulation exempted acquisitions subject to the CBCA from the requirements of the HSR, the exemption is only available if copies of all information and documentary materials filed with the OCC are simultaneously filed with the FTC and the Assistant Attorney General at least 30 days prior to consummation of the proposed acquisition. 

Only those transactions that meet certain threshold tests are subject to the HSR. Generally, those threshold tests relate to the size of the parties to the transaction and the transaction itself. The acquiring party and the target bank must determine the applicability of the HSR to the proposed transaction and, if required, file a copy of the CBCA notice with the FTC and DOJ. The HSR authorizes the assessment of civil money penalties for failure to comply with the HSR provisions.

The implementing regulations provide an exemption of the HSR notice and waiting period if the change in control is necessary to prevent the probable failure of the bank. In such situations, the proposed acquirors must notify the FTC and DOJ their intent to rely on the exemption, and request that OCC act under the exemption. 

Push-down Accounting 

Pursuant to the Call Report Instructions and information contained in the OCC's Bank Accounting Advisory 

Series dated June 1990 and April 1991, push-down accounting is required for financial reporting purposes if an arms-length purchase accounting transaction results in a change in bank control of at least 95 percent of the voting stock of the bank. Definitions

Control is the power, exercised directly or indirectly, to direct the management or policies of the bank or vote 

25 percent or more of any class of voting stock.

Party means an individual or a corporation, partnership, trust, association, joint venture, pool, syndicate, sole proprietorship, unincorporated organization, or any other form of entity not specifically listed in 12 

Key Policies

The CBCA gives the OCC the authority to disapprove changes in control of national banks. The OCC's objectives in its administration of the CBCA are to enhance and maintain public confidence in the national banking system by preventing identifiable serious adverse effects resulting from anticompetitive combinations of interests, inadequate financial support, and unsuitable management in national banks. The OCC will review each notice to acquire control of a national bank and will disapprove transactions that would have serious. harmful effects. If the notice is disapproved, the disapproval letter will contain a statement of the basis for disapproval. The OCC may extend the 60-day review period or may issue a notice of its intention prior to the end of the 

60-day period. An acquisition may not be completed prior to the sixty-first day unless the acquiring party is notified in writing prior to the end of the 60-day review period that the OCC has no objection to the proposed change in bank control. Disclosure

The OCC requires publication of the notice to acquire a national bank by the acquiring party in a newspaper of general circulation in the bank's community. Information contained in the notice will be kept confidential until the OCC has acted. At that time, certain summary information will be released and made available consistent with requests made subject to the Freedom of 

Information Act (FOIA), 5 U.S.C. 552. 

Technically Complete Notices 

The notice must be technically complete for the 60-day statutory time frame to begin. The time frame is computed according to 12 CFR 5.12, except that the last

MARWAHITLAW LIDDAOL 

138

Change in Bank Control Introduction day of any applicable period must be included regardless of whether it is a Saturday, Sunday, or legal holiday. 

The notice will be reviewed promptly upon receipt to determine if it is technically complete and the acquiring party informed of that fact by letter. The letter will also inform the acquiring party of the earliest possible date on which the transaction may be consummated. 

A notice is technically complete when it answers all questions and addresses all requested information. 

Technical completeness does not imply that the content of the notice is acceptable or sufficient for OCC action. 

Review Periods and Extensions 

The OCC has authority under 12 U.S.C. 1817(j) to disapprove a proposed change in control of a national bank. The acquisition may not be consummated if the 

OCC disapproves it within the review period or any extension thereof. 

The 60-day review period commences on the date the notice is declared technically complete by the OCC. 

The acquiring party will be notified of this date and, unless disapproved or the review period is extended, the proposed change in bank control may be consummated in 61 days or more after the technically complete date. The last day of the review period will be the sixtieth day from the date of technical completion regardless of whether it is a Saturday, Sunday or legal holiday. 

The 60-day period for disapproval may be extended for 

30 days at the discretion of the OCC. The review period may be extended an additional two times for not more than 45 days each time if:

The OCC determines that any acquiring party has not furnished all the information required. • In the OCC's judgment, any material information submitted is substantially inaccurate. • The OCC has been unable to complete the investigation of an acquiring party because of any delay caused by, or the inadequate cooperation of, such acquiring party. • The OCC determines that additional time is needed to investigate and determine that no acquiring party has a record of failing to comply with the require ments of subchapter II of chapter 53 of title 31, United States Code (Bank Secrecy Act). 

Standards of Review

The OCC will review the notice to determine if any of the following statutory conditions are present: • The proposed acquisition of control would result in a monopoly or attempted monopoly in the business of banking in any part of the United States. • The effects of the proposed acquisition of control would be substantially anticompetitive and are not outweighed by benefits to the convenience and needs of the public. • The financial condition of any acquiring party would jeopardize the stability of the bank or prejudice the interests of the depositors. • The competence, experience, or integrity of any acquiring party or of any proposed management indicates that it would not be in the interests of the depositors or of the public to allow such control of the bank. • Any acquiring party has failed to furnish the OCC with all required information. • There is a risk to the bank insurance fund.

Information must be provided only once in the notice. 

If information on stock purchases is specified in a tender offer that is also submitted, it may be summarized and incorporated by reference in the notice. 

If a change in bank control is proposed for a trust, credit card, or other nonbank bank, pursuant to the Competi tive Equality Banking Act of 1987 (P.L. 100-86) (CEBA), in addition to the prior notice requirement and filing fee, the OCC must be informed of any changes planned in the business or operations of the bank. In some cases, such limited purpose banks may not have federal deposit insurance. However, even when uninsured, they are subject to the CBCA as a standard condition of their chartering to ensure that the OCC has the opportunity to review any changes in control of a national bank. 

Generally Applicable Procedures 

Filing Fees

The appropriate filing fee must be enclosed with a filed notice (refer to 12 CFR 8 and the Annual Notice of the 

Comptroller of the Currency Fees). Filing fees must be borne by the acquiring party and must not be paid for, directly or indirectly, by the bank. Filing fees are not refunded.

Distribution of Notices to Other Agencies 

The OCC will determine independently the accuracy and completeness of any information provided. This may include referral to the appropriate federal or state law enforcement agencies and will include a background investigation. J 1 20 བ་ 139

Change in Bank Control Introduction 

A copy of the notice will be submitted to the appropriate 

Federal Reserve Bank, Federal Home Loan Bank and 

FDIC regional office. A copy also may be provided to the appropriate state bank regulatory agencies. 

Background Investigation 

The OCC will conduct an investigation of the competence, experience, integrity, and financial ability of each party named in a notice of a proposed acquisition and will make an independent determination of the accuracy and completeness of any information described and required by statute with respect to such party. 

The OCC routinely performs Federal Bureau of Investigation (FBI) and other background checks on individuals seeking to enter the national banking system through changes in bank control. The OCC performs standard background checks on all acquiring parties through the: • FBI. • United States Custom Service. 

Drug Enforcement Administration • LEXIS-NEXIS and Enforcement and Compliance In formation System (ECIS) records search. • Other regulatory agencies' records when the acquiring party has been associated with institutions under another agency's regulatory jurisdiction. • State depository institution regulatory agencies when the acquiring party has been involved with institutions or organizations under a state's regulatory jurisdiction.

Information obtained by the OCC from the background investigation may be shared with the other financial institutions regulatory agencies.

The OCC may not conduct some of the standard background checks if, in individual cases, the performance of the check would be redundant; would not serve the 

OCC's purpose for background investigation; or for other reasons. In other cases, the OCC will pursue additional background checks believed warranted by the circumstances. Checks may include, but are not limited to, requests for background information from the 

Internal Revenue Service (IRS), Securities Exchange Commission (SEC), Commodity Futures Trading Commission (CFTC), National Association of Securities Dealers (NASD), INTERPOL, Central Intelligence 

Agency (CIA), U.S.

State Department, the International 

Monetary Fund (IMF), the World Bank, the U.S. Im migration and Naturalization Service), and state and local law enforcement agencies. The OCC may also require fingerprints from an acquiring party if they are needed to access information necessary to complete a background investigation. The OCC may request additional information deemed necessary to make an independent determination of the accuracy and completeness of any statements in the notice of a change in bank control.

Background investigations involving foreign citizens generally require more time to complete. In those instances where the acquiring party has international ties, in addition to the regular background sources, the OCC requires submission of certified statements as part of a technically complete notice. The certified statements attest to the person's integrity and financial capacity. In addition, all financial statements submitted by foreign citizens should be presented in United 

States dollars.

Accurate and Complete Information 

The CBCA was enacted to protect banks' depositors, customers, minority shareholders, and communities from serious negative consequences that could follow certain changes in control. The CBCA requires the OCC to evaluate proposed changes in control of national banks. The OCC's primary source of information is the 

CBCA notice filed by the acquiring party. The notice must provide complete, accurate, and not misleading information. The acquiring party is fully responsible for understanding the CBCA notice's requirements and for the accuracy of the statements made to the OCC. The OCC is authorized to subpoena records and take testimony to determine whether a party has provided false information in a notice of change in bank control or has otherwise violated the CBCA. The OCC has independent authority to seek civil injunctive relief and is permitted extensions of processing time when needed to ensure complete and accurate information. 

The availability of civil injunctive relief permits the OCC to prevent unlawful acquisitions or require divestiture in cases where banks have been acquired in violation of the CBCA.

The OCC will take prompt remedial action against an acquiring party who provides false or incomplete information. Any misrepresentation or omission of a material fact in a CBCA notice constitutes fraud in the inducement and is grounds for denial of the proposed change in control, and may subject the acquiring party to legal sanctions provided by 18 U.S.C. 1001. In addition, the 

CBCA provides substantial civil penalties for willful violations. The OCC may also publicize any remedial action taken against a potential acquiror. 140

Change in Bank Control Introduction 

Generally, previously issued no objections to a change in control will be revoked prior to consummation if the OCC becomes aware of material violations of law, misrepresentations, or any fraudulent activity on the part of the acquiring party. 

Tender Offers

Any tender offer to purchase stock must be reviewed and cleared by the OCC.

Material Changes 

The acquiring party must notify the OCC promptly, before the date of consummation, of any material change in financial or other conditions that may affect the OCC's decision on the filing.

Publication Requirements 

The acquiring party must publish an announcement of the proposed change in bank control within 10 days of receiving the OCC's confirmation that the notice is technically complete. The announcement must provide the information required by 12 CFR 5.50(4)(h) and state that interested parties have 20 days to file comments with the appropriate district office or Multinational Banking.

The acquiring party must publish in the newspaper having the largest general circulation in the community in which the affected national bank's home office is located an announcement containing the following information: • The name of the affected national bank. • The identity of each party proposing to acquire the national bank. • The date upon which the statutory period for OCC review expires. 

The announcement also must state that: • Comments may be furnished to the appropriate district office or Multinational Banking (identifying the office by name and address) within 20 days of publication of the announcement. • A letter of no objection can be issued in advance of the end of the statutory review period. • The OCC may extend the period of review consistent with the provisions of 12 U.S.C. 1817(j). • Certain portions of the notice will be kept confidential until the OCC has acted, but at that time certain additional summary information will be released and made available, upon the request of any party, consistent with the Freedom of Information Act, 5 U.S.C. 

552 ("FOIA") and 12 CFR 5.50(h)(2).

Within 20 days of receiving the OCC's notification that the notice is technically complete, the acquiring party must submit to the appropriate district office or Multinational Banking a copy of the published announcement and the newspaper publisher's certification or affidavit. 

Filings Subject to the Williams Act Amendments 

Change in Bank Control notices, filed in contemplation of a public tender offer subject to the requirements of the Williams Act Amendments to the Securities and 

Exchange Act of 1934, may be given confidential treatment and publication delayed (but not waived) for up to 34 days after the technically complete notice is filed if: (1) the acquiring party requests such treatment and states that a public announcement of the tender offer and the filing of appropriate forms with either the Securities and Exchange Commission or the appropriate federal banking agency, as applicable, will occur within 34 days from the filing of the notice; and (2) the OCC determines that it is in the public interest to grant such treatment. This procedure was adopted because of the OCC's concern that an acquiring party or bidder complying with the Williams Act Amendments for public tender offers might be disadvantaged due to the delays imposed by the Change in Bank Control Act review period.

When the OCC grants confidential treatment, a public announcement of the change in control is required upon first publication to security holders or on the thirtyfourth day after filing the technically complete notice, whichever occurs first. The acquiring party must send proof of the publication of the announcement to the district office or Multinational Banking that is reviewing the notice within 10 days of the date of its publication. 

For other requests for confidential treatment, the OCC will be guided by the presumption that the filing of such notices should be announced immediately, but may authorize delayed announcement if it would not serve the public interest.

Waiver of Publication The OCC has the authority to waive publication and the 20-day comment period. In accordance with the CBCA, the conditions under which publication can be waived are limited. The CBCA requires public comment on proposed acquisitions unless the OCC has determined in writing that such disclosure and solicitation of public comment would seriously threaten the safety or soundness of the bank. Waivers are limited primarily to failing bank situations. Publication may not be waived for 2 141

Change in Bank Control Introduction those filings for which expeditious processing is requested unless the above condition applies. Decision

Cases may be decided prior to the end of the 60-day statutory review period, but not before the end of the public comment period and receipt of proof of publication.

Section 914 of the Financial Institutions Reform, Recovery, and Enforcement Act of 1989 (FIRREA) re quires banks that undergo a change in control to file a notice with the OCC prior to adding or replacing a member of the board of directors, or employing or changing the responsibilities of a person to a position as senior executive officer for a period of two years following the consummation of the change in control. 

The OCC's implementing regulation, 12 CFR 5.51, provides information on how, what, and where to file. 

Change in Control Advisory Opinions 

Pursuant to statutes, regulations and a policy statement on the interagency exchange of supervisory information, the OCC provides information to the other federal financial regulatory agencies on proposed non-national bank acquirors of financial institutions under the jurisdiction of those agencies when requested. Procedures for responding to these requests are contained in a subsection entitled Change in Bank Control Advisory Opinions.
