1 20 FE 143

Change in Bank Control 

Procedures

Acquiring Party 

Deputy Comptroller (District)(DCD)/(DCMNB) 

Acquiring Party Prefiling 1. Refers acquiring party that requests information to this section of the 

Comptroller's Manual for Corporate Activities and to the applicable regulation (12 CFR 5.50).

NOTE: Changes in bank control resulting from foreclosure of a debt previously contracted in good faith, testate or intestate succession, or a bona fide gift are exempt from the prior notice requirements. However, such changes are not exempt from the notification requirement, and a completed notice and filing fee must be submitted to the OCC within 30 days of acquisition. 

NOTE: If the OCC is presented with a proposal where there may be an assignment of the potential to exercise absolute voting control, but no apparent de facto acquisition of control has occured, as may be the case with an escrow agent, then, before determining whether compliance with the CBCA is required, the DDA or DCMNB will consider: • Whether the time the escrow agents or other assignees could vote a controlling interest in a national bank is long enough to require a 

CBCA notice. 

• Whether an escrow or similar arrangement serves a business purpose. • Whether there is a reasonable likelihood that any shareholder votes will be taken and whether the assignors will instruct the escrow agents or other assignees on how to vote the shares if the shares are required to vote.

Other factors also may be analyzed as required by the facts of a specific case. 

2. May rebut change in control presumption (see Documents-Applicant, Rebuttal Letter, Change in Bank Control). 

NOTE: The rebuttal and/or notice must be mailed or hand delivered to the district office that has the primary supervisory authority over the banking organization. (Multinational banks file with the Deputy Comptroller for Multinational Banking (DCMNB) in Washington.) 

3. Waives the filing of a change in bank control notice requirement if the 

OCC finds such rebuttal to be convincing. Generally notifies acquiring party of waiver within 30 days of receipt of presumption of control rebuttal letter (see Documents-OCC, Rebuttal Response Letter, 

Change in Bank Control).

Filing the Notice 

4. Completes the notice and submits the original and five copies and a check for the filing fee to the appropriate district office or DCMNB (see 

Applications and Notices, Change in Bank Control). Filing fees are not refunded. 

NOTE: All acquisitions of 25 percent or more are subject to the notice requirements of the Change in Bank Control Act (CBCA). The OCC 

presumes acquisition of control when the acquiring party intends to 

HARVARIT LAW LINDRO 

144

Change in Bank Control Procedures DDA/DCMNB 

acquire a voting share in the bank of 10 percent or more, but less than 25 percent. This presumption applies when: (1) the acquiring party will be the largest shareholder after the transaction; or (2) the institution to be acquired has issued any class of securities subject to the registration requirements of the Securities Exchange Act of 1934 [refer to 12 CFR 

NOTE: If two (or more) parties, not acting in concert, each propose to acquire, simultaneously, equal percentages of 10 percent or more of a national bank's voting stock, the OCC interprets the CBCA regulations to mean that both (or all) parties may have acquired control and, consequently, each must comply with the CBCA's prior notice requirement or rebut the control presumption. 

NOTE: When shares are acquired pursuant to a voting trust or voting agreement, the acquiring party must consult with the district office or 

DCMNB to discuss notice filing requirements (see Policy Statements section of this manual for the OCC's policy statement regarding voting trusts).

NOTE: All banks operating with capital levels below those expected by the OCC should be aware that the OCC may object to changes in control or acquisitions of such banks unless capital is restored promptly to appropriate levels. Such situations may pose a risk to the bank insurance fund, a statutory basis for disapproval. 

Processing and Reviewing the Notice 

5.

Establishes the official file and commences processing the notice (see 

Documents-OCC, Processing Checklist, Change in Bank Control). 

Forwards the filing fee, together with a deposit memorandum (Form 

6043-01) to P.O. Box 73150, Chicago, IL 60673-7150. 

6. Makes the initial data entries into the Licensing Information System (LIS). 

7. Promptly reviews a submitted notice to determine that it is technically complete.

NOTE: This review determines whether the notice addresses all required areas, including the Summary Fact Sheet (see Applications and 

Notices, Change in Bank Control, Summary Fact Sheet) and whether sufficient information has been submitted to begin a thorough review of the proposed acquisition. The thorough review of the information will begin after the notice is declared technically complete. A technically complete notice includes: • Executed and signed copies of all pertinent purchase and sale agreements and related documents describing the terms and conditions of a proposed acquisition. • Copies of any loan agreements and commitment letters relating to funds borrowed to make a proposed acquisition. • Copies of any invitations or tender offers or advertisements making a tender offer to stockholders for purchase of their stock to be used for a proposed acquisition. 1 30 145

Change in Bank Control Procedures Acquiring Party 

• Signed financial statements from each acquiring party, that includes detailed information on all significant assets and liabilities, and contingent liabilities, and on current and projected cash flow denominated in U.S. dollars. • In the case of foreign persons, certified statements from officials in the foreign country, attesting to the person's integrity and financial capacity (see Applications and Notices, Standard Sample Docu ment, Foreign Certifications). 

8. If the notice is technically complete, notifies the acquiring party by letter of that fact, of the publication requirement, and of the date the 60-day review period expires (see Documents-OCC, Technically Complete Notice, Change in Bank Control). Enters the technically complete date into LIS.

Checks the Weekly Bulletin for accuracy and completeness and contacts LP&S technicians via EMC2/TAO to report Weekly Bulletin omissions or corrections.

NOTE: The 60-day review period begins on the date the notice is technically complete. That date will be the day of the initial receipt of the original notice if it is found to be technically complete. If corrections or additional information are required, the technically complete date will be the receipt date of the corrected notice provided it is found to be technically complete. 

9. If the notice is incomplete, informs the acquiring party by letter and requests the missing information. The request letter must be mailed promptly after the OCC makes that determination. Enters technically complete date into LIS following receipt of the corrected notice. Checks the Weekly Bulletin for accuracy and completeness and contacts LP&S 

technicians via EMC2/TAO to report Weekly Bulletin omissions or cor rections. 

10. Publishes an announcement of the filing of the notice in a newspaper with the largest general circulation in the community in which the main office of the bank is located (see Documents-Applicant, Legal Notice, Change in Bank Control). 

NOTE: Publication of the announcement must occur within 10 days of receipt of the OCC's letter indicating that the notice is technically complete. Within 20 days, the acquiring party must submit proof of publication to the OCC, including a copy of the published announcement and the newspaper publisher's certification or affidavit. 

NOTE: Except in instances of safety and soundness, all notices must be published during the process. There may be cases qualifying for a waiver of the publication and public comment period requirements. 

When the district or MNB identifies a filing that may qualify for a waiver, they consult with BOS and provide the details of the case. The district or MNB prepares the required written waiver for signature by the appropriate delegated official. If the filing does not qualify for a waiver of publication, the reasons must be documented in the file. A copy of the written waiver or written documentation for those cases not qualifying for a waiver must be forwarded to BOS (see Documents-OCC, Publication Waiver Form, Change in Bank Control). 

HARVA 

146

Change in Bank Control Procedures 

DDA/DCMNB 

NOTE: Tender offer notices under the Williams Act amendments may result in a delay of the 10-day publication requirement. However, publication is still required as described in 12 CFR 5.50(h)(1)(i) and (ii). 

NOTE: The district office or MNB must take appropriate measures to insure that proof of publication is documented in the official file. Normally this should not pose a problem. However, those cases forwarded expeditiously to the Washington Office for decision absent the appropriate publication affidavit must note that the affidavit is not in the file and that it will be forwarded to BOS as soon as it is received. 

11. Sends a copy of the technically complete notice to the appropriate 

Federal Reserve Bank (FRB), Office of Thrift Supervision (OTS), and 

Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation (FDIC) regional office, request ing comments (see Documents-OCC, Agency Comments Request, Change in Bank Control). If applicable, also sends a copy to the proper state regulatory agency (see PPM 6100-3 (Rev.), January 22, 1986).

Sends a copy to BOS if it is apparent that the notice will not be decided under delegated authority. 

12. Conducts an investigation of the competence, experience, integrity, and financial ability of each acquiring party named in the notice and makes an independent determination of the accuracy and completeness of any of the information submitted. 

All requests for background information (including fingerprint cards) should contain the application control number and request the responding agency reference the control number in their response. 

In those instances where there is a conflict between the OCC's need to verify an person's qualifications and a request that a present employer not be contacted, the issue should be resolved in favor of the OCC's need to know. If, however, sufficient information appears to be available through alternative sources, it might not be necessary to contact present employers in all cases. The person should not be given assurances that such contacts will not be made. If contact with present employers will be made, the person should be notified prior to contacting the employer. 

Information obtained by the OCC from the background investigation may be shared with the other financial institutions regulatory agencies. 

The following standard background checks must be performed for all acquiring parties: • Verify those items identified separately in the "Investigation and 

Verification Checklist." 

• Perform background checks on each acquiring party (see Documents-OCC, Request for Background Information, Change in Bank Control). Standard background checks have been adopted by the 

Federal Financial Institutions Examination Council and must be performed for all acquiring parties (see Policy section of this Manual for the interagency agreement on background investigations). If any of the standard background checks are waived, the reasons must be documented fully in the case file and incorporated with the Investigation and Verification Checklist into the confidential memorandum (see 

Documents-OCC, Investigating Notices, Change in Bank Control). 

147

Change in Bank Control Procedures HARYARITLOW LIBRANT 

Standard background checks must include contact with:

• Federal Bureau of Investigation (FBI) General Index. 

• United States Custom Service. 

• Drug Enforcement Administration (DEA). 

(ECIS) records search. ECIS includes a database of persons named in OCC administrative actions or criminal referrals. Persons involved in insider abuse or other situations indicating potential unsuitability for involvement in the affairs of a national bank are also included on 

ECIS. District Counsel should be consulted on accessing these systems. In addition, Enforcement and Compliance (E&C) will review various printouts provided by the other regulatory agencies, which list names of persons against whom enforcement actions have been taken and/or about whom criminal referrals were filed. Contact E&C to determine if a particular person appears on another agency's referral list. • Federal regulatory agencies that have regulatory jurisdiction over the institutions in which the acquiring party has been/is involved (the 

FRB, FDIC, and OTS). • State depository institution regulatory agencies in those cases where an acquiring party has been/is involved with institutions under a state's regulatory jurisdiction.

NOTE: The ECIS and LEXIS-NEXIS systems must be accessed to verify whether an acquiring party has any history of court cases, litigation, etc. 

Consult with District Counsel for access to these systems. Since many small newspapers are not on LEXIS-NEXIS, the OCC's librarian will, if requested by OCC personnel, check local newspapers for information on an acquiring party. If adverse or derogatory information is disclosed from any checked source, determine its significance to the overall filing, include it in the Documents-OCC, Investigating Notices, Summary 

Report of Investigation and Verification, Change in Bank Control and in the confidential memorandum.

NOTE: If, in individual circumstances, any of the standard background checks are not performed because the check would be redundant, does not serve the OCC's purpose, or for any other reason, the file must be documented adequately with the reasons for the waiver. 

In addition to the standard background checks, discretionary checks appropriate to the acquiring party's circumstances should be conducted. Such checks may include, but are not limited to, requests for background information from the Internal Revenue Service (IRS), Securities Exchange Commission (SEC), Commodity Futures Trading Commission (CFTC), National Association of Securities Dealers (NASD), INTERPOL, the International Monetary Fund (IMF), the World Bank, the U.S. Immigration and Naturalization Service, Central Intel ligence Agency (CIA), the U.S. State Department, and local law enforcement agencies. NOTE: All U.S. citizens and all foreign nationals are subject to six mandatory background checks. In addition, for foreign nationals, the 148

Change in Bank Control Procedures OCC will contact the OCC Enforcement and Compliance Division, Interpol, the State Department for a police and embassy check, the CIA, and bank regulators in the persons' home country or other countries where they borrow, own, operate, or manage banks. These checks also may be performed for U.S. citizens, but they are not mandatory. 

Mandatory checks may be waived, but any waivers must be documented in the case file and must include a brief statement of the basis for the waiver.

NOTE: All foreign nationals must include with their filing certified statements from local officials attesting to their integrity and financial capacity 

(see Applications and Notices, Standard Sample Document, Foreign 

Certifications). 

NOTE: U.S. district and state court clerks may be contacted regarding decisions at the trial level in those instances where it is deemed appropriate. Credit checks are performed on acquiring parties when deemed appropriate.

NOTE: The OCC may require additional information to make an independent determination of the accuracy and completeness of any information contained in a change in bank control notice. This may include a requirement to submit fingerprints, if they are required, to access information necessary for a background investigation. 

When fingerprints are required, the OCC will: • Distribute one fingerprint card to each acquiring party required to obtain fingerprints. • Instruct the person in writing that providing fingerprints is their responsibility and is required for the OCC to thoroughly investigate the acquiring party's background. • The OCC may conduct a personal interview or otherwise discuss with the individual adverse information disclosed during the investigation not previously clearly identified or disclosed in the application or by the applicant. • Provide a list of locations convenient to the acquiring party where fingerprinting services are available (i.e., the local police or law enforcement office or post offices). • Allow at least 10 business days for fingerprint cards to be returned to the DDA or DCMNB. 

The DDA or DCMNB will forward for processing completed fingerprint cards to BOS to the attention of the Director for Corporate Activity. 

NOTE: If adverse information is received from the FBI on a person applying for a controlling interest in a national bank, or seeking to be a director or executive officer in a national bank following a proposed change in control, the person is notified and afforded the opportunity to complete, correct, or challenge the information prior to the OCC making a decision (see Documents-OCC, FBI Adverse Information, Change in Bank Control). The person is instructed to refer to 28 CFR 16.34 for 

149

Change in Bank Control Procedures HARVARD LIBRANT 

guidance and to provide the OCC with a written response within 15 days of receipt of notification. 

If the adverse information is received from a source other than the FBI, the person must still be notified and afforded the opportunity to include additional facts germane to the issue in question. This information must be considered when making a decision. 

NOTE: A decision to disapprove based on information in the FBI's record cannot be made until the applicant has been permitted to respond to the information. 

Notice Review Public Comments 

13. Acknowledges all public comments.

NOTE: Contact Congressional Liaison in Washington whenever a written or telephone inquiry is received from a member of Congress or his or her representative. All congressional inquiries must be documented in the case file. 

14. Makes the public file available to all who inquire about the notice. Generally, the public file contains only the public comments filed. The public file also may include the Summary Fact Sheet, if release of it is not objected to by the acquiring party prior to decision (see Summary Fact Sheet). 

Reproduces the public file for the standard charge, if requested. 

Request for Additional Information and Extensions 

15. If, following the review for technical completeness, the OCC finds that additional information is required to determine whether it has no objection to a proposed change in bank control, the DDA/DCMNB informs the acquiring party as soon as possible of the additional information required (see Documents-OCC, Information Request, Change in Bank Control).

NOTE: This may occur as a result of the OCC's statutory obligation to determine the accuracy and completeness of information filed with the notice. The OCC may require the acquiring party to submit additional verifiable information regarding any data submitted with the notice. 

NOTE: Letters requesting additional information contain a date by which the OCC must receive the requested information. This date will be some time prior to the earliest date that the proposed change in control may be consummated. Receipt of the additional information on or before the indicated date provides the OCC with an opportunity to analyze the information within the 60-day statutory time period or within any extension of that period.

If the need for additional information is discovered early within the original 60-day time period, sufficient time may remain to allow the acquiring party to respond to the request and to provide the OCC with the opportunity to analyze the additional information. In such situations, the original 60-day time period is not extended. 150

Change in Bank Control Procedures 

Acquiring Party

DDA/DCMNB However, if that time is not long enough, the OCC may, in its discretion, extend the period during which it must make its decision by an additional 30 days. The OCC also can extend, under certain circumstances, the period for disapproval two additional times for 45 days each time. 

When the decision period is extended, the acquiring party is given a date by which the additional information must be received. This date will be some time prior to the end of the extended time period. 

16. Responds to request for additional information.

NOTE: Acquiring parties who fail to provide requested additional information on or before the date indicated may be subject to a disapproval of a proposed change in control. 

Acquiring parties who do not believe they can submit the additional information on or before the deadline date may request that the OCC suspend processing of the notice until the information is delivered. If processing is suspended, a new earliest consummation date will be determined. That date will be based on the number of days remaining between the date of suspension and the end of the original 60-day period or an extended time period. For example, suppose that on the day processing is suspended, 24 days remain within the original 60-day time period for the OCC to make a decision. In this case, the new earliest consummation date will be 24 days after the date of receipt of the requested information.

Generally, the notice will be held in suspension no longer than 120 days at which time the acquiring party must submit the information or the notice will be considered withdrawn by the OCC. The filing fee is not refunded. If the acquiring party wishes to pursue the change in control after the 120 days have lapsed, a new notice and filing fee are required. 

In some cases, the additional information required by the OCC may be so substantial that its submission would present the OCC with essentially a new change in bank control notice. In such situations, the OCC may require the acquiring party to file an Amended Notice of Change in Bank Control and restart the entire change in bank control notification process. Republication may be necessary and an additional filing fee may be required. 

17. Extends the review period, if necessary. If processing is suspended, notifies the acquiring party of the new earliest date for consummation following receipt of additional information (see Documents-OCC, Ear liest Consummation Date Letter, Change in Bank Control). 

18. Indicates the extension of the review period on LIS. 

19. Reviews the case and makes a recommendation based on the financial ability, competence, experience, and integrity of the acquiring party and other statutory factors. The recommendation must address all statutory factors and the results of investigative efforts. 

NOTE: If the acquiring party anticipates forming a bank holding company to carry the debt related to the proposed acquisition, request contingency plans for carrying the debt or disposing of the stock in the event that the holding company is not formed. Such contingency plans 1 1 

1 151

Change in Bank Control Procedures DCD/DCMNB DDA/DCMNB 

must include detailed cash flow projections for each of the acquiring parties if debt service concerns occur during the initial analysis of the notice.

NOTE: If disapproval is recommended on the grounds of incompetence or illegal or improper actions, those charges must be supported by sound evidence. Examples of such grounds include: • Mismanagement of financial institutions when the acquiring party had control or was a director or senior officer, or other decision making official. • Significant and improper benefit from insider transactions at financial institutions with which the official was previously associated. • Conviction of a felony. Discipline or censure or denial of the right to do business or practice a profession by a state or federal regulatory agency or license granting body. • Intentional or deliberate submission of inaccurate or misleading biographical and/or financial information. 

20. Prepares a confidential memorandum addressing all supervisory and statutory factors, investigative efforts and results, and all public comments. Includes the Investigation and Verification Checklist and Summary Report as part of the memo. Forwards the confidential memorandum to DCD/DCMNB. 

Decision 21. Makes a decision under delegated authority or decides to forward the case to BOS for review, if not delegated. 

22. If delegated, issues a letter of no objection to the acquiring party (see 

Documents-OCC, No Objection Letter, Change in Bank Control). For wards a copy of the no objection letter to the target bank. Notifies interested parties of decision (see Documents-OCC, Interested Par ties Application Disposition Letter, Standard Sample Document). Enters action code on LIS. Checks the Weekly Bulletin for accuracy and completeness and contacts LP&S technicians via EMC2/TAO to report

Weekly Bulletin omissions or corrections. Forwards the official file to 

BOS for those cases that cannot be handled under delegated authority and enters the appropriate action code on LIS. 

NOTE: A copy of any notice to a member of Congress must be sent to the OCC's Congressional Liaison in Washington on or before it is mailed to Congress. If, prior to a decision, a congressional inquiry or advisement of a congressional inquiry had been received by telephone, the district informs the Congressional Liaison of the decision, as soon as it is made.

NOTE: Those cases forwarded to BOS with a recommendation must be sent at least 30 days prior to the end of the 60-day review period or any extension thereof. The district office or DCMNB should alert BOS 

promptly by telephone of forwarded cases.

Comptroller for  notifies Washington personnel in other affected federal agencies (see PPM 6100-3 (Rev.), January 22, 1986). 

24. Makes a decision and returns the official file to BOS. 

NOTE: If decision is to disapprove, forwards draft transmittal letter to 

Litigation for review prior to sending to applicant. 

25. Notifies applicant of the decision by telephone, FAX and/or by letter of no objection or of disapproval on or before the expiration of the notice review period. Sends letter with return receipt requested within three days of decision (see Documents-OCC, No Objection Letter or Disap proval Letter, Change in Bank Control). Forwards a copy of letter to the target bank. Notifies interested parties of decision (see Interested 

Parties Application Disposition Letter). Enters action code on LIS. 

Checks the Weekly Bulletin for accuracy and completeness and contacts the LP&S technicians via EMC2/TAO to report Weekly Bulletin omissions or corrections. 

NOTE: If a decision is not made until the last two or three days of the review period, the acquiring party is notified by telephone in addition to the letter. If the decision is to disapprove, a telegram or FAX also is sent.

NOTE: A copy of any notice to a member of Congress must be sent to the OCC's Congressional Liaison in Washington on or before the date it is mailed to Congress. If, prior to a decision, a congressional inquiry or advisement of a congressional inquiry had been received by telephone, BOS informs the Congressional Liaison of the decision, as soon as it is made.

NOTE: After BOS makes LIS entries on Washington decided cases, the official file is returned to the District or DCMNB if additional processing is required. 

26. Sends a copy of the confidential memorandum, decision, and notification letter for each disapproval promptly to the LP&S quality control coordinator after decision.

Failure to File Notice 

27. Files the notice after consummation of the change in control.

NOTE: If a prior notice is not filed pursuant to the Change in Bank Control 

Act until after the change in control has been consummated, the acquiring party may have violated the CBCA and be subject to civil money penalties. District Counsel will evaluate the information and determine the appropriateness of civil money penalties. When a tardy notice is received and determined to be technically complete, a letter 1 153

Change in Bank Control Procedures DDA/DCMNB DDA/DCMNB/BOS 

Acquiring Party

DDA/DCMNB 

will be sent by the OCC (see Documents-OCC, Technically Complete 

Notice, Change in Bank Control). 

28. Accepts tardy notice filings, enters information into LIS and reviews the filing for technical completeness. If notice is technically complete, notifies controlling party by letter of that fact, of the publication requirement, and that shares in excess of 9.9 percent may not be voted until the acquiring party is notified of a decision by the OCC. 

29. Notifies the LIS hotline of tardy filing's technically complete date for the addendum section of the Weekly Bulletin. Checks the Weekly Bulletin for accuracy and completeness and contacts LP&S technicians via 

EMC2/TAO to report Weekly Bulletin omissions or corrections. 

30. Requests District Counsel to determine whether civil money penalties should be pursued (refer to PPM 5000-7 (Rev.) April 8, 1991). 

31. Performs the same review, investigation, and verification procedures detailed in steps 12 through 20. Tardy notices may be processed under delegated authority. However, the confidential memorandum should include an evaluation by the District's Legal Counsel as to the appropriateness of civil money penalties and whether they will be pursued. 

32. If the decision is "no objection" to the change in control, sends a letter to the acquiring party reporting the decision. The letter should state that a referral was made to District Counsel regarding civil money penalties and, when possible, District Counsel's decision (see No Objection Letter). 

NOTE: If the OCC disapproves the change in control, BOS will coordinate with Litigation and Enforcement and Compliance for the necessary notification and divestiture. 

33. Makes LIS entries regarding decision. Checks the Weekly Bulletin for accuracy and completeness and contacts LP&S technicians via

EMC2/TAO to report Weekly Bulletin omissions or corrections. 

Close Out 

34. Sends notice of the consummation to the DCD/DCMNB in writing within 

10 days of consummation. 

35. If notification of consummation is not received within 90 days of the date of no objection to the notice, contacts the acquiring party by telephone, or by letter, to determine whether the acquisition has been consummated. If contact is by telephone, documents the file on the content of the telephone conversation.

NOTE: All proposed changes in bank control must be consummated within six months from the decision date. Significant changes in an acquiring party's financial condition or competence and integrity over an extended period of time may warrant review by the OCC. If the six-month period lapses, the district or Multinational Banking will inform the acquiring party and the bank by letter that the time limit has expired and that the appropriate file is closed. If an acquiring party wishes to

37. Forwards the official file to Central Records (CR).

Procedures for

Change in Bank Control  

 Advisory Opinions 1. Reviews the request for comments from the Federal Deposit Insurance 

Corporation (FDIC), the Federal Reserve Board (FRB), Office of Thrift Supervision (OTS) or State agency. Makes entries into the Licensing 

Information System (LIS). 

NOTE: Responses to requests should be sent to the appropriate agency within the time period requested by the agency (generally, within 30 days of receipt for routine requests and 10 days of receipt if the OCC is informed that an emergency exists). 

2. Scans the financial and biographical information submitted for relationships and/or banking experience with a national bank. If the acquiring party indicates current or previous association with a national bank, checks the Supervisory Monitoring System (SMS) comments for the applicable bank(s).

NOTE: If the acquiring party has not been associated with a national bank within the last five years or reports no association with a national bank, checks the Enforcement and Compliance Information System (ECIS). ECIS is checked to ensure that negative involvement with a national bank is not overlooked or omitted from the notice. At the district's or Multinational Banking's (MNB) discretion, ECIS may be routinely checked for all Change in Bank Control Act (CBCA) advisory opinion requests. 

3. Requests comments from other areas (i.e., field office, Bank Supervision, District Counsel, Duty Station) as deemed necessary.

NOTE: District Special Supervision must be contacted for comments regarding persons associated with banks under their supervision. Special Supervision also must be contacted when a person reports no recent national bank association and is not known by the analysis division staff. 

4. Prepares the confidential memorandum and comment letter, if applicable, and forwards the file to the Deputy Comptroller (District) (DCD) or the Deputy Comptroller for Multinational Banking (DCMNB) for action 

(see Documents-OCC, Advisory Opinion Memorandum, Change in 

Bank Control).

NOTE: If there are no comments, or the acquiring party is well-known to the OCC and nothing adverse will be conveyed to the requesting agency, prepares a brief advisory opinion memorandum supporting the decision and marks the agency's letter requesting comments as follows: 

OCC-No Comments LADVART 

Deputy Comptroller

NOTE: If adverse comments are made, completes the advisory opinion memorandum addressing all relevant factors, drafts the advisory opinion letter and forwards information to the DCD/DCMNB for action. 

5. If the case is delegated, acts under delegated authority and forwards comments or marked original letter to the requesting agency. 156

Change in Location Introduction Applicability 

The procedures in this section generally apply to national banks that seek a change in location of a head office or a domestic (brick and mortar) branch, or a head office to an existing branch site. They cover all changes in location including, minimal relocations, such as a move of a national bank office to a location across the street. Applicants that desire to retain a former head office as a branch after its relocation must file a separate application in accordance with the requirements and procedures for establishing domestic branches and seasonal agencies (see Domestic 

Branches, Seasonal Agencies, and CBCT Branches section). These procedures cannot be used to request approval to relocate a CBCT (Customer-Bank Communication Terminal) branch. To request such approval, an application to establish a CBCT must be used and the procedures for a branch closing for the 

CBCT at the old site followed. Background

Previous OCC procedures for approval to relocate a domestic branch or head office required a bank to submit a lengthy application. In 1982, the OCC changed its application procedures to the shorter form, included in this section. 

Summary of the Process 

For changes in location of a head office or a domestic branch, a bank must submit an application with the appropriate fee to the district office in the community in which it is located. Multinational banks or their affiliates must submit applications to the Multinational Banking 

Division in Washington. 

On the date the application is submitted, a public notice of the application must be published in a newspaper of general circulation in the community in which the applicant proposes to locate. Applicants that desire to relocate a head office and retain the prior head office as a branch must submit a separate application to establish a branch.

After an application is filed, it is reviewed to ensure technical completeness. If an application is incomplete, it will be returned for correction, or the applicant will be informed of the required corrections by letter or by telephone if they are minor. The applicant is afforded one opportunity to correct technical deficiencies. 

Failure to correct deficiencies upon resubmission may cause the application to be considered withdrawn. 

Filing fees will not be refunded. An application is technically complete when all questions have been answered and required information submitted. Technical completeness does not imply that the content will be found acceptable or that the application will be approved. In this context, "technical deficiencies" means information that was omitted in the original submission.

The application is analyzed for compliance with state and federal laws and, after the required 30-day public comment period, a decision is made to approve, conditionally approve, or deny the application. If approved, the change in location must occur within 18 months. 

Extensions generally are not granted. Applicants must notify the district office of the effective date of the change in location at least 10 days prior to the planned effective date. Any conditions of the approval must have been met prior to final OCC authorization of the relocation.

Relocation of a head office to an existing branch site within city limits requires that the bank submit only a letter of notification. If a national bank desires to relocate its head office to a site other than an authorized branch location within the city limits, it must file an application. A national bank that desires to relocate its head office to a location outside the city limits must file an application and attain approval of its shareholders owning two-thirds of the stock. The head office cannot be relocated more than 30 miles beyond the city, town, or village in which the bank is located. 

Key Policies 

The OCC must approve applications filed under 12 CFR 5.40, provided that approval is consistent with applicable law.

As provided in 12 CFR 5. 13, the OCC reserves the right to deny applications or to grant approval subject to fulfillment of certain conditions, if:

There are significant supervisory concerns about the applicant or any affiliated organizations as defined by 12 U.S.C. 221a. • The applicant's record of helping to meet the credit needs of its entire community, including low- and moderate-income neighborhoods, consistent with the safe and sound operation of the bank, is less than satisfactory. • Any financial or other business arrangement, direct or indirect, involving the proposed branch or head office and bank insiders (directors, officers, employees, and shareholders owning or controlling, directly or indirectly, 10 percent or more of any class of the subject bank's voting stock), includes terms and conditions more favorable to the insiders than 

HADVADODAR 

158

Change in Location Introduction would be available in a comparable transaction with unrelated parties.

Generally Applicable Procedures Additional Information 

The application must state accurately and fully the facts the OCC needs to determine that a proposed branch/ head office relocation is consistent with law and applicable regulations. The OCC may require additional data at any time during the processing of the application. When additional information is requested, the decision generally will be delayed. If an inadequate response is received to requests for additional information, the application may be considered withdrawn or disapproved.

Publication Requirements 

Pursuant to 12 CFR 5.8, notice of the application must be published in a newspaper of general circulation in the community in which the applicant proposes to engage in business. The notice must state that an application is being filed to relocate a branch or head office and must include the name of the bank and the exact location of the proposed site. Fees

A filing fee must accompany the application in accordance with 12 CFR 5.5 and the current Notice of the 

Comptroller of the Currency Fees. Filing fees are not refunded.

State Law Considerations 

Cites of state law pertaining to branch and head office location and bank certification that the proposal complies with state law, including any geographic limitations and applicable qualitative factors, are required as part of the application. 

Investment in Bank Premises Limits

The relocation or the addition of a branch or new head office must not cause the bank to exceed the limits for investment in bank premises (12 U.S.C. 371d and 12 

CFR 7.3100). If the branch would increase the outstanding amount above the prescribed limit, the application should either request an approval for an increase in investment in bank premises or address the ways it will correct the problem.

The approval of the Deputy Comptroller (District) or the Deputy Comptroller for Multinational Banking for multinational companies or their affiliates must be obtained before the consummation of any plans under which the bank's investment in bank premises will be increased to an amount exceeding its capital stock. The Deputy 

Comptroller (District) or the Deputy Comptroller for Multinational Banking ordinarily will approve an investment in fixed assets aggregating an amount up to 50 percent of capital stock, surplus and undivided profits when a reasonable need for such investment can be shown.

Community Reinvestment Act (CRA) 

If the proposed relocation will change the bank's existing community delineation as designated in its CRA statement, the applicant's board of directors must act upon the change at its first regular meeting after the relocation is effected. In the application, the bank must indicate the ways it intends to vary its lending policy, procedures or services at the proposed relocated office, if at all. In considering an application for relocation, the OCC will consider the national bank's CRA record of performance.

For any market area analysis that may be required by state law, the bank's community as delineated in its 

CRA statement should be used.

Change of Corporate Title Introduction 

7 1 a Background

Section 405 of the Garn-St Germain Act (Pub. L. 97320) amended 12 USC 30 by deleting the requirement that the OCC approve any change in a bank's corporate title. As a result, the OCC no longer reviews a proposed name for similarity to those of other depository institutions, approves bank titles, or issues title certificates. A bank also is no longer required to publish in a newspaper the change in its title. 

Key Policies 

Any national bank may change its title (name) provided it amends article first of the articles of association and submits written notice to the appropriate district office. 

The new title must include the entire word "National" (refer to 12 USC 30). Abbreviations of the word "National," such as "N.A." are not acceptable for an official title. 

However, the choice of words in a title for unofficial use, such as advertising, is strictly a bank management decision.

A national bank that wants to protect its proprietary interest in its corporate title may wish to consult legal 

 counsel to determine whether protection is available under state or federal law. Furthermore, a bank may wish to seek legal counsel prior to a title change to determine whether a proposed title could be challenged.

"L il, 169

Introduction Applicability 

Generally OCC must approve any change in a bank's equity capital accounts. 

Changes Requiring Prior OCC Approval 

OCC reviews and approves certain changes in capital before they are made. The bank first requests and receives OCC approval of those changes. It then notifies the OCC when the change is made and that it complies with legal requirements. Upon receipt of the notification, the OCC certifies or authorizes the change, as appropriate, provided it is legal. The change is effective upon OCC certification or authorization. Changes include: 

• Sale of common stock for consideration other than cash. • Issuance of preferred stock. • Reduction in capital stock, i.e., common or preferred stock, or surplus accounts through a distribution of cash or assets. • Reduction in the capital stock or surplus accounts through a transfer to undivided profits. • Property dividends (dividends-in-kind). • Dividends that exceed amounts payable under 12 

U.S.C. 60 without prior OCC approval.

Changes Requiring Only OCC Certification or Authorization 

OCC always will approve certain changes in capital provided they comply with legal requirements. Those changes do not require prior OCC approval. The bank notifies the OCC of the change and certifies that it complies with legal requirements. If it does so, the OCC certifies or authorizes the change upon receipt of the notification, making it effective. Those changes include: • Sale of common stock for cash. • Conversion of preferred stock to common stock. 

Payment of stock dividends. Restructuring to change the par value of capital stock where the change in the capital stock account is offset by an equal change in the surplus account. 

Changes Not Requiring OCC Approval, Certification, or Authorization 

Certain changes in capital do not require OCC approval, although they are subject to other legal requirements, including shareholder approval requirements. 

Those changes are effective upon shareholder approval of the change and include: • A change in the amount of authorized, but unissued capital stock. • A stock split that does not change the amount in the common stock account.

Requirements for Common Changes in Equity Capital 

Sale of Common Stock for Cash 

Shareholders must approve, by a 2/3 vote, any increase in authorized, but unissued common shares. Upon sale of the shares, the bank must notify the OCC and certify that the change in capital meets legal requirements. 

Upon receipt of the notification, the OCC certifies the increase in capital, provided it is legal. 

Sale of Capital Stock for Property 

Shareholders must approve any necessary increase in authorized, but unissued shares. (An increase in authorized common shares requires a 2/3 vote; an increase in authorized preferred shares requires a majority vote.) The bank must submit to the OCC a letter of intent to change equity capital and receive OCC approval prior to making the change. Upon sale of the stock, the bank must notify the OCC and certify that the change in capital meets legal requirements. Upon receipt of the notification, the OCC will certify the increase in capital, provided it is legal. 

Sale of Preferred Stock 

Shareholders must approve, by a majority vote, any necessary increase in authorized, but unissued shares. 

Because the articles of association should define the rights, privileges, and the authorized amount of the issue, the OCC recommends that banks apply for OCC approval prior to the shareholder vote. Prior OCC approval enables a bank to make any modifications needed to qualify the issue as capital before the shareholder vote. The bank must submit to the OCC a letter of intent to change equity capital and must receive its approval prior to making the change. OCC approval will indicate whether the issue qualifies as Tier 1 or Tier 2 capital under OCC capital guidelines. Upon sale of the stock, the bank must notify the OCC and certify that the change in capital meets legal requirements. Upon receipt of the notification, the OCC certifies the increase in capital, provided it is legal. 

Reduction in Capital Stock or Surplus by Distributing 

Cash or Assets

Shareholders must approve, by a 2/3 vote, any reduction in capital stock under 12 U.S.C. 59. Shareholders also must approve, by a 2/3 vote, any related distribution of cash or assets under 12 U.S.C. 59. The bank must submit to the OCC a letter of intent to change 170

Introduction equity capital and receive its approval prior to reducing capital stock or surplus. Upon completion of the reduction, the bank must notify the OCC and certify that the change in capital meets legal requirements. Upon receipt of the notification, the OCC authorizes the reduction in capital, provided it is legal. 

Reduction in Capital Stock or Surplus by Transfer to Undivided Profits 

This category includes the following transactions. 

Quasi-reorganization-A quasi-reorganization allows a bank to restructure its capital accounts without legal reorganization by transferring amounts from capital stock and surplus to the undivided profits account to remove a deficit. It is often used when new owners purchase a troubled bank, infuse new capital, add new management, correct problems, and return the bank to profitability. 

Without a quasi-reorganization, payment of dividends to the new owners is prohibited, until earnings have completely eliminated the prior deficit in undivided profits. 

Upon OCC approval, a quasi-reorganization is recognized as a recapitalization under 12 U.S.C. 56. It allows new owners to pay dividends from future earnings without regard to the prior deficit in undivided profits. 

A quasi-reorganization is available to only those banks that meet criteria stated in OCC Banking Circular 236.

Shareholders must approve the quasi-reorganization and any related reduction in the capital stock account by a 2/3 vote. The bank must submit to the OCC a letter of intent to change equity capital and receive its approval prior to the change. The letter must describe how the bank meets legal, accounting, and regulatory requirements in Banking Circular 236. If the OCC determines that a quasi-reorganization is warranted, it will authorize the capital reduction. The bank must notify the OCC of completion of the change and certify that it meets legal requirements. 

Transfers from "surplus surplus"-A bank that exceeds the capital requirements of 12 U.S.C. 60(a) by having "surplus surplus" can transfer that amount from surplus to undivided profits for the payment of dividends. The bank can transfer that surplus, only if it is earned, i.e., if it came from earnings of prior periods and not from paid in surplus or transfers to record a stock dividend. 

Shareholder approval is not required for this change. 

The bank's board of directors must approve the transfer. The bank must submit a letter of intent to the OCC and receive its approval prior to making the change. Dividends-in-Kind

In accordance with 12 CFR 7.6120, a national bank may declare dividends payable in property with OCC approval. Before the dividend is declared, the property must be written up or down to reflect its current market value. Any write-up or write-down should be recorded in the income statement as if the property had been disposed of in a sale. The dividend should be recorded at an amount equal to the current value of the property. 

The bank must request and receive OCC approval prior to payment of the dividend.

Dividends Requiring Approval under 12 U.S.C. 60 

OCC approval is required if the total of all dividends declared by a bank in any calendar year exceeds net profits of that year plus retained net profits of the preceding two years, less any required transfers to surplus or a fund for the retirement of preferred stock. 

The bank must request and receive OCC approval prior to payment of the dividend. 

Stock Split 

A stock split typically increases the number of shares of stock outstanding and reduces the market price of each. The usual stock split involves reducing the par value of the stock while increasing the number of shares issued. For example, 1,000,000 shares of $10 par value stock might split 2 for 1 which would result in 2,000,000 shares of $5 par value stock. Shareholders must approve an amendment to the articles of association to change the par value of the stock. No OCC approval is required, however, since the amount in the common stock account does not change.

A stock split can also be effected by declaring a stock dividend. Stock dividends exceeding 20 to 25 percent of issued shares are classed as stock splits and accounted for by transferring to permanent capitalization an amount not less than the par value of the additional shares issued. Such dividends are considered a realignment of capital accounts according to generally accepted accounting principles and are not subject to the restrictions of 12 U.S.C. 60. Shareholders must approve any increase in authorized, but unissued shares. Upon payment of the dividend, the bank must notify the OCC and certify that the change meets legal requirements. Upon receipt of the notification, OCC certifies the increase in capital, provided it is legal. 

Reverse Stock Split 

OCC policy does not permit a reverse stock split that has the effect of eliminating minority shareholders. 

Stock Dividends 

A stock dividend is an issuance of shares of stock that evidence current owners' share of accumulated earnings. Stock dividends are accounted for by transferring 10 1 171

Introduction an amount equal to the fair value of the additional shares issued from undivided profits to a category of permanent capitalization (common stock and surplus). 

The amount transferred from undivided profits shall not be less than the par value of the additional shares being issued.

Fractional shares created by stock dividends fall under the purview of 12 CFR 7.6040.

Shareholders must approve any necessary increase in authorized, but unissued shares. Upon payment of the dividend, the bank must notify the OCC and certify that the change meets legal requirements. Upon receipt of the notification, OCC certifies the increase in capital, provided it is legal. 

Changes in Par Value of Capital Stock 

Shareholders must approve, by a majority vote, an amendment to the articles of association to change the par value of capital stock. Shareholders must also approve any reduction in capital stock by a 2/3 vote under 12U.S.C. 59. Changes that are strictly an internal restructuring of capital do not require prior OCC approval. (An internal restructuring of capital means changes in par value of capital stock when the change in the capital stock account is offset by an equal change in the surplus account.) Upon completion of the change, the bank must notify the OCC and certify that the change meets legal requirements. Upon receipt of the notification, OCC certifies the change, if it results in an increase in the capital stock account or authorizes it if a decrease in the capital stock account results and it is legal. 

Key Policies

The OCC generally will approve changes in capital that comply with law and regulation upon receipt of the required information from the banks. That information gives advance notice of the type and amount of the proposed change. For banks in satisfactory condition, approval generally will be granted unless the proposed capital structure is not considered adequate under OCC policies. For banks in unsatisfactory condition, the change in capital may be denied or approved conditionally at the OCC's discretion for reasons of bank safety and soundness and any other supervisory concerns. Specifically, approval of a change in capital may be withheld for banks that: • Fail to comply with a capital plan submitted to the OCC. • Propose a capital structure that the OCC considers inadequate. • Violate laws or regulations. • Exhibit conditions that threaten safety and soundness. • Fail to provide adequate information.

Summary of the Process 

For those changes requiring prior OCC approval, the bank should submit a letter of intent to change equity capital. The letter describes the proposed change in capital and explains the reason for it. The OCC will review the change to determine that: • It conforms to the terms of any capital adequacy agreement. • Proposed issues can be considered capital under the definitions adopted by the OCC. • The change complies with laws, regulations, and supervisory concerns.

A bank may consider the proposed change approved 30 days after the OCC receives the letter of intent, unless it is notified by the OCC that approval is delayed, conditioned, or denied. If the OCC agrees with the change, it will approve the transaction and advise the applicant of any additional steps, including a letter of notification, required to effect the change. Upon completion of any capital change requiring OCC certification or authorization, the bank should notify the OCC by letter that the change has been made and that it complies with legal requirements. A bank may consider the change certified or authorized seven days after the OCC receives the notification, unless otherwise advised. The OCC will certify or authorize those changes in capital upon receipt of the letter, provided the changes are legal. 

Generally Applicable Procedures

Securities Disclosure Requirements 

For all changes in capital, a bank must satisfy securities disclosure requirements. It must obtain any necessary shareholders' approval for the change in capital, and when registered pursuant to 12 CFR 11, submit proxy materials to OCC Securities and Corporate Practices for review and clearance. Before beginning the sale of capital stock, it must prepare and file any proposed offering documents with OCC Securities and Corporate Practices for review, comment, and clearance. It must also file any other documents required under 12 CFR 16 before beginning the sale of capital stock. Fees

Fees applicable to changes in equity capital are published by the OCC annually in a Banking Bulletin, 172

Introduction entitled Notice of Comptroller of the Currency Fees. 

Fees generally are not refunded.

Completion of Changes 

Approved changes in capital generally must be completed within one year of preliminary approval. Generally, the OCC does not extend this period.

Citizenship Waiver Request Introduction Summary of the Process 

Qualification of Directors, 12 U.S.C. 72, requires that every director be a citizen of the United States for his or her entire term of service, except that for a subsidiary or affiliate of a foreign bank, the Comptroller of the Currency (OCC) may waive the requirement of citizenship for no more than a minority of the total number of directors.

Bank requests for a waiver of the citizenship requirement must be submitted to the OCC in writing. The request must contain: • An attestation that the subject bank is an affiliate or subsidiary of a foreign bank, and that the OCC has not waived the citizenship requirement for more than a minority of the subject bank's total number of directors. • Biographical information on the proposed director. 

The OCC reviews the request to determine that: • All required information has been provided. • A waiver would not violate applicable laws, rulings, and regulations. • The reputation and integrity of the proposed director would not threaten the stability of the bank or prejudice the interest of the depositors. 

Depending on the country of citizenship, OCC reviews generally last from 60 to 90 days. If the OCC becomes aware of material misrepresentations, fraudulent activities, or questionable business or bank activities by individual(s), the request will be denied. 

Sometimes if, following the granting of a citizenship waiver, a change in bank control causes a national bank to no longer qualify as a "subsidiary" or "affiliate" of a foreign bank, all non-U.S. citizen directors will be required to relinquish their seats on the bank's board of directors. Background

Previous OCC procedures for processing and review ing citizenship waiver requests provided minimal guidance on processing requirements and decision criteria. The revised procedures describe in detail the filing and processing procedures.

Applicability 

These procedures apply when a bank wishes to elect or appoint one or more non-United States citizens to its board of directors. In some cases a separate notification may be required pursuant to Section 914 of the 

Financial Institution Reform, Recovery, and Enforcement Act of 1989 (FIRREA). 

Definitions 

Affiliate, as stipulated in 12 U.S.C. 221, includes any corporation, business trust, association, or other similar organization— (1) of which a member bank, directly or indirectly, owns or controls either a majority of the voting shares or more than 50 percent of the number of shares voted for the election of its directors, trustees, or other persons exercising similar functions at the preceding election, or controls in any manner the election of a majority of its directors, trustees, or other persons exercising similar functions; or (2) of which control is held, directly or indirectly, through stock ownership or in any other manner, by the shareholders of a member bank who own or control either a majority of the shares of such bank or more than 50 percent of the number of shares voted for the election of directors of such bank at the preceding election, or by trustees for the benefit of the shareholders of any such bank; or (3) of which a majority of its directors, trustees, or other persons exercising similar functions are directors of any one member bank; or (4) which owns or controls, directly or indirectly, either a majority of the shares of capital stock of a member bank or more than 50 percent of the number of shares voted for the election, or controls in any manner the election of a majority of the directors of a member bank, or for the benefit of whose shareholders or members all or substantially all of the capital stock of a member bank is held by trustee. 

Subsidiary as defined under 12 U.S.C. 1841(d) is— (1) any company 25 percent or more of whose voting shares (exclusive of shares owned by the United States or by any company wholly owned by the United States) is directly or indirectly owned or controlled by such [foreign bank], or is held by it with power to vote; (2) any company the election of a majority of whose directors is controlled in any manner by such [foreign bank]; or (3) any company with respect to the management of policies of which such bank holding company has the power, directly or indirectly, to exercise a controlling influence, as determined by the Board, after notice and opportunity for hearing. 179

Citizenship Waiver Request Introduction 

Foreign bank refers to a corporation, business trusts, association, or other similar organization organized to carry on the business of banking outside the jurisdiction of the United States.

Conversion Introduction Summary of Process 

An existing bank that wishes to convert to a national bank submits to the Deputy Comptroller (District) in its district, or to the Deputy Comptroller for Multinational Banking if it is an affiliate of a multinational bank, a letter of intent. The letter contains appropriate information on the bank's status and condition, including a legal opinion, reports of condition and income, and audited financial statements. The OCC normally will conduct a pre-conversion field investigation of the bank. The information obtained in that investigation may be shared with other regulators. If problems of safety, soundness, or ownership exist, the conversion may be denied. If the bank's condition, management, and community service are satisfactory, the OCC will generally grant preliminary approval and notify the bank of conversion requirements and procedures.

This section includes procedures also for converting a thrift institution to a national bank. Generally, OCC procedures and areas of consideration for thrift conversions parallel those for an existing bank that wishes to convert to a national bank. However, certain additional information is normally required, so that issues arising from differences in laws and regulations for thrifts and banks may be addressed in the OCC's decision. The 

OCC will not give final approval to some thrift conversions until holding company approval has been given by Federal Reserve Board (FRB). 

Termination of status as a national banking association is also covered in this section. The termination process involves filing a letter of intent and compliance with appropriate legal requirements. Background

The OCC has determined that simplified procedures are appropriate when an existing bank wishes to convert to a national bank. Therefore, a letter of intent has replaced the formal application. Information supplied by the bank and reports of supervisory activity form the basis for the OCC's decision. Definitions

A bank incorporated by special law of any state or of the United States or organized under the general laws of any state or of the United States may convert into a national banking association. The material discussed in this subsection for "banks" also generally applies to thrift institutions. A thrift institution, for the purposes of these procedures, includes a state savings bank, a state savings association, a federal savings association, or a federal savings bank. Mutual thrifts must convert to stock thrifts before they may convert to a national bank. 

State bank, is defined further by 12 U.S.C. 35 as any bank, banking association, trust company, savings bank, or other banking institution engaged in the business of receiving deposits and incorporated under the laws of any state, any territory of the United States, Puerto Rico, or the Virgin Islands, or operating under the Code of Law for the District of Columbia (except a national banking association).

Key Policies 

Existing Bank to National Bank Conversion An existing bank may convert to a national bank with OCC approval. Approval will be granted on the basis of sound condition and management and on the satisfaction of statutory requirements relating to a conversion. A converting bank may retain authorized branches as a national bank, if such retention is consistent with state law. It may retain statutory and operating subsidiaries, if such retention is determined by the 

OCC to be part of or incidental to banking. Thrift branches that would be illegal under state banking laws may not be retained. A conversion may be prohibited if a bank's condition poses undue supervisory concern or if the conversion is used to escape supervisory action by the current regulator. 

[For thrift conversions] The Internal Revenue Service issued Revenue Ruling 90-54 in July, 1990, stating that a financial institution which is chartered as a bank cannot qualify as a savings and loan via the qualifying asset test. The OCC's review of the ruling and the governing statute indicates that any institution which previously met the definition of "domestic building and loan association," for tax purposes, but subsequently is considered to be a "bank" according to the Revenue Ruling, would be taxed immediately for taking an excess bad debt deduction that would not be deductible for a bank. Any excess deductions would be considered taxable income, and the resulting tax payment would be a charge to current earnings. The converting thrift must provide information on the effect on the institution following conversion to a national bank.

All directors must comply with the requirements set forth in 12 U.S.C. 72. Procedures in the Citizenship

Waiver Request section of this manual must be followed to request a citizenship waiver.

A national bank is required to obtain specific prior OCC approval to exercise fiduciary powers. This requirement applies uniformly to all converting institutions regardless of whether they currently exercise trust powers. If approval to exercise fiduciary powers is requested, the converting institution must also comply 186

Conversion Introduction with the procedures in 12 CFR 5.26 and the Fiduciary

Powers section of this manual. The following should be included in the letter of intent to convert for those institutions wishing to exercise fiduciary powers: • A statement that full or limited powers are requested (specify powers). • An opinion of counsel that the proposed activities do not violate state, federal or local law (include citations of applicable law). • A statement that the capital and surplus of the converting institution is the same or exceeds the capital and surplus required by state banks or thrifts, trust companies, and other corporations exercising fiduciary powers. • Sufficient biographical information on existing or proposed trust management to enable the OCC to assess their qualifications.

Charter conversions of BIF-insured and SAIF-insured institutions (e.g., savings associations) generally follow the same policies and similar procedures to convert to a national banking association. 

Generally Applicable Procedures 

Additional Information

A letter of intent is reviewed to ensure that it is technically complete, that is, when all questions have been answered and all required information submitted. If it is incomplete or the appropriate filing fee is not included, the letter will be returned for corrections or the applicant informed of the required corrections. If the letter needs only minor corrections, the applicant may be contacted by telephone. In general, the applicant will be given only one opportunity to make corrections. If corrections are not made, the letter of intent may be considered withdrawn or disapproved. In that case, the associated filing fees will not be refunded. 

Technical completeness does not imply that the content will be found acceptable or that the proposal will be approved. 

State and Federal Law Considerations

A state bank or a thrift institution desiring to convert to a national banking association must include with the letter of intent an opinion from counsel that the proposal is not in contravention of applicable state or federal laws. Supporting documentation should also be submitted. Counsel should indicate that any prefiling action required by applicable law has been taken and any further required action will occur prior to consummation of the proposal. 

Field Investigation

A pre-conversion field investigation of the converting institution may be scheduled by the OCC to ascertain an applicant's qualifications to convert to a national banking association. A separate fee will be assessed for the pre-conversion field investigation. 

Shareholder Approval Shareholders owning no less than 51 percent of the institution's capital stock must approve the proposed conversion. However, if the laws of the state in which the converting institution is organized require more than 

51 percent affirmative vote of the shareholders, the converting bank must comply with state law. Federal thrifts must comply with applicable federal law. 

Publication Requirements 

Publication requirements under 12 CFR 5.8 normally do not apply to conversions. However, pursuant to 12 CFR 

5.24(b), the OCC may require publication if, in its judgment, a proposed conversion would result in a policy change or raise issues important to the public or the banking community.

Capital 

Capital requirements under 12 U.S.C. 36 provide that the aggregate capital of every national bank and its branches shall equal the capital and surplus required of state chartered institutions to establish branches. 

In addition, national banks are subject to other statutory and regulatory minimum capital requirements. The minimum capital required pursuant to 12 U.S.C. 51 is determined by the population of the cities or other places where the bank has offices. A bank must have at least $50,000 in capital stock for each city in which it has an office(s) and the population does not exceed 6,000; at least $100,000 in capital stock for each city in which it has an office(s) and the population is between 6,001 and 50,000; and at least $200,000 in capital stock for each city in which it has an office(s) and the population is greater than 50,000. In addition, the bank must have surplus at least equal to 20 percent of capital stock.

The applicant should also refer to 12 CFR 3, Minimum Capital Ratios, for the required minimal acceptable capital ratios for national banks. 

Branch Authorization 

Branch authorization letters will be issued when appropriate to cover the existing branch offices (brick and mortar branches and CBCTs) of the converting institu- 187

Conversion Introduction tion upon consummation of the transaction. Approved, but unopened, branches must be authorized to open in accordance with OCC policy (12 CFR 5.30 and 5.31).

However, to receive a branch certificate number, the institution must notify the OCC 10 days before any such branch is opened. The OCC will not authorize the opening of an approved branch, if it is not opened within 18 months after the date of its original approval. 

Extension Policy 

Generally, a conversion must be completed within six months after the date of OCC approval. Requests for extensions of the six-month period generally will be denied unless they are required by factors beyond the control of the applicant. Decisions on those requests will be processed on a case-by-case basis. Fees

Filing fee(s) must accompany the letter of intent and associated applications. An additional fee is assessed for an OCC pre-conversion field investigation in accordance with 12 CFR 8.6 and 12 CFR 5.5(c) to recover the costs of the activities. The bill for any OCC pre-conversion field investigation will be forwarded to the applicant with the letter informing the applicant of the OCC's decision on the proposed conversion. 

National to State Bank Conversions 

A national bank that desires to convert to a state bank will be given instructions on how to terminate its status as a national bank upon receipt by the OCC of the bank's letter of intent to take such action. No approval by the OCC is required.

Conversion to a National Bank Pre-filing Meeting 

1.

Refers an institution that requests instructions to this section of the 

Comptroller's Manual for Corporate Activities. 

2. Provides additional information to the institution about the pre-filing meeting, if necessary, and informs the institution of what must be filed. 

3. Meets with the applicant to review the requirements and procedures for converting to a national bank, if necessary. The pre-filing meeting is held at the discretion of the District Director for Analysis (DDA) or the Deputy Comptroller for Multinational Banking (DCMNB). However, if the DDA or 

DCMNB waives the pre-filing meeting, the case file should note the reason.

NOTE: The applicant should be advised at the pre-filing meeting that the OCC will review the converting institution's written policies and procedures to determine that they meet or exceed those described in the OCC's Minimum Policies and Procedures guidelines (see the Documents-OCC, Organization Checklist-Minimum Policies and Proce dures, Standard Sample Document). If the decision is made to waive minimum policies and procedures totally or in part, the file should be documented to support the waiver. 

If a pre-filing meeting is not held, the converting institution may be advised of this requirement at the time it files its letter of intent (see step 

9 and Documents-OCC, Acknowledgment of Receipt Letter, Conver sion). 

4. Presides over the pre-filing meeting and documents the meeting with a memorandum to the file. 

5. Submits to the appropriate Deputy Comptroller (District) (DCD) or to the 

DCMNB a letter of intent to convert to a national bank signed by the president or cashier (see Applications and Notices, Conversion, Letter of Intent to Convert to a National Bank). The letter must be hand delivered, or sent by mail, return receipt requested. The letter should include or be accompanied by: • An attachment checklist. • An opinion of counsel that the conversion (including fiduciary assets, if applicable) is not in contravention of applicable state or federal law. • [For thrifts] A statement that the institution (will be/will not be) BIF-in sured by the Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation (FDIC). The institution must contact FDIC if it intends to change from SAIF-insured to BIF-insured as authorized by FIRREA. • The proposed title of the new national bank. • A statement of the reason(s) the institution wishes to convert to a national bank. • A risk-based capital schedule that details separately elements of Tier 1 and Tier 2 capital on a present and pro forma basis and includes a calculation of the risk-based capital ratio and the leverage ratio and 191

Conversion Procedures Co how liquidation accounts will be handled (see Applications and 

Notices, Conversion, Risk-based Capital Schedule). 

• The most recent report of condition. • The most recent report of income. • The most recent audit report. (If an external audit is not available, the directors' audit may be submitted.) [If applicable] The most recent annual report and/or 10K for the parent company. [For thrifts, if applicable] The name of the thrift holding company and a statement on whether the thrift conversion involves the conversion of the thrift holding company to a bank holding company. If it is not the intent of the thrift holding company to become a bank holding company, explain resolution of the ownership issue. • A copy of the proposed Community Reinvestment Act statement for the national bank, upon completion of the conversion, and a map depicting the delineated community. 

A listing of non-conforming assets, along with the divestiture plans or plans to bring the assets into conformance. NOTE: Examples of non-conforming assets include, but are not limited to, the following: 

Real estate investment companies or equity investments in real estate. 

Travel agencies. 

Loans exceeding legal limits under 12 U.S.C. 84 and 12 CFR 32.

Securities ineligible or excessive for investment by national banks. • A list of any outstanding and/or proposed options/warrants. • A list of all statutory and operating subsidiaries, along with the nature of their operations. NOTE: Upon review of the list of operating subsidiaries and their activities the OCC may require the institution to submit a letter of notification to determine that the activities are part of or incidental to banking. (Refer to the Operating Subsidiaries and 

Other Equity Investments section of the Comptroller's Manual for Corporate Activities.) The appropriate filing fee may also be required. If the OCC determines the activities are not permissible for national banks, the institution will be required to provide divestiture plans. • A discussion of any difference between accounting used by the institution and OCC accounting standards. Provide details of the effect of adopting OCC standards on the balance sheet and income statement. • A description of the future business plans if and when the institution converts to a national bank. At a minimum the description should address or provide: 

Any anticipated changes in operations. 

A list of directors and anticipated changes in the directorship. 

Any anticipated changes in management. 192
