A list of shareholders owning 10 percent or more of capital stock. 

Any plans to raise additional capital. 

Any anticipated funding changes.

Any anticipated changes in the loan composition. 

Any anticipated expansionary plans.

Any anticipated changes in the institution's CRA delineation or 

CRA statement.

Management's plans to familiarize themselves with national banking laws, rules, and regulations. 

Pro forma financial statements to cover a period of three years (along with any adjusting entries resulting from the conversion). • A list of operating branches (both brick and mortar and CBCTs) as defined in 12 U.S.C. 36; their addresses and popular names; certification that the locations are legal locations in which a national bank may have branches and CBCTS; and an indication of the branches and CBCTS that will continue to operate after conversion; and a list of branches approved, but unopened, and plans for their operation. 

NOTE: Normally, no separate filing fee is levied to authorize branches or CBCTs if the location is considered legal and they are operating or have been approved by the primary regulator. However, following review of the list of branches and CBCTS, and their locations, the OCC may require the institution to submit a branch and/or CBCT application and appropriate filing fee to determine that the location is legal. • Certification of compliance with 12 U.S.C. 371d and 12 CFR 7.3100 regarding investment limits in bank premises. • All directors and executive officers must complete and submit the 

Biographical Report and Financial Report (see Applications and Notices, Standard Sample Document, Biographical Report and Financial Report). All directors must comply with the requirements set forth in 12 U.S.C. 72. 

NOTE: Procedures detailed in the Citizenship Waiver Request section of this manual must be followed to request a citizenship waiver. [For thrifts, if applicable] Information on the effect of the Internal 

Revenue Service Ruling (90-54) on the institution following conversion to a national bank. Include the appropriate adjustments to the existing pro forma balance sheet and income statement. The adjustments should be supported by the advice of the institution's tax counsel. • If the institution currently exercises fiduciary powers, a statement as to whether it wishes to continue to do so upon conversion. 

NOTE: If the institution requests or desires to retain fiduciary powers, it must include with its letter of intent, a fiduciary powers filing fee, and an attachment containing the following additional information. 

A request for full or limited fiduciary powers. (Limited powers must be specified.) 193

DDA/Multinational Banking

12 CFR 9. In rare cases, such as issuance of powers limited to the administration of land trusts, the OCC may grant limited powers.

[For thrifts] A certification that the capital and surplus of the thrift is not less than that required by state or federal law for state banks, trust companies, and other corporations that exercise fiduciary powers.

Procedures 7. Establishes the official file to maintain original documents relating to the proposed conversion. 

8. Contacts the institution's state and federal supervisors by phone or by letter (see Documents-OCC, Request to Other Regulatory Agencies for Information, Conversion) to: • Obtain their comments. • Determine whether the institution is subject to an agreement, other enforcement proceeding or order. • Obtain reports of examination of the institution during the most recent three years. Review 9. Reviews the information submitted by the institution. Requests corrections, clarifications, or additional information, as necessary. 

NOTE: Minor corrections will be handled over the telephone. If there are major deficiencies in the filing, a letter is sent to the converting institution detailing the deficiencies and requesting corrections. The converting institution generally will have one opportunity to make corrections. 

Failure to make required corrections promptly may result in the filing being considered withdrawn. Filing fees will not be refunded. 

NOTE: If a pre-filing meeting was NOT held, the applicant must be advised that the OCC will review the converting institution's written policies and procedures to determine that they meet or exceed those described in the OCC's Minimum Policies and Procedures guideline. If the decision is made to waive minimum policies and procedures totally or in part, the file should be documented to support the waiver. 

NOTE: If an OCC pre-conversion field investigation is conducted, the examiner will review the converting institution's policies and procedures to assure they meet or exceed the minimum requirements. If a pre-conversion field investigation is not conducted, the converting institution must certify that its policies and procedures meet or exceed the OCC's minimum criteria. The board of directors is responsible for the regular review of policies and procedures and for assuring continuous compliance with them. If the decision is made to waive minimum policies and procedures totally or in part, the file should be documented to support the waiver. 

10. If necessary, starts an interested parties checklist (see Documents- 

OCC, Interested Parties Checklist, Standard Sample Document). 

11. Performs background checks on all directors and executive officers and principal shareholders identified in the application. Verifies that existing and/or proposed directors meet the residency requirements of 12 U.S.C. 

72. All requests for background information (including fingerprint cards) should contain the application control number and request the responding agency reference the control number in their response. 

In those instances where there is a conflict between the OCC's need to verify an person's qualifications and a request that a present employer 195

Conversion Procedures not be contacted, the issue should be resolved in favor of the OCC's need to know. If, however, sufficient information appears to be available through alternative sources, it might not be necessary to contact present employers in all cases. The person should not be given assurances that such contacts will not be made. If contact with present employers will be made, the person should be notified prior to contacting the employer. 

Background checks provide information on the competence, experience, integrity, and financial ability of each person named in the application and provides an independent determination of the accuracy and completeness of the information submitted. Information obtained by the OCC from the background investigation may be shared with the other financial institutions regulatory agencies. Standard background checks have been adopted by the Federal Financial Regulatory Examination Council and must be performed for all executive officers, directors, and principal shareholders (see Policy Statements section of this manual for the interagency agreement on background investigations).

Standard background checks must be made through the: • Federal Bureau of Investigation (FBI). • United States Custom Service (USCS). 

Drug Enforcement Administration (DEA). 

(ECIS) records search. ECIS includes a database of persons named in OCC administrative actions or criminal referrals.

Persons involved in insider abuse or other situations indicating potential unsuitability for involvement in the affairs of a national bank are also included on ECIS. District Counsel should be consulted on accessing these systems. In addition, Enforcement and Compliance (E&C) will review various printouts provided by the other regulatory agencies which list names of persons against whom enforcement actions have been taken and/or about whom criminal referrals were filed. Contact E&C to determine if a particular person appears on another agency's referral list. 

NOTE: The ECIS and LEXIS-NEXIS systems must be accessed to verify whether an person has any history of court cases, litigation, etc. Consult with District Counsel for access to those systems. The 

OCC's librarian will check local newspapers of organizers, proposed CEOS, and principal shareholders, if requested. When these persons are not nationally or regionally prominent, local newspapers may contain information not found in a major paper. If adverse or derogatory information is disclosed from any checked source, determine its significance to the overall filing, and if it is significant, include it in the confidential memorandum. • Federal regulatory agencies that have regulatory jurisdiction over the institutions in which any of the persons or holding company are involved (the FRB, FDIC, and OTS/FHLB). 196

Conversion Procedures 

• State depository institution regulatory agencies in those cases where any of the persons or holding companies have been involved with institutions under a state's regulatory jurisdiction. 

NOTE: If, in individual circumstances, any of the standard background checks are not performed because the check would be redundant, does not serve the OCC's purpose, or for any other reason, the file must be adequately documented with the reasons for the waiver. 

In addition to the standard background checks, discretionary checks appropriate to the persons' circumstances should be conducted. Such checks may include, but are not limited to, requests for background information from the Internal Revenue Service (IRS), Securities Ex change Commission (SEC), Commodity Futures Trading Commission (CFTC), National Association of Securities Dealers (NASD), INTERPOL, the International Monetary Fund (IMF), the World Bank, the U.S.

Immi gration and Naturalization Service, the Central Intelligence Agency 

(CIA), the U.S. State Department and local law enforcement agencies. 

NOTE: AII U.S. citizens and all foreign nationals are subject to six mandatory background checks. In addition, for foreign nationals, the 

OCC will contact E&C, Interpol, the U.S. State Department, the CIA, and bank regulators in the persons' home country or other countries where they borrow, own, operate, or manage banks. These checks also may be performed for U.S. citizens, but they are not mandatory. Mandatory checks may be waived, but any waivers must be documented in the case file and must include a brief statement of the basis for the waiver. 

NOTE: All foreign nationals must include with their filing certified statements from local officials attesting to their integrity and financial capacity 

(see Applications and Notices, Standard Sample Document, Foreign 

Certifications). 

NOTE: U.S. district and state court clerks may be contacted regarding decisions at the trial level in those instances where it is deemed appropriate.

NOTE: Credit checks are performed on acquiring parties when deemed appropriate.

NOTE: The OCC may require additional information to make an independent determination of the accuracy and completeness of any information contained in a conversion application. This may include a requirement to submit fingerprints if they are required to access information necessary for a background investigation. 

When fingerprints are required, the OCC will: • Distribute one fingerprint card to each person required to obtain fingerprints. • Instruct the person in writing that the provision of fingerprints is their responsibility and is required for the OCC to investigate the party's background thoroughly. 197

Procedures

Field Examiner 

• Provide a list of locations convenient to the person where fingerprinting services are available (i.e., the local police or law enforcement office or post offices). • Allow at least 10 business days for fingerprint cards to be returned to the DDA.

The DDA will forward completed fingerprint cards to  (BOS) to the attention of the Director for Corporate Activity for processing.

NOTE: If adverse information is received from the FBI on a person applying for director or executive officer position of a new national bank, the person must be notified and afforded the opportunity to complete, correct or challenge the information prior to the OCC making a decision. 

The person must be instructed to refer to 28 CFR 16.34 for guidance and to provide the OCC with a written response within 15 days of receipt of notification (see Documents-OCC, FBI Adverse Information, Stand ard Sample Document). 

If the adverse information is received from a source other than the FBI, the person must still be notified and afforded the opportunity to include additional facts germane to the issue in question. This information must be considered when making a decision. 

NOTE: No decision to deny the charter or employment with the new bank based on information in the FBI's record can be made until the applicant has been permitted to respond to the information. 

12. Reviews recent examination reports and other information pertaining to the condition of the converting institution. Decides if an OCC pre-conversion field investigation is needed. If needed, notifies the appropriate field office director to schedule it with the institution. Specifies matters to be considered (see Documents-OCC, Request to Field Office 

Director for Pre-Conversion Field Investigation, Conversion). 

13. If no pre-conversion field investigation is performed, documents the file with the reason(s) for the waiver. 

14. Performs the pre-conversion field investigation (normally within 60 days from date of receipt of the letter of intent). 

15. Determines the institution's condition based on judgments that: • The institution's management ensured compliance with applicable thrift or state bank laws, rulings, and regulations. • The institution's management exhibits strategic planning skills that reflect a reasonable prospect for successful operation of the proposed bank. • The operations of the institution reflect sound credit skills and funds management. • The institution's compliance with national banking laws, regulations, and rulings will be satisfactory. Includes an assessment of non-conforming assets. 198

Conversion 

Procedures

NOTE: A complete and detailed list of all non-conforming assets, such as loans, investment securities, real estate, and other assets, that the converting institution could not own under current national banking laws or regulations, must be included in the report of findings of the pre-conversion field investigation. Other examples of non-conforming assets are a travel agency, real estate investment companies, or an insurance agency which issues credit life or another type of insurance that the OCC does not permit national banks to sell or issue. Specific statements about the method of and anticipated time period for disposal must be included in the pre-conversion investigation report.

NOTE: The scope of the pre-conversion field investigation includes an assessment of the board of directors' internal control systems to assure the institution's compliance with the currency reporting and recordkeeping requirements of the Bank Secrecy Act (BSA).

NOTE: The scope of the pre-conversion field investigation may address any difference between accounting used by the institution and OCC accounting standards. Provide details of the effect of adopting OCC standards on the balance sheet and income statement. 

NOTE: The scope of the pre-conversion field investigation also includes a review of the converting institution's policies and procedures to assure that they meet or exceed the minimum requirements for all de novo national bank charters, unless specifically waived. 

16. Determines the soundness of management based on the condition of the institution and on management's ability to recognize and correct problems. 

17. Determines that the institution's record of CRA performance is adequate. 

18. Conducts an exit interview with senior management of the institution. 

Discusses results of the pre-conversion field investigation (e.g., asset quality, nonconformance with national banking laws, and non-conforming assets). Does not disclose the recommendation on the application, possible conditions that might be imposed on the applicant's approval, or potential administrative action which might be taken after the conversion. Requests that the institution's management responds to major criticisms. 

19. Assigns preliminary ratings, including CRA assessment. 

NOTE: Preliminary ratings must not be disclosed to the institution's management. The composite rating will be disclosed in the pre-conversion field investigation report. 

NOTE: If the pre-conversion field investigation reveals concerns, the

OCC may request additional information or clarification from the converting institution.

NOTE: The pre-conversion field investigation report will be provided to the applicant after evidence of bill payment has been received by the 199

Conversion 

Procedures

DDA/MNB 

Deputy Comptroller District (DCD)/DCMNB DDA/MNB district and the conversion is effective. Reports will include clear warnings against improper use or disclosure. 

20. Prepares billing information in accordance with 12 CFR 8.6. 

21. Prepares a recommendation on the conversion in a separate memorandum and forwards the memo and the pre-conversion field investigation billing information to the district office. Decision 22. Solicits comments from other OCC divisions, as appropriate. 

23. Prepares a confidential memorandum and decision letter recommending a decision to the DCD or the DCMNB (see Documents-OCC, Confidential Memorandum and Approval/Conditional Approval Letter (Conversion)). 

24. Makes a decision under delegated authority or refers the case to 

Washington for decision. 

NOTE: Adherence to the "Minimum Policies and Procedures" generally is required. Correction of any deficiencies in this regard should be included as conditions, as appropriate, if an approval decision is made. 

If the decision is made to waive minimum policies and procedures totally or in part, the file should be documented to support the waiver. 

NOTE: If the case is referred to Washington, the pre-conversion field investigation and the bill for the investigation must be included. NOTE: If the institution's condition or management is not sound, the conversion will normally be denied. If the CRA record of performance is not satisfactory, the conversion may be denied, or preliminary approval may be granted conditioned on the development and implementation of an adequate CRA statement or plan.

Subordinated Debt as Capital Introduction Summary of Process 

A bank must receive approval from the Office of the Comptroller of the Currency (OCC) for an issue of subordinated notes or debentures to be considered capital. To issue subordinated debentures as capital, the bank submits a letter of intent and the note form and agreement to the appropriate district office or Multinational Banking. The OCC has 30 days from receipt of the letter to grant preliminary approval of the issue or to extend the time period for review. If the OCC does not object to the issue or extend the review period, the bank must: • Obtain shareholder approval of the issue, if necessary. • Prepare and file with the OCC for review and clearance all necessary offering materials required by 12 CFR 16. • Issue the debentures. • Submit a letter of notification that the debentures have been issued and receive a letter of final approval from the OCC. Final approval is automatic unless the applicant is notified otherwise within seven days from the date the OCC receives the letter of notification.

The debentures should be issued within one year of preliminary approval.

Key Policies 

Approval to issue debentures as capital may be withheld for banks that: • Fail to comply with a capital plan submitted to the OCC. • Propose a capital structure that the OCC considers inadequate. • Violate laws or regulations. • Exhibit conditions that threaten safety and soundness. • Fail to provide adequate information.

Generally Applicable Procedures Standards of Review (Refer to OCC Guidelines for Subordinated Debt Instru ments for a description of mandatory provisions and a discussion of OCC policy on other provisions. See

Documents-OCC, OCC Guidelines for Subordinated Debt Instruments, Subordinated Debt as Capital.) 

In assessing a proposed debt instrument's compliance with the previous guidelines, the OCC will concentrate on provisions that may: • Accelerate the repayment date of the indebtedness. • Restrict the bank from engaging in activities that would otherwise be permissible. • Place note holders in a secured creditor status or give them priority to other creditors.

For an issuance of subordinate debt to be considered capital, it must: • Have an original weighted average maturity of at least five years. • Not be insured by FDIC, be a deposit, or be collateralized. • Be subordinated to the claims of depositors. • Be defined as capital according to 12 CFR 3.100 or any other rules, regulations, or policies published or promulgated by the OCC. • Be ineligible as collateral for a loan by the issuing bank. • Provide that no repayment prior to maturity (other than a regularly scheduled repayment already approved by the OCC), either pursuant to an acceleration in the event of default or otherwise, be made without the prior written OCC approval. • Provide that once any scheduled payments of principal begin, all shall be made at least annually and the amount repaid each year shall be no less than in the prior year. • Comply with disclosure requirements under the federal securities laws and OCC regulations. 

Statutory Limitations or Minimums 

Subordinated debt that satisfies those standards of review is considered part of Tier 2 capital for computing statutory minimums according to 12 CFR 3. The amount of subordinated debt and intermediate term preferred stock eligible for inclusion as Tier 2 capital is limited to a maximum of 50 percent of Tier 1 capital. The amount eligible for inclusion as Tier 2 capital is reduced by 20 percent of the original amount of the instrument at the beginning of each of the last five years of the instrument's life.

Shareholders' Approval 

A bank must obtain the approval of shareholders owning a majority of the stock entitled to vote before 206

Subordinated Debt as Capital Introduction issuing or prepaying subordinated debentures, unless such action is already authorized by the articles of association.

Prepayment 

No prepayment may be made without the OCC's prior written approval. A bank must submit a letter of intent and obtain approval before the terms in the note agreement are changed, or an unscheduled payment is made. The appropriate filing fee must accompany the letter of intent.

Voluntary Liquidation Introduction Summary of Process 

A national bank that wishes to close through voluntary liquidation must have approval of shareholders owning two-thirds of its stock. The voting procedure must follow the bank's articles of association if it has preferred stock.

The Office of the Comptroller of the Currency (OCC) requires a letter of notification from the bank, giving the date the bank entered into liquidation and certification of the necessary shareholder approval. Such notification permits the OCC to begin monitoring the compliance of the national bank with the appropriate national banking laws for liquidations. 

Purchase and Assumption 

A liquidation agreement that also involves the purchase of assets and assumption of liabilities by another bank must be approved by the liquidating bank's shareholders owning two-thirds of its stock. Those shareholders must ratify the liquidation and the purchase agreement made by the board of directors before the OCC acts on the purchasing bank's application. A voluntary liquidation cannot be effected before consummation of any related purchase and assumption agreement. (See the Mergers section of this manual for purchase and assumption transactions.)

Liquidating Agent or Committee 

Shareholders must appoint an agent or committee to conduct the liquidation. The liquidating agent or committee posts a bond in favor of the bank. The bond is held by the chairman of the board of directors or a designee who is not an agent or member of the liquidating committee. The bond must carry an amount deemed adequate by the board after considering the nature and value of the assets to be liquidated. 

Newspaper Publication 

The bank, after shareholders' approval, and before or simultaneously with the commencement of liquidation, must publish notice of its liquidation in a local newspaper daily for two months. If only weekly publication is available, the notice must be published for nine consecutive weeks.

Liquidation Process 

On the commencement date of liquidation, the bank must proceed at once to liquidate the bank. The liquidating agent or committee must report annually to the OCC the progress of liquidation as of December 31, and to the shareholders at the annual meeting. The board of directors must supervise the bank's liquidation through the liquidating agent or committee. 

Upon completion of the liquidation process, the liquidating agent or committee submits a Final Report of Liquidation. At that time, the charter certificate, branch certificates, and trust permit (if any) must be returned to the OCC. The bank must also certify that it has shredded all OCC reports of examination. Instructions are detailed in the accompanying procedures. 

The OCC may examine the bank at any time until the completion of the liquidation process. 

The OCC imposes assessments on a national bank in voluntary liquidation. 

Banks with Trust Powers

Regulation 9, promulgated by the OCC under authority of the provisions of 12 U.S.C. 92(a), provides that whenever a national bank exercising fiduciary powers is placed in voluntary liquidation, the liquidating agent or committee shall, in accordance with the laws of the state in which such national bank is located, proceed at once to liquidate its affairs of the trust department as follows: 

• All court trusts and estates under the jurisdiction of a court shall be closed or disposed of as soon as practicable in accordance with the orders or instructions of the court having jurisdiction. • All voluntary trusts which can be closed promptly should be closed as soon as practicable and a final accounting should be made. • All other trusts shall be transferred by appropriate legal proceedings to properly appointed substitute fiduciaries. Because beneficiaries or successor fiduciaries of trust estates, formerly administered by the bank, may have valid claims against the bank based upon its acts or omissions in administering the estates, it is unwise to make a total distribution to stockholders of the remaining proceeds of the liquidation of the bank until all trust estates have been disposed of properly.

The notice should state clearly the time, place, and purpose of the meeting (see Documents-Applicant, Notice of Shareholders' Meeting, Voluntary Liquidation). 4. Obtains approval for voluntary liquidation and for any attendant purchase of assets and assumption of liabilities by another bank from shareholders owning two-thirds of the stock of the liquidating bank. 

NOTE: If the bank has preferred stock, the provisions of the articles of association should be followed for the required vote to place the bank in liquidation.

NOTE: Any person(s), including directors or attorneys for the bank, but not officers or employees, may be designated to act as proxy. National bank directors, however, cannot direct their own vote by proxy. 

NOTE: Shares of capital stock held by any holding company affiliated with the liquidating bank may be voted without obtaining permission from the Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve System. 

NOTE: The resolutions adopted should specify the date and hour on which the voluntary liquidation is to commence. The commencement date of the liquidation cannot precede the date on which the resolutions are adopted by the shareholders, even though the bank already may 214

Voluntary Liquidation Procedures have discontinued regular business and been absorbed by another association.

NOTE: If a P&A transaction is involved, the voluntary liquidation should commence upon consummation of the purchase and assumption agreement entered into between the banks. Shareholders of the liquidating bank must ratify the agreement before the OCC can decide on the purchasing bank's application. 

5. Obtains the shareholders' designation of a liquidating agent or committee. 

NOTE: A national or state bank that lacks fiduciary powers may not act as liquidating agent.

NOTE: The OCC recommends that shareholders' meetings be recessed subject to recall when further shareholder participation is required. Notification 6. Sends Notice of Voluntary Liquidation, which has been certified under the bank's seal by the president or cashier (or secretary, if pursuant to 

12 CFR 7.5245), to the Deputy Comptroller (District) (DCD) for the OCC district office where the bank is located or to Deputy Comptroller  in the case of a multinational bank or its affiliates (see Applications and Notices, Voluntary Liquidation, Notice of Voluntary Liquidation). 

The notice should include: 

• Record of shareholder vote.

Approved resolutions, including a planned commencement date that the bank will be placed in liquidation. • A list of the names and addresses of the directors. • The name(s), business address(es), and phone number(s) of the liquidating agent(s). • If a liquidating committee is appointed, a correspondent for the committee. • The amount fixed for the bond to be posted by the agent or committee. 

7. Publishes the notice of liquidation for two months in every issue of a newspaper in the community in which the main office of the bank is located or, if no newspaper is published there, in the nearest community. 

If a newspaper is issued only weekly, the notice must be published for nine consecutive weeks. The notice announces that the bank is closing its affairs, and creditors should present their claims against the association for payment (see Documents-Applicant, Legal Notice, Voluntary Liquidation). If the publication schedule is not followed, the 

DCD/DCMNB should be notified immediately.

Regulation K Application Comments Introduction Summary of Process 

The Federal Reserve banks request advisory comments on Regulation K applications relating to national banks or their affiliates. Normally the OCC has 30 days in which to respond with comments and/or recommendations based on supervisory factors. Background 

Regulation K addresses foreign operations of Federal

Reserve member banks and applications pertaining to the establishment of foreign branches, Edge Act and Agreement corporations, investments in foreign companies and subsidiaries, and requests to engage in new activities not specifically authorized by the regulation. The approval or disapproval of Regulation K applications is a primary responsibility of the Federal Reserve. To make an informed decision on each application relating to national banks or their affiliates, the 

Federal Reserve requests advisory comments from the

OCC, which responds with recommendations based on supervisory factors. This allows the OCC to inform the 

Federal Reserve of any concerns to be considered in the decision process.
